### [1] [EvalDetectBench: A Benchmark for Measuring Evaluation Awareness in Frontier Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01611)
*Xinning Li,Kemunto Ochwang'i,Aryasomayajula Ram Bharadwaj,Alexandra Souly,Robert Kirk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvalDetectBench is an open pipeline and benchmark for measuring LLM evaluation awareness to ensure valid safety assessments, correcting methodological biases in existing approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier LLMs can recognize when they're being evaluated (evaluation awareness), which undermines the validity of evaluation results if models behave differently in evaluations vs deployment. Current AI safety frameworks rely heavily on evaluation results, making this a critical issue.

Method: Introduces EvalDetectBench - an open pipeline compatible with any Inspect-compatible evaluation. Includes a curated transcript suite covering frontier system-card evaluations and diverse deployment sources. Uses per-model probe calibration and stratified generator-harmonization to correct for biases like model identity effects and elicitation prompt performance variations.

Result: Identifies two systematic biases in existing literature: model identity accounts for 11.25% of measurement variance and can reorder model rankings; elicitation prompts optimized for one model can perform near chance on others. The benchmark serves dual purposes: measuring how reliably LLMs recognize evaluations and assessing how detectable benchmarks are as evaluations.

Conclusion: EvalDetectBench provides a standardized way to measure evaluation awareness in LLMs, correcting methodological biases to ensure more valid safety evaluations. The open pipeline allows practitioners to test against current and future benchmarks, improving the reliability of AI safety assessment frameworks.

Abstract: Frontier large language models can often recognize when they are being evaluated, a capability known as evaluation awareness. If models behave differently in evaluations than in deployment, this undermines the validity of evaluation results, which are a crucial component of current AI safety frameworks. We introduce EvalDetectBench, an open pipeline and benchmark for measuring evaluation awareness that works with any Inspect-compatible evaluation, allowing practitioners to test against current and future benchmarks. EvalDetectBench ships with a newly curated transcript suite covering current frontier system-card evaluations and diverse deployment sources. The benchmark serves two purposes: measuring how reliably frontier LLMs recognize that they are being evaluated, and assessing how detectable individual benchmarks are as evaluations. We identify two methodological choices in the existing literature that introduce systematic bias: the identity of the model that generated the deployment transcripts accounts for 11.25% of measurement variance and can reorder model rankings; and elicitation prompts selected for high performance on one model can perform near chance on others. EvalDetectBench corrects for both via per-model probe calibration and a stratified generator-harmonisation procedure.

</details>


### [2] [Meta-ethics and AI: exploring the novel meta-ethical questions in the era of AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01685)
*Shang Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: If future AI develops genuine moral reasoning capabilities, meta-ethics would need to expand beyond human-centered frameworks to address AI's own ethics and cross-perspective moral analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The emergence of advanced AI with genuine moral reasoning capacities would challenge current meta-ethical frameworks that are human-centered, necessitating new approaches to understand ethics in the AI era.

Method: Proposes a conditional framework for analyzing meta-ethical questions if advanced moral AI emerges, distinguishing four domains of inquiry: human ethics from human perspective, AI ethics from human perspective, human ethics from AI perspective, and AI ethics from AI perspective.

Result: Identifies that traditional meta-ethical theories (cognitivism/non-cognitivism, error theory, relativism, objective realism) would require substantial revision to address AI's own ethics and the cross-perspective ethical domains created by morally capable AI systems.

Conclusion: The potential emergence of AI's own ethics would place significant pressure on current meta-ethical frameworks, potentially requiring refinement, reconstruction, or reconceptualization to accommodate non-human moral agents and perspectives.

Abstract: With the development of artificial intelligence (AI), the landscape of meta-ethics, which has largely centred on human ethics, faces pressures that may significantly reconfigure it. In particular, if future AI systems were to exhibit sufficiently integrated capacities for moral reasoning, moral intentionality, and moral reflection, novel meta-ethical questions would arise concerning what I call "AI's own ethics", as distinct from ethical principles merely imposed on AI by human designers. This paper offers a conditional and methodological framework for identifying the questions that would emerge if such AI systems were to arise. On that basis, the paper distinguishes four domains of meta-ethical inquiry in the era of AI: questions about the nature of human ethics from the human perspective; questions about the nature of AI's own ethics from the human perspective; questions about the nature of human ethics from the AI perspective; and questions about the nature of AI's own ethics from the AI perspective. The paper then considers how some existing mainstream meta-ethical theories (such as cognitivism and non-cognitivism, error theory and success theory, relativism, and objective realism) might illuminate these domains, while arguing that many familiar human-centred formulations of those theories may not transfer straightforwardly to AI cases without substantial revision. The overall conclusion is that the emergence of AI's own ethics would place significant pressure on current frameworks and may require substantial refinement, reconstruction, or reconceptualisation.

</details>


### [3] [Epistemic Sybil Resistance: Multiplying AI Agents Without Multiplying Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01873)
*Marc Bara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies an "epistemic Sybil problem" where AI agents can produce redundant reports from shared evidence, and shows that collective inference systems need to track evidential ancestry and dependence rather than just counting reports or agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent AI systems often treat each agent's report as independent evidence, but reports may descend from the same evidence (replication) rather than representing independent corroboration. This creates a problem where apparently independent reports may be redundant, misleading aggregation algorithms.

Method: 1) Formalize the "epistemic Sybil problem" where reports are Sybil extensions if they provide no additional information given other reports. 2) Develop Gaussian shared-root model to analyze redundancy. 3) Conduct empirical tests with over 20,000 controlled LLM-agent calls on synthetic documents, manipulating evidence roots and report multiplicity. 4) Test correlation of extraction errors and develop correlated-extraction aggregator. 5) Isolate representation similarity from evidential ancestry through controlled manipulation.

Result: Holding evidence fixed while increasing reports (1 to 32) collapses naive posterior coverage from 0.940 to 0.263. Increasing evidence roots (1 to 16) closes this gap. Agents' replicate extraction errors are correlated (gamma_cal = 0.719). Representation similarity manipulations change inferred cluster counts significantly (1.425), while true ancestry changes have minimal effect (0.040).

Conclusion: Collective inference systems must track evidential ancestry and dependence rather than relying on agent/report counts or similarity. Simple report-space deduplication fails to distinguish replication from independent corroboration, leading to miscalibrated aggregators.

Abstract: Multi-agent AI systems improve inference by spawning agents and synthesizing reports. But another agent is not another observation: apparently independent reports may descend from the same evidence, and genuinely independent evidence can produce nearly identical reports. We formalize this as an epistemic Sybil problem. A report Z is an epistemic Sybil extension relative to reports R when I(Theta; Z | R) = 0. No report-only aggregator can generally distinguish replication from independent corroboration: identical reports can warrant different posteriors under unobserved ancestry. A Gaussian shared-root model shows common ancestry does not imply complete redundancy. Repeated extraction adds information toward a source-level ceiling, and correlated extraction errors, which a shared base model can induce among independent agents, lower that ceiling further. We test these predictions with more than 20,000 controlled LLM-agent report and extraction calls on synthetic evidentiary documents. Holding one evidence root fixed while report multiplicity rises from 1 to 32 collapses naive posterior coverage from 0.940 to 0.263. Holding report count fixed while evidence-root multiplicity rises from 1 to 16 closes the gap, and the aggregators are statistically indistinguishable at k = 16. The agent's replicate extraction errors are correlated (gamma_cal = 0.719, estimated out of sample), and a correlated-extraction aggregator restores calibration accordingly. A controlled manipulation isolates representation similarity from evidential ancestry. It changes a report-space deduplication mechanism's mean inferred cluster count by 1.425 (95% CI [1.363, 1.485]), whereas a fourfold change in true ancestry changes it by only 0.040 ([-0.045, 0.120]). Collective inference should therefore track evidential ancestry and dependence, not agent or report multiplicity or similarity.

</details>


### [4] [When Can a Machine Trust a Statute? A Survival Certificate for Machine-Extracted Legal Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01741)
*Surya Saka*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A method for certifying which formal logic implications survive extraction noise in statutes, with certificates that show premise spans and minimal counterexamples, but the approach is fragile and requires careful calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Statutes are increasingly parsed by machines before humans read them, but different parsers disagree significantly (e.g., 0.43 false-negative rate on Missouri statutes), creating noise in automated legal analysis. The paper asks what formal logic can survive such extraction noise.

Method: Builds a passive survival certificate for the Duquenne-Guigues implication basis of machine-extracted statutory contexts. Measures per-attribute inter-extractor disagreement, replays it against the basis in 1,000 Monte Carlo trials, and certifies implications only when a one-sided Wilson 95% lower bound on survival reaches 0.95. Each certified implication carries premise spans and a minimal counterexample.

Result: The approach passed preregistered held-out tests (10 statute families across 7 Titles exact; 16 across 11 with 5% tolerance) on 29,365 Missouri sections and 502 Indian central-Act sections. However, 93.2% of held-out chapters fell below the informativeness floor under one globally deployed error model. A 2x2 factorial analysis attributed this to calibration-rate transfer issues, not selection bias.

Conclusion: The certificate is usable but fragile - it must be deployed with per-chapter calibration or error tolerance. The paper released code, data products, and audit trail including one retracted claim.

Abstract: Statutes are increasingly parsed by machines before people read them, and the parsers disagree: on Missouri's statutes, two independently written extractors diverge on numeric-threshold presence at a false-negative rate of 0.43. We ask what formal logic survives such noise. We build a passive survival certificate for the Duquenne-Guigues implication basis of machine-extracted statutory contexts: per-attribute inter-extractor disagreement is measured, replayed against the basis in 1,000 Monte Carlo trials, and an implication is certified only when a one-sided Wilson 95% lower bound on survival reaches 0.95; every certified implication carries premise spans and a minimal counterexample. On 29,365 Missouri sections and 502 Indian central-Act sections, the preregistered held-out gate passes (10 statute families across 7 Titles exact; 16 across 11 with 5% tolerance), yet under one globally deployed error model 93.2% of held-out chapters fall below the informativeness floor, and a 2x2 factorial assigns that to calibration-rate transfer, not selection. The certificate is usable but fragile: deploy it per-chapter-calibrated or error-tolerant. Code, data products, and the audit trail, including one retracted claim, are released.

</details>


### [5] [When Agents Implement Systems: A Case Study in Defects, Detection, and Evaluation Rigor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01985)
*Phanindra Reddy Madduru*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a case study examining how LLM coding agents handle systems-level requirements in data engineering, documenting implementation defects and evaluating retrieval trade-offs on the HotpotQA benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM coding agents increasingly perform end-to-end engineering work, there's a lack of empirical characterization of how they behave on systems-level requirements like schema design, async orchestration, configuration correctness, and retrieval-filtering trade-offs.

Method: 1) Case study of an LLM agent implementing a multi-component data system against a detailed pre-existing specification with fixed architecture decisions. 2) Evaluation on HotpotQA benchmark comparing filtered vs. unfiltered retrieval strategies using gold evidence labels as substitutes for entity identification.

Result: 1) Cataloged five implementation defects categorized by constraint violated and detection method. 2) Filtered retrieval reaches ceiling recall by budget 3 (since limited to gold paragraphs), while unfiltered search recovers all required evidence only 69% of the time at budget 10, with statistically significant gap at all budgets (p < 0.0001). 3) Found instances where claimed performance fixes weren't re-measured on motivating regressions.

Conclusion: The study reveals both successes and limitations of LLM agent autonomy in systems engineering, showing that while agents can implement complex systems, they introduce subtle defects and fail to properly validate performance improvements, highlighting the need for rigorous oversight even with advanced agents.

Abstract: As LLM coding agents increasingly perform end-to-end engineering work, we lack empirical characterization of how they behave on systems-level requirements: schema design, async orchestration, configuration correctness, and retrieval-filtering trade-offs. We present a case study of one such agent implementing a multi-component data system against a detailed pre-existing specification. Storage technologies, schema, entity-resolution algorithm, and retrieval-filtering strategy were fixed in advance; the agent autonomy was in the implementation, in diagnosing and fixing defects it introduced, and in interaction-design choices left open. Over a single session, we catalog five such defects, categorized by constraint violated and detection method. We further evaluate, on the public HotpotQA benchmark, the one retrieval trade-off specified in that architecture: restricting candidates to a graph-identified entity set before ranking versus unfiltered search. We substitute the benchmark gold evidence labels for entity identification, since we lacked LLM access to run that stage, and report standard recall rather than the benchmark own accuracy metrics. Across retrieval budgets from 1 to 10 and 100 questions against a pooled corpus of 2994 paragraphs, filtered recall reaches its ceiling by a budget of 3, expected once candidates are restricted to the gold paragraphs themselves, while unfiltered search recovers all required evidence only 69 percent of the time even at a budget of 10, a gap that holds at every budget tested, with sign test p less than 0.0001. We close with a discussion of where the agent autonomy succeeded versus required correction, including one instance where a claimed performance fix was never re-measured on the regression that motivated it.

</details>


### [6] [When Does Information Sharing Improve Decentralized Discovery? Aggregation, Independent Rescue, and Equilibrium Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01814)
*Yohei Nakajima*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes information sharing in finite discovery models, showing how centralization affects pooled estimation and when sharing improves discovery compared to independent rescue attempts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To separate the effects of information sharing on pooled estimation improvement versus elimination of independent rescue actions, and to understand when sharing is beneficial in discovery processes.

Method: Uses exact finite discovery models, centralized action-budget profiles, registered incremental-sharing protocols, and two-agent Bayesian games with hidden mixture of signal sources to analyze information sharing effects.

Result: Equal individual accuracy can coexist with different portfolio values; sharing improves discovery when pooled residual error contracts faster than independent rescue attempts; registered selected equilibrium yields strict positive sharing interval at 3/5 signal accuracy, but results are selection-dependent.

Conclusion: Information sharing effects are nuanced in finite models, with benefits depending on specific protocols and equilibrium selection, rather than being universal properties of sharing mechanisms.

Abstract: Information sharing can improve a pooled estimate while eliminating independent rescue actions. This paper separates those effects in exact finite discovery models. A centralized action-budget profile shows that equal one-person accuracy can coexist with different portfolio values. Under a registered incremental-sharing protocol, a sharing step improves discovery exactly when pooled residual error contracts faster than an independent rescue attempt. Exact bounded registries exhibit compression, aggregation, neutral curves, and a bounded zero mixed class. In a two-agent Bayesian game with a hidden mixture of common and independent signal sources, the registered selected equilibrium yields a strict positive sharing interval at signal accuracy 3/5, while alternative equilibria show that the result is selection-dependent rather than universal. The models are synthetic and finite; no human or organizational data are used.

</details>


### [7] [ClaimReceipt: Verifying Evidence Sufficiency and Coverage in Agent Evaluations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01992)
*Peiying Zhu,Sidi Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ClaimReceipt introduces a claim-relative receipt system for verifying agent evaluations by binding evidence to signed manifests, ensuring both sufficiency and coverage of evidence for reproducible claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent evaluations lack reliable methods to verify two key aspects: 1) whether reported claims can be recomputed from retained evidence (sufficiency), and 2) whether retained records cover the committed experiment set (coverage). Generic logs and hash-linked transcripts fail to address these questions reliably.

Method: ClaimReceipt is a claim-relative receipt specification and selective verifier that binds typed transaction evidence to a signed experiment manifest. It freezes the specification before implementation and provides verdicts (PASS, INVALID, INCONCLUSIVE) per claim. The system includes selective verification, transaction evidence binding, and supports both deterministic replay and post-generation records.

Result: On 1,392 historical records, ClaimReceipt reproduced all manual audit verdicts, exactly replayed deterministic and post-generation records, made all field groups non-redundant under ablations, and correctly handled semantic faults with zero false positives. Prospective testing with 30 assignments showed complete evidence yields PASS, missing receipts yield INCONCLUSIVE_COVERAGE, and evidence withholding preserves protocol verification but makes economic claims inconclusive. The system adds minimal overhead: 0.021% of model-inference time and 9.9 KB per transaction.

Conclusion: Claim verification requires both claim-sufficient evidence and a committed universe where omissions become visible. While ClaimReceipt demonstrates effective verification, its current specification may not be fully unambiguous to independent readers, indicating need for further specification legibility improvements.

Abstract: Agent evaluations face two distinct evidentiary questions: whether a reported claim is recomputable from retained evidence (sufficiency), and whether the retained records cover the committed experiment set (coverage). Generic logs and hash-linked transcripts answer neither reliably. We introduce ClaimReceipt, a claim-relative receipt specification and selective verifier that binds typed transaction evidence to a signed experiment manifest and returns PASS, INVALID, or INCONCLUSIVE per claim. We freeze the specification before implementation (SHA-256 18d109...b81). On 1,392 historical buyer--seller records, a CR-2 verifier reproduces all five manually labeled audit verdicts, exactly replays 600 deterministic and 792 post-generation records, makes every one of 13 declared field groups non-redundant under tested ablations, and returns the expected result on 11/11 semantic faults with 0/8 false positives. We then run a separate prospective CR-3 epoch: 30 assignments are committed before inference, terminal receipts are signed and chained, and private evidence is encrypted for an auditor. Complete evidence yields coverage and accounting PASS; withholding one terminal receipt returns INCONCLUSIVE_COVERAGE, while withholding all private openings preserves coverage and protocol verification but makes economic claims inconclusive, exactly matching a preregistered prediction. Receipt instrumentation adds 0.021% of model-inference time and 9.9 KB per transaction. A specification-legibility probe indicates that our own frozen specification is not yet unambiguous to an independent reader. Claim verification therefore requires both claim-sufficient evidence and a committed universe against which omissions become visible.

</details>


### [8] [Induction and Inquiry via Probabilistic Reasoning over Language and Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01815)
*Wasu Top Piriyakulkij,Sam Acquaviva,Cassidy Langenfeld,Joshua Tenenbaum,Kevin Ellis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A computational model combining LLM-guided Bayesian learning with symbolic mental programs explains how humans efficiently learn abstract concepts from sparse, noisy data while capturing gradations of uncertainty and flexible concept representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how humans grow and maintain abstract knowledge from sparse, streaming noisy data, which requires a computational model that is data-efficient, captures uncertainty gradations for intelligent inquiry, and flexibly represents endless concept ranges.

Method: Encoding symbolic knowledge as mental programs combining natural language with source code, and sequentially inferring mental programs using LLM-guided Bayesian learning algorithms.

Result: The model successfully reproduces quantitative signatures of human inductive learning and active inquiry (anchoring, garden-pathing, etc.) across behavioral studies. Pure LLMs and classic Bayesian models fail at the task, don't reproduce human behavior, or require exorbitant computational cost.

Conclusion: Humans likely grow knowledge by representing hypotheses across language-like and program-like representations, revising them to approximate Bayesian updates, with bottom-up neural mechanisms (LLMs) making inference tractable and learnable.

Abstract: How humans grow and maintain abstract knowledge from the sparse, streaming noisy data of experience is a longstanding challenge in cognitive science. Any computational account must satisfy at least three desiderata: It must be (1) data-efficient and compute-efficient, (2) capture gradations of uncertainty to support intelligent inquiry and information gathering, and (3) be flexible enough to mentally represent the endless range of concepts people can learn and think about. Here we introduce a computational model that captures these three properties, by encoding symbolic knowledge as mental programs that combine natural language with source code, and sequentially inferring mental programs using LLM-guided Bayesian learning algorithms. Across a range of behavioral studies this model successfully reproduces quantitative signatures of human inductive learning and active inquiry, such as anchoring, garden-pathing, and other effects. In contrast, pure LLMs and classic Bayesian models either fail at the underlying task, or do not reproduce human behavior, or succeed only at exorbitant computational cost. These results suggest that one way humans continually grow their knowledge is by mentally representing many hypotheses spanning language-like and program-like representations, then revising those hypotheses to approximate Bayesian updates, while a bottom-up neural mechanism (an LLM) makes inference both tractable and learnable.

</details>


### [9] [Propose to Learn, Learn to Propose: Evaluability-Aware Assistance under Bounded Rationality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02242)
*Yifan Zhu,Sammie Katt,Samuel Kaski*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI assistance planning needs to consider user evaluation limitations; this paper proposes ProSE-Plan, a method that treats proposals as both interventions and probes to learn user preferences and constraints, outperforming standard approaches when evaluation costs are high.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI assistance methods assume users can reliably evaluate any proposal, but this fails in practice due to bounded rationality (cognitive limitations). There's a need for "evaluability-aware" planning where proposals serve dual purposes: as task interventions and as probes to learn about user preferences and evaluation constraints.

Method: The paper formalizes ProSE (hidden-parameter sequential assistance problem) with a KL-regularised bounded-rational binary response model where acceptance trades off value gain against a distance-dependent evaluability penalty. They develop ProSE-Plan, a depth-2 Bayes-adaptive planner that scores proposals by considering possible user responses and the posterior beliefs those responses would induce.

Result: In controlled graph simulations, ProSE-Plan outperforms evaluability-unaware and myopic baselines when evaluation cost is the bottleneck. A probe-commit ablation confirms that the method selects informative proposals that simpler methods miss, demonstrating that likely accepted proposals and informative probes need not coincide.

Conclusion: User evaluability is a planning-relevant dimension of AI assistance, complementary to generation quality and preference inference. The paper establishes evaluability-awareness as a critical consideration for AI assistants and shows that proposals serving as both interventions and probes can lead to better assistance outcomes when users face evaluation constraints.

Abstract: AI assistants often collaborate by proposing candidate edits, plans, or designs that users evaluate before adoption. Existing assistance methods focus on proposal quality or user-goal inference, often assuming that the user can reliably evaluate any proposal, which can fail in practice because of bounded rationality. We study evaluability-aware proposal planning, where proposals serve both as task interventions and as probes for learning latent preferences and evaluation constraints, where the resulting belief updates then guide later proposals. We formalise this setting as ProSE, a hidden-parameter sequential assistance problem, and instantiate it with a KL-regularised bounded-rational binary response model in which acceptance trades off value gain against a distance-dependent evaluability penalty. Analysing the planning consequence of this likelihood reveals that likely accepted proposals and informative probes need not coincide, which explains why planners that only pursue acceptance systematically underperform. We operationalise ProSE with \textsc{ProSE-Plan}, a depth-2 Bayes-adaptive planner that scores proposals by possible responses and response-induced posterior beliefs. In controlled graph simulations, \textsc{ProSE-Plan} improves over evaluability-unaware and myopic baselines when evaluation cost is the bottleneck, and a probe-commit ablation confirms that our approach selects informative proposals that simpler methods miss. Our results thus identify user evaluability as a planning-relevant dimension of AI assistance, complementary to generation quality and preference inference.

</details>


### [10] [Architecting Conversational Data Systems for Stateless LLM APIs: The Hydration Proxy Pattern](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01834)
*Joseph Axisa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Hydration Proxy Pattern to solve the architectural gap caused by stateless LLM APIs in enterprise conversational platforms, enabling better state management while maintaining platform sovereignty over conversational data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise platforms are moving to conversational reasoning interfaces, but stateless LLM APIs create an architectural gap. This statelessness forces client applications to manage all conversational state and semantic memory, creating burden and inefficiency in enterprise settings.

Method: The paper proposes the Hydration Proxy Pattern, an architecture that decouples session persistence from the reasoning engine. It also introduces the Context Stabilization Mandate to address the tradeoff between sovereign state management and KV (key-value) caching.

Result: The framework enables platform sovereignty over conversational data while supporting secure, multi-stage semantic grounding. It provides a solution that balances the scalability benefits of stateless LLM APIs with the practical needs of stateful conversational interfaces in enterprise environments.

Conclusion: The Hydration Proxy Pattern addresses critical architectural challenges in enterprise conversational AI systems by bridging the gap between stateless LLM APIs and stateful conversational interfaces, enabling better data sovereignty and efficient state management without compromising scalability.

Abstract: As enterprise platforms transition to conversational reasoning interfaces, the stateless nature of LLM APIs creates an architectural gap. While statelessness enables horizontal scalability for AI providers, it forces client applications to manage the entire burden of conversational state and semantic memory. The work identifies the Hydration Proxy Pattern, an architecture that decouples session persistence from the reasoning engine. The framework ensures platform sovereignty over conversational data while enabling secure, multi-stage semantic grounding. We further propose the Context Stabilization Mandate to resolve the tradeoff between sovereign state management and KV caching.

</details>


### [11] [Codebook Agent: Amortized Topology Design for LLM Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02264)
*Jinxi Yu,Yubei Li,Eric Hanchen Jiang,Zhi Zhang,Dong Liu,Wenxiao Zhao,Levina Li,Kai-Wei Chang,Ying Nian Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Codebook Agent: A novel LLM multi-agent system that uses vector-quantized autoencoders to compress successful communication topologies into a 16-entry codebook, enabling fast, accurate topology selection without iterative search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM multi-agent communication topology designers are misaligned with the problem: (1) topologies collapse to about 6 distinct graphs regardless of codebook capacity, (2) edge count is negatively correlated with token consumption, so sparser graphs make inference more expensive, (3) existing message-passing scorers are adjacency-invariant when agents share profiles, making them unable to rank candidates in benchmark configurations.

Method: Three key components: (1) Vector-quantized autoencoder compresses successful topologies into query-independent 16-entry codebook; (2) Reward-weighted MLP maps query embedding to distribution over codes; (3) MLP proxy that reads flattened adjacency matrix, regressed on measured utility and normalized token cost, reranks top decoded candidates in single batched forward pass.

Result: Most accurate method on all six benchmarks (84.6 average vs 83.0 for prior best), emits topology in 2.4 ms, uses 21.9-33.2% fewer LLM tokens, with no iterative search or message passing at test time.

Conclusion: Codebook Agent demonstrates that compressing successful communication topologies into a small codebook and using simple MLP-based ranking provides superior accuracy, efficiency, and speed compared to conventional conditional graph generation approaches for LLM multi-agent systems.

Abstract: Adapting the communication topology of an LLM multi-agent system to each query improves both accuracy and efficiency, yet current designers treat this as conditional graph generation: a variational, autoregressive, or diffusion decoder searches the $N \times N$ adjacency space, and a graph-network proxy trained on utility and a structural cost such as edge count ranks the sampled candidates. We argue that this formulation is misaligned with the problem. Empirically, topologies that survive a reward filter collapse to about six distinct graphs even when the codebook capacity grows from 8 to 64; edge count is negatively correlated with measured token consumption (Pearson $r \approx -0.4$), so sparsifying the graph makes inference more expensive; and a message-passing scorer over agent-profile nodes is adjacency-invariant whenever agents share a profile---the default configuration of published benchmarks---so it cannot rank candidates at all in that regime. These three facts motivate Codebook Agent: a vector-quantized autoencoder compresses successful topologies into a query-independent 16-entry codebook; a reward-weighted MLP maps the query embedding to a distribution over codes; and an MLP proxy that reads the flattened adjacency, regressed on measured utility and per-task normalized token cost, reranks the top decoded candidates in a single batched forward pass. With no iterative search and no message passing at test time, Codebook Agent is the most accurate method on all six benchmarks we compare (84.6 average against 83.0 for the strongest prior designer), emits a topology in 2.4 ms, and uses 21.9--33.2% fewer LLM tokens.

</details>


### [12] [SSAKG 2.0: An Open-Source Package for Structural Associative Sequence Memory and Context-Based Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01849)
*Przemysław Stokłosa,Janusz A. Starzyk,Paweł Raif*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SSAKG 2.0 is an open-source Python/C package for constructing Structural Sequential Associative Knowledge Graphs that represent objects as vertices and ordered sequences as connection patterns, enabling sequence reconstruction from partial context through efficient memory-aware graph operations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to provide an efficient system for storing and retrieving sequences through associative memory, addressing the computational and memory overhead challenges of working with large sparse graphs while enabling sequence reconstruction from partial, unordered contexts.

Method: SSAKG 2.0 uses a hybrid Python/C implementation where Python provides high-level programming flexibility and C handles performance-critical graph operations. It introduces new algorithms that exploit individual bits of computer memory for efficient graph connection searching, representing objects as graph vertices and ordered sequences as structural patterns of connections.

Result: The package successfully stores and reconstructs sequences from partial contexts across three datasets: randomly generated numerical sequences, NLTK corpus sentences, and mRNA sequences. Evaluation shows the system's capability to handle sequence retrieval while allowing analysis of graph density, sequence length, and memory size effects on performance.

Conclusion: SSAKG 2.0 provides a practical, efficient open-source solution for associative memory applications that require sequence storage and reconstruction from partial contexts, with a hybrid architecture balancing programming flexibility and computational performance for large sparse graphs.

Abstract: This article presents SSAKG 2.0, an open-source software package for constructing and operating Structural Sequential Associative Knowledge Graphs (SSAKGs). An SSAKG represents objects as graph vertices and ordered sequences as structural patterns of graph connections. The resulting sparse graph is used as an associative memory in which complete sequences can be reconstructed from a partial, unordered context.
  Version 2.0 introduces new algorithms that exploit individual bits of computer memory to efficiently search graph connections. The package is implemented in Python, while performance-critical graph operations are implemented in C and exposed through a Python interface. This hybrid implementation provides a flexible high-level programming environment while reducing the memory and computational overhead associated with large sparse graphs.
  The algorithms were evaluated using randomly generated numerical sequences, sequences derived from sentences in the NLTK corpus, and mRNA sequences. The experiments demonstrate the ability of the package to store and reconstruct sequences from partial contexts and provide a basis for evaluating the effects of graph density, sequence length, and memory size on retrieval performance.
  SSAKG 2.0 is distributed under the Apache 2.0 open-source license. The package includes documentation and reproducible examples and is publicly available through GitHub and the Python Package Index (PyPI).

</details>


### [13] [Collective creativity in hybrid societies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02620)
*Mason Youngblood,Katie Mudd,Manuel Anglada-Tort,Cameron Jones,Elena Miu,Diana Omigie,Margaret Schedel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Generative AI transforms creativity from an individual trait to a property of human-AI hybrid collectives, where AI increases individual novelty but risks reducing collective diversity, with outcomes depending on how humans and AI systems are composed and interact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the debate about whether generative AI enriches or impoverishes culture by arguing that current disagreement stems from conflating two distinct components of creativity: novelty (property of single artifacts) and diversity (property of populations). Researchers need a better framework to understand how AI actually affects creative processes and outcomes.

Method: The authors propose conceptualizing creativity as a property of hybrid collectives - populations of interacting people and algorithms rather than individuals. They analyze how AI-assisted ideation affects both novelty and diversity, examining complementary search behaviors between humans and AI models, and studying how composition (which agents, in what proportion, and how connected) determines outcomes.

Result: AI-assisted ideation reliably increases novelty of individual outputs but narrows diversity in aggregate. However, mixed human-AI groups can outperform and out-diversify groups of either kind alone when properly composed. Machine-discovered solutions can enter human culture and persist. The key finding is that outcomes depend on composition: which agents are present, in what proportion, and how they are connected.

Conclusion: Creativity in the generative AI era should be understood as emerging from hybrid human-AI collectives rather than individuals. The critical question shifts from whether AI helps or harms creativity to which specific mixtures of human and AI agents allow individual gains (increased novelty) to accumulate without eroding collective diversity. Proper composition of these hybrid systems determines creative outcomes.

Abstract: Generative AI is changing how cultural artifacts are created and circulated, and with it our understanding of creativity itself. Researchers disagree about whether these tools enrich or impoverish culture, and we argue that much of that disagreement comes from conflating two distinct components of creativity: novelty, a property of single artifacts, and diversity, a property of populations. We argue further that creativity in the context of generative AI is best understood as a property of hybrid collectives, or populations of interacting people and algorithms, rather than of individuals. AI-assisted ideation reliably raises the novelty of individual output while narrowing diversity in the aggregate, but this is not an inevitable consequence of putting machines in the loop. Because humans and models search in complementary ways, mixed groups can outperform and out-diversify groups of either kind alone, and machine-discovered solutions can enter human culture and persist there. What decides the outcome is composition: which agents are present, in what proportion, and how they are connected. The question is no longer whether AI helps or harms creativity, but which mixtures let individual gains accumulate without eroding collective diversity.

</details>


### [14] [The Memory Trust Gap: Capability-Dependent Failures in Persistent-Memory Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01852)
*Jundong Hu,Shekar Ramachandran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Large language models consistently trust stale stored facts over current evidence, with over-trust patterns varying by model size - smaller models benefit from memory while larger models are more harmed by it when stale information looks current.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persistent memory in personalized agents can be harmful when stale stored facts override current authoritative evidence, creating a "Memory Trust Gap" where models over-trust outdated information without warning.

Method: Evaluated a frozen, closed-set, action-scored benchmark with 2 suites: Benefit suite (requires memory) and Safety suite (has authoritative tools). Tested across Qwen3 model series (0.6/1.7/4/8B) with a 2×2×2×2 factorial design examining features like labels, recency, source authority, and position. Also validated findings on Llama-Instruct series and external datasets (RGB, MisBench).

Result: Models answer with stale values 92-100% of the time in Benefit suite across all scales. In Safety suite, harm is capability-gated: larger models collapse more when stale notes appear current. Feature impact depends on model scale - removing labels amplifies over-trust at all sizes, while recency features fool larger models more. Mitigation strategies are capability-dependent.

Conclusion: Memory over-trust is a significant problem in LLMs, with harm patterns scaling with model capability. Larger models are paradoxically more vulnerable to stale information that appears current, requiring different mitigation strategies than smaller models. This creates a fundamental tension between memory benefits and safety.

Abstract: Persistent memory supports personalized agents, but a stale stored fact can override current authoritative evidence without warning. We study when this harm begins as model capability changes. We evaluate a frozen, closed-set, action-scored benchmark with 2 suites that represent 2 different meanings of "no memory" (a Benefit suite, unsolvable without the stored fact, and a Safety suite, in which an authoritative tool always holds the correct value), on a same-family model-size series (Qwen3 0.6/1.7/4/8B). The Memory Trust Gap reflects over-trust rather than confusion. In the Benefit suite, models answer with the stale value 0.92-1.00 of the time at every scale. In the Safety suite, harm below the no-memory baseline under the trap conditions ($Δ_{\mathrm{mem}}$) is capability-gated, with the larger models collapsing most once a stale note is made to look current. In a $2\times2\times2\times2$ factorial, which feature triggers over-trust depends on both the feature and model scale. Removing a label amplifies over-trust at every size, and a recency feature (stale dated newer) fools the larger models harder. Source authority is weak and scale-flat, and position changes from positive to negative across the Qwen3 model-size series. We confirm these scale interactions with direct cross-size contrast tests rather than overlapping per-model intervals. Mitigation is likewise capability-dependent: exposing metadata improves accuracy for the capable models, but only pre-resolving the conflict restores accuracy for the 2 smaller checkpoints. The same pattern appears on the capable models in an independent Llama-Instruct model-size series and on 2 external datasets (RGB, MisBench). A framing control finds no consistent advantage for the memory label: at the 3 smaller scales, models trust a stale document more than a stale memory; at 8B, the difference is not significant.

</details>


### [15] [Belief-Calibrated Optimization: An Explicit World Model for Agentic Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01861)
*Yuhan Chen,Zhihua Tian,Mahavir Dabas,Charith Peris,Rahul Gupta,Ming Jin,Feiyang Kang,Siyuan Zhang,Nan Wang,Ruoxi Jia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BCO (Belief-Calibrated Optimization) improves LLM agent performance by making implicit beliefs explicit as a persistent world model document that guides iterative scaffold optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent optimization uses coding agents that keep beliefs about environmental responses implicit in their reasoning or parameters. This prevents later optimization steps from leveraging these beliefs, limiting scaffold improvement effectiveness.

Method: BCO writes down beliefs about how the environment responds to edits as a persistent in-context document (world model), which is continually revised as new candidates are evaluated. This world model guides the optimization process alongside standard evaluation loops.

Result: BCO achieves higher train passrates than matched controls across five benchmarks (memory QA, tool-use QA, code-as-action app agents, terminal agents), maintains gaps on held-out splits, and shows robustness after target-model swaps. Offline ablation confirms the world model's content provides reusable predictive information.

Conclusion: Making implicit optimization beliefs explicit as a persistent, revisable world model significantly improves LLM agent scaffold optimization, with the world model's content (not just its form) carrying reusable information about environmental responses to edits.

Abstract: The performance of an LLM agent depends on the scaffold around a frozen model. A common way to improve that scaffold is to use a coding agent as an optimizer: it reads current scores and traces and iteratively edits the source, producing a new candidate each round. Each edit is chosen according to a belief about how the environment will respond: what went wrong, and which change should help. That belief is typically implicit. It lives in the coding agent's reasoning on the current call, or remains latent in its parameters, rather than as something written down. Later calls therefore see scores and traces, but they do not use that belief. We introduce Belief-Calibrated Optimization (BCO), a method that writes that belief down as a persistent in-context document and continually revises that document as new candidates are evaluated. The resulting document is a world model: the current account of how the environment responds to edits. Added to an otherwise standard loop, BCO reaches a higher train passrate than a matched control that lacks only the world model, on five benchmarks spanning memory QA, tool-use QA, code-as-action app agents, and terminal agents. The gap remains on every held-out split, which is not used to select the candidate. After a target-model swap, in which the frozen model is replaced and the scaffold is not, the selected BCO scaffold leads on the tasks we test, except where context-window overruns leave it unfinished. An offline ablation then asks whether that gap comes from what the world model says. A fresh predictor given the accumulated document forecasts how the environment will respond more accurately than predictors given either no document or a same-form copy whose content has been falsified. The comparison indicates that the document carries reusable information in its content, not only in its form.

</details>


### [16] [The Ceiling Is in the Channel: Auditing Learner Gaps and Measurement Frontiers in Clinical Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01909)
*Sayeed Shafayet Chowdhury,Nusrat Jahan,Snehasis Mukhopadhyay,Shiaofen Fang,Vijay R. Ramakrishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a framework to separate clinical prediction saturation into learner gaps vs. measurement-channel ceilings, provides methods to estimate these bounds, and shows that measurement channel changes yield higher performance gains than model improvements within the same channel.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical prediction models often saturate in performance, but it's unclear whether this saturation comes from limitations of the learning algorithms or fundamental limitations of the measurement variables themselves. Understanding this distinction is crucial for deciding whether to improve the model or collect better data.

Method: The authors propose separating saturation into "learner gap" (failure to extract available information) and "measurement-channel ceiling" (fundamental population frontier). They develop theoretical bounds using total-variation separation, cross-fitted ceiling estimators, label-permutation optimism floors, and underfit curves. They validate on three real cohorts and conduct a PRISMA-guided synthesis of 104 clinical tasks.

Result: Well-tuned gradient boosting nearly reaches the estimated frontier in some datasets, while deficient learners retain large gaps. Different measurement channels show complementarity rather than simple dominance. AUROC gains coexist with larger Bayes decision-flip rates, and measurement channel changes yield higher performance than model family changes within the same channel.

Conclusion: The framework enables systematic auditing of prediction saturation: improve learners when headroom remains, but focus on improving measurement when it does not. Structured clinical data shows diminishing same-channel gains but consistent benefits from changing measurement channels across diverse clinical domains.

Abstract: Clinical prediction can saturate for two different reasons: a fitted learner may fail to extract available information, or the recorded variables may impose a population frontier. We separate these quantities through the \emph{learner gap} and the \emph{measurement-channel ceiling}. Optimal balanced accuracy is characterized by total-variation separation, yielding architecture invariance, a sharp partial-identification result under replacement contamination, a cross-fitted ceiling estimator, and exact conditions for multimodal decision improvement. We add two finite-sample diagnostics, namely a label-permutation optimism floor and an underfit curve, and validate the audit on three real cohorts: UCI readmission ($n=99{,}343$), BRFSS diabetes ($n=253{,}680$), and NHANES HbA1c ($n=10{,}219$). Well-tuned gradient boosting nearly reaches the estimated frontier in UCI and BRFSS, whereas deliberately or practically deficient learners retain large gaps. NHANES yields a null difference between questionnaire and measured marginal frontiers but a significant joint complementarity gain, refining the simplistic claim that an objective modality must dominate. Across all cohorts, modest AUROC gains coexist with substantially larger Bayes decision-flip rates, and several architectures estimate similar frontiers while their achieved balanced accuracy differs sharply. A PRISMA-guided synthesis of 104 clinical tasks then shows that the same channel-level regularities recur across more than 18 disease categories: a broad but non-universal structured-clinical region, diminishing same-channel gains across model families, and higher performance when measurement channels change. The framework converts saturation from an empirical observation into an auditable decision: improve the learner when headroom remains; improve measurement when it does not.

</details>


### [17] [Looped Transformers under the Jacobian Lens: Does the Global Workspace Survive Recurrence?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01924)
*Wenlong Wang,Fergal Reid*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers investigate whether global workspace functionality emerges in depth-recurrent transformers (which reuse weights across depth) by applying workspace analysis techniques to looped and depth-recurrent architectures, finding workspace formation occurs but access patterns differ from standard feedforward transformers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work identified a global workspace analogue in standard feedforward transformers, but it's unknown whether similar workspace functionality emerges when depth is implemented through recurrence rather than distinct layers.

Method: Extended the Jacobian lens to iterated architectures using a virtual-unrolling adapter, then applied the full workspace analysis suite (lens fitting, readout, and eleven causal experiment families) to Ouro-2.6B (48 layers looped 4 times) and Huginn-0125 (4-layer core recurred 16 times), using Qwen3.6-27B (64 untied layers) as baseline.

Result: Workspace formation occurs in the iterated part of each architecture, but recurrence changes access patterns: Ouro reconstructs workspace content in every loop with linear transport unable to cross loop boundaries; Huginn carries content forward across all recurrences with operations limited to a sliding window of about two recurrences. Verbalizability of new content tracks per-iteration supervision, while steering of existing content does not.

Conclusion: Global workspace functionality does emerge in depth-recurrent transformers, but the mechanisms differ from standard feedforward architectures - recurrence fundamentally changes how workspace content can be accessed and manipulated across iterations, with access patterns constrained by architectural implementation details.

Abstract: Recent work identifies a mid-depth band of verbalisable, causally potent representations in a standard feedforward transformer --- a functional analogue of a global workspace. Whether the same workspace functionality emerges when depth is implemented through recurrence rather than a stack of distinct layers remains unknown. Looped and depth-recurrent transformers provide a direct test of this question because they reuse the same weights across depth. We extend the Jacobian lens to iterated architectures using a virtual-unrolling adapter. We apply the full workspace suite --- lens fitting, readout, and eleven causal experiment families --- to Ouro-2.6B (48 layers looped 4 times, deeply supervised) and Huginn-0125 (a 4-layer core recurred 16 times, trained for latent reasoning), using Qwen3.6-27B (64 untied layers) as the standard baseline. We find that a workspace forms in the iterated part of each architecture, but that recurrence changes how it can be accessed. Ouro reconstructs workspace content in every loop, and linear transport cannot carry that content across loop boundaries; writes and ablations must therefore span every remaining loop. Huginn carries content forward across all sixteen recurrences, while reads, writes, and ablations act only within a sliding window of roughly two recurrences. Whether newly injected content can be verbalised tracks explicit per-iteration supervision; whether existing content can be steered does not.

</details>


### [18] [Post-Training Ternarization of Qwen3-4B Capability, Effective Bit Budget, Storage Compression, and Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01962)
*Anirudh Malik,M Sparsh Mehra,Poojith Devan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents an ultra-low-bit quantization method for language models, converting a 4B-parameter Qwen model to 1.641 effective bits per weight, achieving 3.96 GiB model size but with performance degradation and slower inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ultra-low-bit language model quantizations have misleading labels (like "1.58-bit") that don't accurately describe the actual storage representation, retained capabilities, or runtime behavior. The paper aims to provide a comprehensive analysis of what happens during such aggressive quantization.

Method: The authors perform end-to-end post-training quantization of a Qwen instruction-tuned 4B-parameter model using KOTMS rotation, E2M-ATQ ternarization, and GPTQ-style error compensation from TWLA. This is weight-only quantization (activations remain at 16-bit precision) targeting 81.62% of model parameters. They also implement packing to reduce model size further.

Result: The conversion achieves 1.641 effective bits per weight, reducing model size from 8.29 GiB to 3.96 GiB after packing. However, task accuracy drops from 64.5% to 54.7%, with uneven degradation across tasks (BoolQ retains 84.6% teacher performance while ARC-Challenge retains only 43.8%). Perplexity increases significantly across all datasets. Inference speed is 4.6x slower than FP16 cuBLAS on tested shapes.

Conclusion: While ultra-low-bit quantization achieves substantial model size reduction, it comes at significant performance cost - both in accuracy/perplexity degradation and inference speed. The authors caution that compression alone doesn't yield faster inference and highlight the need for more nuanced understanding of quantization metrics beyond simple "bit" labels.

Abstract: Ultra-low-bit language models can reduce storage and memory bandwidth, but a nominal "1.58-bit" label does not fully describe the stored representation, retained capability, or runtime behavior.
  We study an end-to-end post-training conversion of Qwen, an instruction-tuned 4B-parameter model, using KOTMS rotation, E2M-ATQ ternarization, and GPTQ-style error compensation from TWLA. The experiment is weight-only: activations remain at 16-bit precision, so ILA-AMP is omitted. We evaluate effective bit accounting, task capability retention, perplexity, calibration sensitivity, checkpoint composition, and deployment behavior.
  The final conversion uses 1.641 effective bits per weight for quantized linear weights, with 81.62% of model parameters targeted. Across ten scored capability comparisons, accuracy falls from 64.5% to 54.7%. Degradation is uneven: BoolQ retains 84.6% chance-corrected teacher performance, while ARC-Challenge retains 43.8%. Perplexity rises from 13.639 to 18.748 on WikiText-2, 24.700 to 31.992 on PTB, and 19.831 to 28.966 on C4.
  A subsequent packing run preserves the ternary planes and scales, reducing reported model size from 8.29 GiB to 3.96 GiB with essentially unchanged perplexity. A separate third-party packing attempt was lossy and is excluded from the primary artifact claim. The packed artifact has not been benchmarked end-to-end for task accuracy or generation throughput. A preliminary Triton GEMV microbenchmark is 4.6x slower than FP16 cuBLAS on one tested shape. We therefore do not claim that compression alone yields faster inference.

</details>


### [19] [Benchmarking Language Models for Statistical Problem Formulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01982)
*Chen Wang,Junzhe Zhao,Xin Cong,Wanlu Deng,Ke Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs struggle with statistical problem formulation - the critical upstream step of translating user goals into specific statistical tasks and identifying relevant data variables, achieving only 72% accuracy on fine-grained classification and 63% on variable identification despite being benchmarked on 1,013 diverse statistical problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluations assume analysis targets are already specified, but in practice users arrive with informal goals and heterogeneous data. The critical "upstream step" of translating these informal goals into specific statistical problems and identifying relevant data remains unaddressed and challenging for LLMs.

Method: Formalized Statistical Problem Formulation as two subtasks: (1) Statistical Problem Classification and (2) Variable Identification & Role Assignment. Created StatFormBench benchmark from five cross-domain statistics textbooks and a data science case library with 1,013 samples covering 20 coarse-grained and 85 fine-grained statistical problem categories. Evaluated 14 open- and closed-source LLMs using zero-shot approaches.

Result: Best zero-shot models achieved only 72.0% fine-grained classification accuracy and 63.2% variable set overlap. No model performed consistently best across both subtasks. Enhanced prompting strategies yielded only limited or inconsistent gains, showing LLMs' fundamental limitations in statistical problem formulation.

Conclusion: LLMs have significant limitations in the critical statistical problem formulation stage, indicating that current LLM capabilities for statistical assistance remain incomplete without this upstream ability. The benchmark provides a foundation for improving LLM statistical reasoning and problem formulation skills.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as assistants for statistical and data science work, yet existing evaluations largely assume the analysis target is already specified. In practice, users arrive with informal goals and heterogeneous data, leaving the model to decide what statistical task is implied and which data are relevant. We first formalize this upstream step as Statistical Problem Formulation and decompose it into two subtasks: (1) Statistical Problem Classification and (2) Variable Identification & Role Assignment. We then introduce StatFormBench, a benchmark built from five cross-domain statistics textbooks and a data science case library, covering diverse problem types, data representations, and scenario styles. It contains 1,013 samples spanning 20 coarse-grained and 85 fine-grained statistical problem categories. Across 14 open- and closed-source LLMs, the best zero-shot models reach only 72.0 fine-grained classification accuracy and 63.2 variable set overlap. No model performs consistently best across the two subtasks, while enhanced prompting strategies yield only limited or inconsistent gains. We release the benchmark data on Hugging Face at https://huggingface.co/datasets/THU-CongLab/StatFormBench and the evaluation code on GitHub at https://github.com/THU-CongLab/StatFormBench.

</details>


### [20] [HeadWiseKV: Budgeted Per-Head Cache Residency for Hybrid Long-Context Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02029)
*Renjie Xie,Juncheng Yang,Aoting Hu,Mingxi Zhang,Liyao Wu,Zheheng Hong,Wei Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HeadWiseKV is a training-free framework that compresses residual global KV caches in hybrid language models by assigning each KV head a static multilevel history window, reducing GPU memory consumption while maintaining model quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context inference in hybrid language models consumes substantial GPU memory due to growing KV caches during decoding, which can reduce generation throughput. The residual global-attention layers in hybrid models dominate context-dependent cache demand, creating a bottleneck that needs to be addressed under memory constraints.

Method: HeadWiseKV introduces a training-free framework that compresses residual global KV caches while preserving native local, recurrent, and linear paths. It assigns each physical KV head a static, multilevel history window for predictable cache demand. The framework uses SeqCalib as the core policy-generation algorithm, which processes layers in execution order and conditions decisions on lower-layer policies to account for interactions across depth. A grouped-cache runtime materializes the selected policy as actual per-head KV residency.

Result: HeadWiseKV maintains near-Full-KV quality (RULER and LoCoMo metrics) across four hybrid long-context models. In fixed-model systems study with Qwen3.6-27B, it reduces sampled peak device memory by 8.59% at 112K context length and extends the largest verified successful context from 114K to 161K.

Conclusion: HeadWiseKV provides an effective training-free solution for KV cache compression in hybrid language models, enabling longer context handling with reduced memory consumption while preserving model quality, making it practical for real-world deployment under memory constraints.

Abstract: Long-context inference retains a growing key--value (KV) cache during decoding, which consumes substantial GPU memory and can reduce generation throughput. This bottleneck remains in hybrid language models because their residual global-attention layers can dominate context-dependent cache demand. We study how to allocate this state under an aggregate KV-residency budget. We introduce HeadWiseKV, a training-free framework that compresses the residual global KV caches of hybrid language models while preserving their native local, recurrent, and linear paths. It assigns each physical KV head a static, multilevel history window, making cache demand predictable before serving. We formulate this allocation as a restricted operational rate--distortion problem and propose SeqCalib as the core policy-generation algorithm in HeadWiseKV. SeqCalib processes layers in execution order and conditions each decision on the lower-layer policy used at deployment, thereby accounting for interactions across depth. A grouped-cache runtime materializes the selected policy as actual per-head KV residency rather than a mask over a full cache. We evaluate downstream quality across four hybrid long-context models and study physical residency and serving behavior on Qwen3.6-27B. HeadWiseKV retains near-Full-KV RULER and LoCoMo quality across the evaluated models. In the fixed-model systems study, it reduces sampled peak device memory by 8.59\% at a 112K context length and extends the largest verified successful context from 114K to 161K.

</details>


### [21] [Monitoring Web Agents Without Internal Signals: Observable Trajectories and Key-Step Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02057)
*Sitong Pan,Yipeng Shen,Yilin Lu,Caiwen Ding,Lu Cheng,Qianwen Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers develop methods to predict web agent failures using only observable trajectory data when internal model uncertainty signals are unavailable, achieving competitive performance with internal-signal baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable web-agent monitoring is challenging when model-internal uncertainty signals (like token logits) are unavailable, making it difficult to detect when an agent execution is going off track or heading toward failure.

Method: Developed two observable trajectory representations: Macro features (summarizing cross-step agent-environment behavior and feedback) and Micro features (measuring consistency of intention, action, and anticipated state changes through repeated black-box queries). Used key-step boundary labeling where the first critical error that remains uncorrected and leads to final failure is identified, preserving valid early prefixes as on track.

Result: Observable trajectory signals are competitive with internal-signal baselines across WebArena-Lite and Online Mind2Web benchmarks with five open- and closed-source backbones. The predictors support early intervention under fixed false-cut budgets and transfer across held-out website categories.

Conclusion: Observable trajectory signals support valuable risk prediction abilities for web agents even without access to internal model uncertainty signals, enabling reliable monitoring and early intervention capabilities.

Abstract: Reliable web-agent monitoring is difficult when model-internal uncertainty signals such as token logits are unavailable. In this work, we study prefix-level risk prediction for web agents using observable trajectory signals: given an evolving prefix, estimate whether the current execution remains on track or is tending toward failure. We derive two observable trajectory representations: Macro features summarize cross-step agent--environment behavior and feedback, while Micro features measure the consistency of intention, action, and anticipated state change through repeated black-box queries. Instead of inheriting the final result label, we label the first critical error that remains uncorrected in the observed continuation and is associated with final failure as a key-step boundary, preserving valid early prefixes of failed trajectories as on track. Across WebArena-Lite and Online Mind2Web web agent benchmarks with five open- and closed-source backbones, observable trajectory signals are competitive with internal-signal baselines. The resulting predictors also support early intervention under fixed false-cut budgets and transfer across held-out website categories. These findings show that observable trajectory signals support valuable risk prediction abilities.

</details>


### [22] [DocHop: Benchmarking Out-of-domain Multi-hop Reasoning in Information-Dense Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02059)
*Zhuoran Yu,Le Thien Phuc Nguyen,Jaden Park,Xinyi Gu,Zexue He,Soochahn Lee,Rogerio Feris,Yong Jae Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DocHop is a new benchmark for testing multimodal LLMs on integrated chart-context reasoning in document images, where models must use textual narrative to determine how to select, interpret, and aggregate data from multiple charts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks evaluate chart and document understanding in isolation, missing the crucial ability of models to use textual context to guide how chart evidence should be selected, interpreted, and aggregated. There's a need to test multimodal models on integrated reasoning across both narrative context and visual chart data.

Method: Created DocHop benchmark via a stochastic logic-first generation pipeline with controllable reasoning depth and visual density, covering 2,074 examples across six task categories. Questions are grounded on semantic reference labels in the narrative, requiring entity resolution before evidence aggregation across multiple charts.

Result: Experiments show substantial gap: human annotators achieve >90% accuracy, while best model reaches only 62.83%. Reasoning-enhanced models perform better but degrade with increased complexity. The benchmark reveals current models' limitations in multi-hop document reasoning.

Conclusion: DocHop provides a controlled testbed for challenging multi-hop document reasoning, highlighting current multimodal models' deficiencies in integrated chart-context understanding and offering systematic evaluation capabilities through its structured generation approach.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong performance on structured visual understanding tasks such as chart and document question answering. However, existing benchmarks typically evaluate these domains in isolation, leaving underexplored a key capability: whether models can use textual context to determine how chart evidence should be selected, interpreted, and aggregated. We introduce DocHop, a benchmark for integrated chart--context reasoning in document-style images. In DocHop, the document narrative specifies multi-step compositional constraints, while charts provide the corresponding data values. Questions are grounded on a semantic reference label defined in the narrative, requiring models to resolve target entities from context before aggregating evidence across multiple charts. To enable systematic evaluation, we construct DocHop via a stochastic logic-first generation pipeline with controllable reasoning depth and visual density, covering 2,074 examples across six task categories. Experiments on a wide range of proprietary and open-source MLLMs show a substantial gap to human performance: annotators achieve over 90% accuracy, while the best model reaches only 62.83%. Reasoning-enhanced models consistently show improved results, but performance degrades as reasoning complexity increases. Overall, DocHop provides a controlled testbed for challenging multi-hop document reasoning.

</details>


### [23] [MineTRACE: An Evidence-Grounded Interactive Reasoning System for Mineral Prospectivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02060)
*Yiran Zhang,Jinwen Liu,Daniel Su,Yisu Chen,Qiang Sun,Chris Gonzalez,Eun-Jung Holden,Marco Fiorentini,Wei Liu,Yihao Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MineTRACE is a web-based system for transparent mineral exploration that provides evidence-grounded prospectivity analysis for 8 commodities using natural language interaction and interpretable scoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mineral exploration requires integrating heterogeneous geochemical, geophysical, and geological evidence, but existing prospectivity systems often provide only opaque scores or heatmaps without clear supporting evidence or interpretability.

Method: A web-based system with transparent expert tree (informed by geological knowledge and known deposits) that combines multi-source evidence into interpretable prospectivity scores. Features conversational assistant for natural language querying, location/region exploration, and evidence inspection.

Result: The system achieves spatial AUC values up to 0.917 across different test scenarios. End-to-end evaluation assesses query accuracy and response grounding. Supports exploration of 8 commodities: Cu, Au, Ni, W, Sn, Co, Ta, and Mn.

Conclusion: MineTRACE makes public geoscience data more accessible, interpretable, and verifiable, supporting more efficient and transparent mineral exploration through evidence-grounded analysis and natural language interaction.

Abstract: Mineral exploration requires integrating heterogeneous geochemical, geophysical, and geological evidence, yet existing prospectivity systems often provide only opaque scores or heatmaps. We present MineTRACE, a web-based system for evidence-grounded exploration of eight commodities: Cu, Au, Ni, W, Sn, Co, Ta, and Mn. Users can explore prospectivity maps, query locations or regions, inspect supporting evidence, and interact through natural language. A transparent expert tree, informed by geological knowledge and known deposits, combines multi-source evidence into interpretable prospectivity scores. For a new location, the conversational assistant retrieves the score and supporting evidence from the analysis pipeline and presents them in natural language. The scorer achieves spatial AUC values of up to 0.917 across different test scenarios, while end-to-end evaluation assesses query accuracy and response grounding. MineTRACE makes public geoscience data easier to access, interpret, and verify, supporting more efficient and transparent mineral exploration.

</details>


### [24] [ToolGate: An Executable Acceptance Pipeline for Tool-Dependent Scientific Benchmark Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02067)
*Ke Zhang,Yankang Liu,Roya Zandi,Maziar Raissi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ToolGate is an automated pipeline for generating and validating scientific benchmark questions that require specialized computation tools, using three verification gates to ensure script executability, non-triviality, and solvability by tool-using AI agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current scientific benchmark creation requires substantial manual labor by domain experts, involving writing tasks, cross-checking, and adapting existing materials. Language models can generate candidates quickly, but the challenge is determining which generated items are valid and non-trivial for evaluating tool-using AI systems.

Method: ToolGate employs a three-gate verification process: 1) Executable solution script verification - ensures generated code runs with scientific software and reproduces the proposed answer; 2) Randomized no-tool screening - rejects candidates solvable by models from prompt alone; 3) Tool-using agent verification - requires a tool-using AI agent to solve each item within time limits. The system is instantiated with FEniCSx software.

Result: Starting with 500 generation attempts: 478 passed executable verification, 222 were excluded by no-tool screening, 121 more excluded by GPT-5.5 API, and from the remaining 135, a GPT-5.5 Codex CLI agent solved 130. After deduplication, 128 unique protocol survivors remained. The process demonstrates automated validation while preserving domain expert oversight.

Conclusion: ToolGate provides an auditable, automated pipeline for generating and validating scientific benchmark questions that require specialized computational tools, transforming repetitive answer checking and difficulty screening into a systematic process while maintaining final review authority with domain experts.

Abstract: Scientific benchmarks are commonly built by domain experts who write tasks and cross-check one another's work, or who adapt existing material from textbooks, published papers, and online resources. These routes can produce strong evaluations, but they require substantial per-item labor. Language models can reduce this repeated work by proposing candidates quickly. The remaining problem is acceptance. We target scientific questions whose answers require computations with specialist software rather than unaided reasoning alone. A candidate is invalid if its script fails or returns a different answer, or trivial if a model answers it without the software. We present ToolGate, which treats every generated item as a proposal and keeps it only if three gates pass. First, an executable solution script must reproduce the proposed answer when run with the scientific software. Second, randomized no-tool screening rejects candidates that models can already solve from the prompt alone. Third, a tool-using agent must solve each survivor within a fixed time limit. We instantiate ToolGate in FEniCSx with 500 generation attempts. The local-verification gate retains 478 candidates.
