For final reporting, we rescreen this pool after generation: two randomized no-tool screens exclude 222 from the reported pool, and direct GPT-5.5 API calls at medium reasoning (the API default) exclude another 121. Of the remaining 135, a GPT-5.5 Codex CLI agent with access to FEniCSx solves 130; exact deduplication leaves 128 unique protocol survivors. ToolGate turns repeated answer checking and difficulty screening into an auditable process while leaving domain design and final review to experts.

</details>


### [25] [CHIME: Credit-Aware Hierarchical Memory Evolution for Long-Horizon Agentic Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02074)
*Yongshi Ye,Tian Lan,Feihu Jiang,Muyang Ye,Bin Zhu,Qianghuai Jia,Longyue Wang,Zhao Xu,Weihua Luo,Xiaodong Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CHIME is a self-evolving memory framework that addresses credit assignment in planning by separating memory into planning and execution banks and using outcome attribution before memorization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-evolving memory methods have a credit assignment problem - they rely on final task outcomes as feedback, but outcomes conflate plan quality with execution errors and environmental factors, leading to biased and noisy accumulated planning experience.

Method: CHIME maintains separate planning and execution memory banks and follows an "attribute-before-memorize" principle: it first attributes each task outcome to the plan, execution, both, or neither, then updates only the corresponding memory bank.

Result: Extensive experiments on four long-horizon agent benchmarks show CHIME consistently outperforms state-of-the-art training-based and self-evolving memory baselines. It accumulates effective memory with fewer items, learns memory values that reflect downstream utility, and transfers effectively across backbone models.

Conclusion: CHIME successfully addresses the credit assignment problem in self-evolving memory by separating planning and execution feedback, leading to more effective and efficient accumulation of reusable planning experience without parameter updates.

Abstract: Planning is a central capability that enables agents to decompose complex long-horizon tasks into manageable steps. Test-time search and training-based methods improve planning but incur high inference costs or require expensive training data. Self-evolving memory instead accumulates reusable experience from agent interaction outcomes into an external memory bank, so planning capability keeps improving at inference time without parameter updates. However, existing self-evolving memory methods share an inherent credit assignment problem: they rely on final task outcomes as feedback, but such outcomes conflate plan quality with execution errors and environmental factors, so the accumulated planning experience is often biased and noisy. To address this problem, we propose Credit-Aware Hierarchical Memory Evolution (CHIME), a self-evolving memory framework that maintains a separate planning bank and execution bank and follows an attribute-before-memorize principle: CHIME first attributes each task outcome to the plan, the execution, both, or neither, and then updates only the corresponding memory bank. Extensive experiments on four long-horizon agent benchmarks show that CHIME consistently outperforms state-of-the-art training-based and self-evolving memory baselines. Further analyses reveal several interesting findings. For example, CHIME accumulates effective memory with far fewer items. In addition, the learned memory values faithfully reflect downstream utility: high-quality planning memories are more valuable than execution memories. Finally, the accumulated memory effectively transfers across backbone models. Code will be released at https://github.com/ATH-MaaS/Marco-DeepResearch.

</details>


### [26] [Beyond Outcome Gaps: Process-Aware Fairness Diagnosis for LLM-based Multi-Agent Decision Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02092)
*Yiran Zhao,Lu Zhou,Liming Fang,Yufei Chen,Jiafei Wu,Zhe Liu,Xiaogang Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based multi-agent hiring systems can show hidden fairness issues in decision trajectories that outcome-based audits miss, requiring process-aware diagnosis and repair.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional outcome-based fairness audits for LLM-based multi-agent systems can miss where risks arise within decision trajectories, potentially hiding unfairness even when final hire rates appear balanced.

Method: Developed SCOPED-Hiring pipeline: constructs controlled resume variants, runs role-based hiring committees, logs over 311K structured decision trajectories, and converts trajectory fields into quantitative fairness signals using six diagnostic lenses.

Result: Reveals that balanced final hire rates can mask trajectory unfairness (career gaps trigger suspicion, proxy cues shape judgments, identity cues lead to unequal investigation). Targeted repair reduces total layered burden by 72.3% while shifting hire rate by only 1.86 percentage points.

Conclusion: Process-aware fairness diagnosis can uncover hidden unfairness in LLM-based multi-agent decision systems and guide effective repairs that significantly reduce burden while minimally affecting final outcomes.

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) are increasingly considered for high-stakes decision-making, yet outcome-based fairness audits can miss where risks arise within the decision trajectory. We present SCOPED-Hiring, a process-aware fairness diagnosis pipeline for LLM-based hiring MAS. SCOPED-Hiring constructs controlled resume variants, runs role-based hiring committees, logs over 311K structured decision trajectories, and converts trajectory fields into quantitative fairness signals organized by six diagnostic lenses: final outcome, counterfactual, process, pathway, dynamic, and design effects. SCOPED-Hiring reveals that balanced final hire rates can mask hidden trajectory unfairness in multi-agent decision trajectories: career gaps trigger suspicion, proxy cues shape qualification judgments, and identity cues lead to unequal investigation. Targeted repair guided by these diagnoses reduces total layered burden by 72.3% while shifting the hire rate by only 1.86 pp, showing that process diagnosis can guide effective repair. Project Page: https://scoped-hiring-project-page.vercel.app/

</details>


### [27] [MASkills: Continual Skills Optimization for Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02094)
*Huaiyuan Yao,Xiaoou Liu,Charles Fleming,Tianlong Chen,Hua Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MASkills is a continual learning framework that optimizes multi-agent LLM systems through structured agent skills, enabling systematic skill evolution via refinement, induction, consolidation, and pruning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based multi-agent systems show strong performance but struggle with continual improvement from interaction experience. Existing self-reflection methods use experience memories that are hard to invoke, refine, or scale, while agent skills offer more actionable structured procedural knowledge.

Method: MASkills introduces a new agent-optimization pipeline with skill-conditioned credit assignment, hierarchical credit aggregation, and momentum-smoothed optimization. This enables agent skill libraries to evolve through refinement, induction, consolidation, and pruning processes.

Result: Experiments on HotpotQA, LoCoMo, and GAIA demonstrate the effectiveness of MASkills across multiple agentic tasks, showing improvements in continual learning for multi-agent LLM systems.

Conclusion: MASkills provides an effective framework for continual learning in multi-agent LLM systems through structured agent skills, addressing limitations of existing memory-based approaches and enabling scalable skill evolution.

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent systems have shown strong performance on complex tasks, yet continual improvement from interaction experience remains challenging. Existing self-reflection methods build experience memories, but memories are mostly hard to invoke, refine, or scale, while agent skills offer a more actionable unit: structured procedural knowledge that specifies when to act, how to act, and which resources or tools to use. We introduce MASkills, a continual learning framework that optimizes multi-agent LLM systems through agent skills. MASkills presents a new agent-optimization pipeline that integrates skill-conditioned credit assignment, hierarchical credit aggregation, and momentum-smoothed optimization, enabling agent skill libraries to evolve through refinement, induction, consolidation, and pruning. Experiments on HotpotQA, LoCoMo, and GAIA demonstrate the effectiveness of MASkills across multiple agentic tasks. Our code is available at https://github.com/DaRL-GenAI/MASkills

</details>


### [28] [READY or Not: Reliable Enterprise Agent Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02095)
*Veronica Chatrath,Bryan Zhu,Jingxuan Fan,George Pu,Soham Dinesh Tiwari,Soham Dan,Ryan Young,Yuan,Li,Yuang Yao,Apaar Shanker,Minglai Yang,Daniel Yue Zhang,Yunzhong He,Ying Liu,Chenguang Wang,Zhijun Yin,Yuan Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: READY is a framework for qualifying AI agents for enterprise deployment by evaluating reliability, human oversight burden, and cost, shifting focus from autonomous performance to practical deployment conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI agent benchmarks measure task completion but fail to address enterprise deployment needs which require reliability guarantees, acceptable human oversight, and tolerable costs. There's a gap between autonomous performance evaluation and practical deployment requirements.

Method: READY framework measures reliability and operating costs of human-AI systems, selects minimum-cost oversight policies meeting reliability targets, and statistically qualifies deployment on held-out cases using an open testbed that decouples workflow specification, execution, evaluation, and qualification.

Result: In a clinical-audit case study with 16 agent systems and 750 cases, READY revealed hidden differences: two systems with similar autonomous accuracy (72.8% vs 72.5%) required significantly different human review rates (39.2% vs 29.6%) to achieve the same 76% reliability target.

Conclusion: READY transforms enterprise agent evaluation from measuring autonomous performance to determining reliable deployment conditions and costs, providing explicit, statistically testable criteria for comparing systems, setting oversight requirements, and making evidence-based deployment decisions.

Abstract: An AI agent can perform well on benchmarks and still be unsuitable for deployment. Existing AI-agent benchmarks measure whether an agent can complete realistic professional work, whereas enterprise deployment asks a different question: whether an agent can meet a required reliability level, under acceptable human oversight, and at tolerable cost. We introduce Reliable Enterprise Agent Deployment (READY), a framework for qualifying AI agents for deployment on enterprise workflows. READY preserves each workflow's own definition of successful execution while applying a common qualification procedure. Given an agent, a workflow, and a class of candidate oversight policies, READY measures the reliability and operating cost of the human-AI system, selects the minimum-cost policy that satisfies a specified reliability target, and statistically qualifies it on held-out cases. The resulting deployment profile characterizes the supported operating point: reliability, human-oversight burden, and cost. READY is implemented as an open testbed that decouples workflow specification, execution, evaluation, and qualification, and runs on existing agent-evaluation infrastructure. In an end-to-end clinical-audit case study spanning 16 agent systems and 750 cases, READY reveals differences hidden by autonomous performance: two systems separated by only 0.3 points in autonomous accuracy (72.8% vs. 72.5%) require 39.2% versus 29.6% human review, respectively, to qualify at the same 76% reliability target under the evaluated oversight policy. READY thus shifts enterprise agent evaluation from how well can the agent perform the work? to under what conditions, and at what cost, can it be reliably deployed? By making those conditions explicit and statistically testable, READY provides a basis for comparing agent systems, setting oversight requirements, and making evidence-based deployment decisions.

</details>


### [29] [Semantic Signal-Assisted Inspection and Recovery Allocation in Reverse Logistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02116)
*Jiani He,Dingyan Shang,Yihua Xu,Shiqi Huang,Yan Lyu,Jize Li,Shangjing Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Semantic Signal-Assisted Decision Support converts return notes into condition factors and quality scores to guide inspection depth and recovery allocation under shared labor constraints, improving net recovery value while reducing inspection costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reverse-logistics operators must decide how to inspect and route returned assets before full condition observation, but full inspection consumes scarce labor resources. There's a need for methods to efficiently guide inspection depth and recovery allocation decisions.

Method: The framework converts return notes into a condition factor and signal-quality score. It uses keyword, phrase, and large language model extractors to analyze narrative evidence from returns. The approach is evaluated through simulation across three scenarios: IT decommissioning, aircraft maintenance, and consumer-electronics returns.

Result: Across 30 paired simulation seeds, keyword implementation improves net recovery value relative to structured-feature comparator with noisy full inspection while reducing inspection costs in all three scenarios. Score-guided targeting adds $53.9k per batch in aircraft scenario but has little effect in other configurations. Phrase and LLM extractors provide further gains in aircraft scenario.

Conclusion: Narrative evidence from return notes can effectively support inspection allocation decisions before recovery decisions are made, demonstrating practical value in resource-constrained reverse logistics operations.

Abstract: Reverse-logistics operators often decide how to inspect and route returned assets before their condition is fully observed, while full inspection consumes scarce labor. Semantic Signal-Assisted Decision Support converts return notes into a condition factor and a signal-quality score that guide inspection depth and recovery allocation under shared labor capacity. We evaluate the framework in three synthetic benchmark scenarios spanning information technology decommissioning, aircraft maintenance, and consumer-electronics returns. Across 30 paired simulation seeds, the keyword implementation improves net recovery value relative to a structured-feature comparator with noisy full inspection while reducing inspection cost in all three scenarios. A risk-blind comparator that skips inspection altogether still records higher value under the benchmark's purely economic objective. At matched inspection cost, score-guided targeting adds 53.9 thousand United States dollars per batch in the aircraft scenario but has little economic effect in the other two configurations; phrase and large language model extractors provide further gains in the aircraft scenario. These results show how narrative evidence can support inspection allocation before recovery decisions are made.

</details>


### [30] [Beyond Context Windows: Persistent Discovery Context for Data-Centric Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02129)
*Jalal Mahmud*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Persistent discovery context - a lightweight memory layer storing prior intent-to-object mappings - improves data retrieval for data-centric agents across structured environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-centric agents repeatedly perform discovery steps to identify relevant data objects, but these successful discovery outcomes are typically discarded rather than reused, representing wasted effort and lost context.

Method: Introduces a persistent discovery context - a lightweight memory layer that stores prior intent-to-object mappings and reuses them to augment future retrieval, evaluated across three structured data environments.

Result: Persistent discovery context consistently improves retrieval quality over metadata-only search, remains effective with automatically generated memories, works especially well in lexically sparse domains (where memory-only retrieval can outperform metadata-based retrieval), but exposes a reproducible interference failure mode.

Conclusion: Discovery outcomes constitute a useful form of reusable context for data-centric agents, suggesting that capturing and reusing these mappings can significantly enhance agent performance in data discovery tasks.

Abstract: Data-centric agents repeatedly perform a discovery step before planning or execution: identifying the data objects relevant to a task. Yet successful discovery outcomes are typically discarded rather than reused. We introduce persistent discovery context, a lightweight memory layer that stores prior intent-to-object mappings and reuses them to augment future retrieval. Across three structured data environments, persistent discovery context consistently improves retrieval quality over metadata-only search, remains effective with automatically generated memories, and exposes a reproducible interference failure mode. In lexically sparse domains, memory-only retrieval can even outperform metadata-based retrieval. These findings suggest that discovery outcomes constitute a useful form of reusable context for data-centric agents.

</details>


### [31] [EmoStance: Response-Side Affective-Orientation Control for Empathetic Response Generation via Emoji Weak Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02133)
*Ziyuan Jin,Yuxuan Ge,Zheng Tian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EmoStance enhances empathetic response generation by using emoji distributions as weak affective evidence to induce a latent control space, steering a frozen LLM to produce contextually specific and responsive empathetic dialogue.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Empathetic response generation requires deciding both what to say and how to respond to the speaker's affective situation. Traditional approaches treat emoji as output symbols or gold labels, but this paper uses them as weak affective-attitudinal evidence to model listener stance.

Method: The paper introduces EmojiDialogue (an extension of EmpatheticDialogues with emoji votes/confidence scores) and EmoStance model, which models source-side affective expression, predicts soft response-side orientation from dialogue context/speaker roles, and steers a frozen instruction-tuned LLM through continuous prefix embeddings.

Result: In blind pairwise evaluation with 20 annotators and 800 judgments, EmoStance achieves a 62.2% decisive win rate, showing clearest gains in contextual specificity and perceived responsiveness, while remaining complementary to external-knowledge methods.

Conclusion: EmoStance successfully operationalizes response-side affective-orientation control through emoji distributions as weak evidence, creating a latent control space that approximates listener stance and improves empathetic dialogue generation without modifying the base LLM architecture.

Abstract: Empathetic response generation requires models to decide not only what to say, but also how to respond to the previous speaker's affective situation. We formulate this as response-side affective-orientation control and use multi-annotator emoji distributions as weak affective--attitudinal evidence, rather than as output symbols or gold labels, to induce a latent control space that operationally approximates listener stance. We construct EmojiDialogue, an utterance-level extension of EmpatheticDialogues with emoji votes and confidence scores, and propose EmoStance, which models source-side affective expression, predicts a soft response-side orientation from dialogue context and speaker roles, and steers a frozen instruction-tuned LLM through continuous prefix embeddings. In blind pairwise evaluation with 20 annotators and 800 judgments, EmoStance achieves a 62.2% decisive win rate, with the clearest gains in contextual specificity and perceived responsiveness, while remaining complementary to external-knowledge methods. Code, annotation metadata, and reconstruction scripts are available in our GitHub repository: https://github.com/18277390221/EmoStance.

</details>


### [32] [FUSE: An Evaluating Framework for Dangerous Capabilities of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02168)
*Zhengyi Jin,Ru Zhang,Xiao Chen,Xinbo Liu,Jiaxuan Lin,Jia Huang,Jianyi Liu,Zhen Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A modular safety evaluation framework (K-D-H pipelines) assesses dangerous AI capabilities across models, revealing divergent safety profiles and non-monotonic capability evolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current fragmented safety evaluation undermines governance of dangerous AI capabilities. There's a need for standardized, comparable assessments of models' safety characteristics across different domains.

Method: A modular framework with three orthogonal pipelines: Knowledge (K), Defense (D), and Harm (H), using pluggable modules for scenario seeds, knowledge banks, hazard queries, and judge rubrics. Evaluated 12 commercial LLMs from four families with chemical-biological module and cyber pilot demonstration.

Result: Models show sharply divergent safety profiles - comparable knowledge doesn't mean similar refusal resilience, strong defense doesn't reduce harmful content. Family-level patterns separate Claude, DeepSeek, and GPT. Dangerous capability hasn't monotonically declined - newer models have deeper knowledge but only partial defense improvement. Good reliability (cross-judge consistency ρ>0.79, pipeline orthogonality ρ∈[0.32,0.52]).

Conclusion: The modular K-D-H framework enables standardized dangerous-capability profiling. Key findings show scaling and alignment progress don't uniformly translate to safety, and capability evolution isn't monotonically improving, highlighting need for continuous, multi-dimensional safety evaluation.

Abstract: Fragmented safety evaluation undermines the governance of dangerous AI capabilities. We present a modular framework that evaluates each model through three orthogonal pipelines---Knowledge ($K$), Defense ($D$), and Harm ($H$)---under a unified protocol, aggregating results into a standardized dangerous-capability profile $φ$. Pluggable modules supply scenario seeds, knowledge banks, hazard queries, and judge rubrics, while the core evaluation engine remains unchanged across domains; the CB evaluation is complemented by a cyber pilot demonstrating protocol transfer.
  Instantiating the framework with a chemical-biological (CB) module, we evaluate 12 commercial LLMs from four families. Our first contribution is a horizontal comparison of dangerous capability across models and model families: the three dimensions expose sharply divergent profiles---models with comparable knowledge differ in refusal resilience, and strong defenders do not generate less harmful content when they do comply---while family-level patterns further separate Claude, DeepSeek, and GPT models. The second is a temporal analysis of capability evolution: tracking $K$, $D$, and $H$ against model release dates reveals that dangerous capability has not monotonically declined; newer models deepen knowledge while only partially improving defense, showing that scaling and alignment progress do not uniformly translate into safety. Reliability is established via cross-judge consistency (bootstrap $ρ> 0.79$, 4 of 5 judges) and pipeline orthogonality ($K$--$D$--$H$ inter-correlations $ρ\in [0.32, 0.52]$).

</details>


### [33] [Examining the Vulnerability of Multi-Agent Medical Systems to Human Interventions for Clinical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02191)
*Benjamin C Liu,Dillon Mehta,Rishi Malhotra,Adam Zobian,Yong Ying Tan,Samir Chopra,Daniella Rand,Natalie Pang,Abhiram Gudimella,Kevin Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Human interventions at specific "fault points" in AI agent conversations can dramatically affect diagnostic accuracy in multi-agent medical systems, with correct interventions improving accuracy by up to 40% but incorrect interventions degrading performance by up to 6%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human interventions at fault points in AI agent conversations can alter diagnostic accuracy, but the specific impact on multi-agent medical systems needs systematic analysis. There's a need to understand how different types of interventions affect reasoning and diagnostic performance.

Method: Used the MedQA dataset to analyze simulated doctor-patient conversations. Defined fault points as moments where an agent's reasoning became most vulnerable to external influence. Measured how different interventions shifted reasoning patterns and diagnostic accuracy.

Result: Correct intervention methods improved baseline diagnostic accuracy by up to 40%, while incorrect or bias-related interventions degraded performance by up to 6% and increased diagnostic drift and uncertainty. The study also revealed behavioral similarities between cognitive biases in simulated agent environments and real-world clinical practice, including premature closure and susceptibility to misleading cues.

Conclusion: Identifying and guiding fault points with appropriate human interventions can provide a mechanism for improving diagnostic robustness in multi-agent medical systems. The research demonstrates that strategic human input at vulnerable reasoning moments significantly impacts overall system performance and reliability.

Abstract: Human interventions at fault points can alter the diagnostic accuracy of multi-agent medical systems. We defined fault points as moments in AI agent conversations, in which an agent's reasoning became most vulnerable to external influence. Using the MedQA dataset, this study analyzed simulated doctor-patient conversations to measure how interventions shifted reasoning and accuracy. Correct intervention methods showed an improvement in baseline diagnostic accuracy of up to 40%, while incorrect or bias-related interventions degraded performance by up to 6% and increased diagnostic drift and uncertainty. Beyond performance changes, our analysis revealed behavioral similarities between cognitive biases in simulated agent environments and real-world clinical practice. Examples included premature closure and susceptibility to misleading cues. Overall, these findings demonstrate that identifying and guiding fault points with human interventions may provide a mechanism for improving diagnostic robustness in multi-agent medical systems.

</details>


### [34] [ASCII Attack: Recontextualising Harmful Requests as Artistic Critique in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02215)
*Da Cheng Gu,Yifei Dong,Xinghao Yang,Yongshun Gong,Wei Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The ASCII Attack bypasses LLM safety alignment by embedding harmful requests in ASCII art, presenting them as artwork for critique, achieving 62% success rate vs 42% for plain requests across 11 models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety alignment in LLMs primarily targets surface-level harmful requests, leaving vulnerabilities to recontextualized content. The paper aims to expose this weakness by showing how simple ASCII art representations can bypass safety filters while keeping the harmful content fully readable.

Method: Single-turn black-box attack using ASCII art to embed harmful requests as artwork, paired with direct-question controls. Tested across 11 models and 8 harm topics, using harm-aware classifiers to judge responses. Compared framed prompts (ASCII art) against plain text controls to isolate the recontextualization effect.

Result: 62% of framed prompts were judged harmful vs 42% of controls. Best-performing model had 93% success rate. Attack matched/exceeded published single-query attacks on 4 of 5 harm judges. Effect is model-dependent (not topic-dependent) and doesn't diminish with scale. Nearly two-thirds of framed prompts showed judge disagreement, indicating measurement validity issues.

Conclusion: Current safety alignment focuses too much on surface form, leaving LLMs vulnerable to recontextualization attacks like ASCII art. The attack reveals fundamental limitations in harm detection that span across models and topics, suggesting mismatched generalization in safety training.

Abstract: Safety alignment trains large language models to refuse harmful requests stated plainly, but that training is applied mostly to surface form. Requests that only recontextualise the same operational content, changing how the model reads it, are therefore only weakly covered. The ASCII Attack is one such recontextualisation. It is single-turn and black-box: one message, with no access to model internals. It embeds a fully legible harmful request in ASCIl-art characters, presents it as artwork, and asks for feedback. Unlike ArtPrompt, it hides nothing: the request stays readable. The reply is written as artistic critique and can contain operational detail that a plain request would have been refused for. Every framed prompt is paired with a direct-question control, so the contrast is isolated from topic, model and decoding variation. The contrast identifies a bundled surface, not one isolated channel. Across eleven models and eight harm topics, a harm-aware classifier judges 62% of framed prompts harmful against 42% of controls. On the most susceptible model the framed prompt succeeds 93% of the time. A single query matches or exceeds published single-query attacks under four of five harm judges. The effect tracks the model more than the topic and does not diminish with scale. At least one judge dissents from the panel majority on nearly two-thirds of framed rows, which is itself a measurement-validity finding. That pattern is consistent with mismatched generalisation.

</details>


### [35] [PEARL: Path-Entity Aligned Relational Learning with Contextual Subgraphs for Inductive Knowledge Graph Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02216)
*Yunchi Yang,Longlong Li,Cunquan Qu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PEARL is an inductive knowledge graph completion framework that uses LLM-guided path retrieval and contextual subgraph modeling with contrastive learning to adapt path embeddings to structural contexts, achieving state-of-the-art performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing IKGC approaches treat relational paths independently of their surrounding query subgraphs, ignoring that path relevance varies across different structural contexts, limiting transferability to unseen entities.

Method: 1) Constructs query-specific contextual subgraph from query entities' neighborhoods, 2) Uses LLM-guided retriever to extract semantically relevant paths, 3) Builds bipartite interaction graph connecting paths, contextual entities, and global subgraph representation for adaptive path embeddings, 4) Employs dual-view contrastive objective to reduce noise from enlarged context.

Result: Achieves best average Hits@10 on WN18RR, FB15k-237, and NELL-995 benchmarks among compared IKGC methods. Ablation studies confirm contributions of contextual subgraph modeling, semantic path retrieval, path-entity interaction, and contrastive regularization.

Conclusion: PEARL effectively addresses context-dependent path relevance in IKGC by aligning paths with structural evidence through contextual modeling, semantic retrieval, and contrastive regularization, demonstrating superior performance and robustness.

Abstract: Inductive knowledge graph completion (IKGC) aims to predict missing links involving entities unseen during training, requiring models to learn transferable relational and structural patterns. Existing subgraph- and path-based approaches often encode relational paths independently of their surrounding query subgraphs, although the predictive relevance of a path may vary across structural contexts. We propose PEARL, a Path-Entity Aligned Relational Learning framework that models paths as context-conditioned reasoning signals. PEARL constructs a query-specific contextual subgraph from the union of the query entities' neighborhoods and uses a large language model (LLM)-guided retriever to distill semantically relevant paths. It then builds a bipartite interaction graph over paths, contextual entities, and a global subgraph representation, allowing path embeddings to adapt to local and global structural evidence. To suppress noise introduced by the enlarged context, PEARL employs a dual-view contrastive objective that promotes representation consistency under stochastic contextual perturbations. Experiments on WN18RR, FB15k-237, and NELL-995 show that PEARL obtains the best average Hits@10 among the compared IKGC methods on all three benchmarks. Ablation studies, efficiency analyses, and case studies further validate the contributions of contextual subgraph modeling, semantic path retrieval, path-entity interaction, and contrastive regularization.

</details>


### [36] [SkillGLoW: Procedural-Family Skill Consolidation for Self-Improving Agents on Long-Horizon Task Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02217)
*Ao Yan,Xin Zhang,Jiawei Du,Joey Tianyi Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillGLoW is a method for LLM agents to self-improve by organizing skills into procedural families that balance global priors with local regeneration, outperforming flat pools and single-document approaches across multiple domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM self-improvement approaches (global document or flat per-task pool) fail on long-horizon workloads where tasks demand different solutions: documents become too generic, while pools inflate with instance-bound entries that don't transfer well. The missing unit of effective reuse is the solving procedure shared by related tasks.

Method: SkillGLoW (Global-Local Weave) organizes skills around procedural families: local skills from task execution are aggregated into families and compressed into de-instantiated global priors. Instance detail is regenerated per task rather than stored. A commit gate admits priors only when real execution shows they don't degrade the deployed library.

Result: Across four benchmarks (mathematical reasoning, terminal automation, software repair, embodied control) and three models, SkillGLoW gains 17.2 points over no-skill baseline on hard tasks, with positive gains in all 12 continual-improvement runs (18.0 with local regeneration). Library holds one prior per procedural family, 3.6x more compact than per-task pool. Outperforms single-document optimizer on 15 of 21 cells and lifts success on unseen ALFWorld tasks from 73.9% to 83.9%.

Conclusion: SkillGLoW demonstrates that effective skill transfer occurs at the procedural level rather than task memory, enabling efficient self-improvement for LLM agents across diverse long-horizon workloads through a balanced global-local architecture.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly self-improve by writing and reusing textual skills, kept either as one global document or as a flat pool of per-task entries, though most of the evidence comes from domains with structurally similar tasks. On long-horizon workloads where each task demands a different solution, the two forms fail in opposite ways: the document collapses into generic discipline, while the pool inflates and its entries stay bound to the instance that wrote them. We argue the missing unit of reuse is the solving procedure shared by a cluster of related tasks, and build SkillGLoW (Global-Local Weave) around it: the local skills a task writes from its own execution are aggregated into procedural families and compressed into de-instantiated global priors, while the instance detail they hold is regenerated per task rather than stored; a commit gate admits a prior only when real execution shows it does not degrade the deployed library. Across four benchmarks (mathematical reasoning, terminal automation, software repair, and embodied control) and three models, the priors gain 17.2 points (hard) over the no-skill baseline on average, with positive gains in all 12 continual-improvement runs, and 18.0 with local regeneration, while the library holds one prior per procedural family, 3.6x more compact than the per-task pool. Under the same protocol GLoW leads a published single-document optimizer on 15 of 21 cells. Unmodified, the library lifts success on unseen ALFWorld tasks from 73.9% to 83.9%, evidence that what transfers is procedure rather than task memory.

</details>


### [37] [PhoenixNest-Video: Evidence-Grounded Multimodal Agent Framework for Automated Video Interview Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02231)
*Fan Yuxuan,Huang Miaojun,Zhang Haimei,Wu Jingshen,Liu Hao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PhoenixNest-Video is an AI framework for automated video interview assessment that uses evidence-grounded multimodal analysis and rubric-based reinforcement learning to produce transparent, traceable scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human-only interview evaluation is costly and inconsistent with high applicant volumes, while existing AI approaches produce opaque scores without traceable rationale.

Method: Builds semantic video graph as structured memory, performs rubric-conditioned retrieval with cross-modal verification across visual/audio/text, and uses Rubrics-based Reinforcement Learning with dual rewards for rubric alignment and score differentiation.

Result: Achieves 91.50% grade-level accuracy on VInterview-2025 benchmark, outperforming substantially larger proprietary models. The compact agent scores candidates in closer agreement with expert panels than direct prompting of larger models.

Conclusion: A compact, rubric-grounded AI agent can effectively automate video interview assessment while providing transparent evidence for each score, offering better accuracy than larger models and addressing the limitations of both human-only and existing AI approaches.

Abstract: Interview assessment requires per-criterion judgments grounded in behavioral evidence, yet surging applicant volumes have made human-only evaluation costly and inconsistent, while existing AI approaches yield opaque scores without traceable rationale. We introduce PhoenixNest-Video, an evidence-grounded multimodal agent framework for automated video interview assessment. It builds a semantic video graph as structured working memory, performs rubric-conditioned retrieval with cross-modal verification across visual, audio, and textual streams, and produces per-criterion scores anchored to the candidate's materials. A Scorer trained via Rubrics-based Reinforcement Learning with dual rewards for rubric alignment and score-level differentiation internalizes the discriminative structure of multi-level rubrics. PhoenixNest-Video attains 91.50\% grade-level accuracy on VInterview-2025, outperforming substantially larger proprietary models. A compact, rubric-grounded agent therefore scores candidates in closer agreement with an expert panel than direct prompting of much larger models, and exposes the evidence behind each score for human review.

</details>


### [38] [PGPO: Potential-Guided Policy Optimization for Multi-Turn Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02236)
*Yuyao Zheng,Haipeng Sun,Junwei Bao,Lemao Liu,Hongfei Jiang,Yang Song,Dejing Dou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PGPO introduces potential-guided policy optimization for multi-turn agentic tasks, using cross-trajectory credit propagation to provide finer-grained step-level credit assignment, especially within failed trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current group-based RL methods for LLM post-training provide coarse credit assignment in multi-turn agentic tasks with sparse terminal rewards. Even recent step-level advantage methods (like GiGPO) rely on final trajectory outcomes, causing actions within failed trajectories to remain poorly differentiated - effective actions receive the same unfavorable credit as erroneous ones.

Method: Proposes Potential-Guided Policy Optimization (PGPO) that: 1) estimates empirical state potentials from anchor-state-group return statistics within each rollout group, 2) derives action advantages from potential differences between adjacent states, enabling cross-trajectory credit propagation for finer-grained step-level assignment.

Result: Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show strong overall performance relative to recent group-based RL methods. Analysis provides evidence that PGPO yields more informative failure-side credit signals with negligible training overhead.

Conclusion: PGPO effectively addresses the credit assignment problem in multi-turn agentic tasks by enabling cross-trajectory credit propagation through potential-guided optimization, providing better differentiation of effective vs. erroneous actions within failed trajectories with minimal computational cost.

Abstract: Group-based reinforcement learning (RL) has become an effective paradigm for LLM post-training, but in multi-turn agentic tasks with sparse terminal rewards, it often provides coarse credit for intermediate actions. To obtain more fine-grained credit assignment, recent work such as GiGPO introduces step-level advantages for intermediate actions. However, these step-level signals still rely on the final outcome of each individual trajectory. As a result, actions within failed trajectories can remain poorly differentiated, so effective actions can receive the same unfavorable credit as erroneous ones. In this work, we propose Potential-Guided Policy Optimization (PGPO) for multi-turn agentic tasks. PGPO estimates empirical state potentials from anchor-state-group return statistics within each rollout group. It then derives action advantages from potential differences between adjacent states, enabling cross-trajectory credit propagation. This provides finer-grained step-level credit assignment, especially within failed trajectories. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show strong overall performance relative to recent group-based RL methods. Further analysis provides evidence that PGPO yields more informative failure-side credit signals with negligible training overhead.

</details>


### [39] [Task-Level Natural Language Priors as Learning Signals for Low-Resource LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02244)
*Jian Gao,Xiao Zhang,Xun Zhu,Miao Li,Ji Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PGT uses natural-language task priors as auxiliary learning signals during training, not just as input context, to improve LLM performance on ambiguous/low-resource tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with ambiguous or incomplete low-resource training data. Existing approaches use natural-language priors only as input context rather than as learning signals during training.

Method: Proposed Prior-Guided Tuning (PGT) perspective incorporates natural-language priors as auxiliary learning signals. Specifically introduces Contrastive Prior Steering (CPS) which adds positive and negative prior-conditioned auxiliary losses while keeping original supervised objective intact.

Result: CPS consistently improves over plain and prompt fine-tuning across multiple benchmarks: 97.6% accuracy on AmbiMath, 9.5% Macro F1 improvement on Jigsaw (even with 1/10 training data), and 8.3-5.2% non-entailment accuracy improvement on HANS while maintaining comparable in-domain MNLI accuracy.

Conclusion: Task-level natural-language priors can effectively serve as auxiliary learning signals for low-resource LLM training, providing guidance that significantly improves model performance on ambiguous or incomplete datasets.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often struggle when low-resource training data are ambiguous or incomplete. Task-level natural-language priors can provide useful guidance in such settings, but existing approaches usually treat these priors as input context rather than as learning signals during training. We propose Prior-Guided Tuning (PGT), a training perspective that incorporates natural-language priors as auxiliary learning signals for low-resource LLM training. Under this perspective, we introduce Contrastive Prior Steering (CPS), which keeps the original supervised objective intact while adding positive and negative prior-conditioned auxiliary losses to encourage task-consistent learning and discourage plausible but misleading alternatives. Experiments on AmbiMath, Jigsaw, and MNLI/HANS show that CPS consistently improves over plain and prompt fine-tuning. On AmbiMath, CPS achieves 97.6% average exact-match accuracy. On Jigsaw, CPS improves average Macro F1 by 9.5 percentage points over standard fine-tuning, and with 1/10 of the experimental training data slightly exceeds full-data plain fine-tuning. On HANS, CPS improves non-entailment accuracy by 8.3 and 5.2 percentage points for LLaMA 3.1 8B and Qwen 2.5 7B, respectively, while maintaining comparable in-domain MNLI accuracy. These results support our central claim: task-level natural-language priors can provide useful guidance as auxiliary learning signals for low-resource LLM training. Our code and data will be publicly available.

</details>


### [40] [LLM-as-a-Judge Is Not an Oracle: Why Self-Improving Agents Need Deterministic Guardrails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02246)
*Vansh Wahi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PROCTOR introduces a Teacher-Student loop with deterministic guardrails to prevent LLM judge failures in self-improving agent systems, demoting the LLM judge from oracle to advisor role.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM judges in self-improving agent pipelines are problematic because they have the final say on system improvement but frequently fail. The authors identified 11 types of evaluation signal failures across four classes: judge bias, harness/metric failures, ground-truth errors, and reward hacking, where agents achieved perfect scores through cheating rather than genuine capability.

Method: PROCTOR (a Teacher-Student loop) with stateful orchestrator controlling all tool access, stateless subagents for diagnosis and mutation drafting, and a Teacher that grades mutations under five deterministic guardrails: hermetic sandboxes, capability-disjoint roles, acceptance checks that outrank the Teacher, frozen holdouts, and engineered canary cases.

Result: The system prevented previously identified failures where agents cheated (e.g., reading cached answers for 100% pass rates concealing 68% true capability, corrupt ground truth causing deletion of correct rules). However, the Teacher itself is an LLM judge, so some failures still occurred.

Conclusion: LLM judges in self-improving systems should be demoted from oracles to advisors, with every change gated by deterministic verification layers they cannot override. Structural constraints and guardrails are necessary to prevent evaluation signal failures in autonomous prompt optimization loops.

Abstract: Self-improving agent pipelines have a problem at their center. An optimizer rewrites prompts to score higher, and the score comes from a judge that is itself an LLM. That judge has the last word on whether the system is getting better, and our position is that it has not earned it. The judge should be demoted from oracle to advisor: its verdict becomes one input among several, and every change is gated instead by a deterministic verification layer the judge cannot override. We reached this position by building the alternative and running it. Over months of running autonomous prompt-optimization loops in production across contract analysis, compliance review, and code quality, we cataloged eleven ways the evaluation signal failed, in four classes: judge bias, harness and metric failures, ground-truth errors, and reward hacking. Agents achieved perfect scores by reading cached answer keys from their environment, a 100% pass rate concealing 68% true capability. A corrupted ground-truth label caused the optimizer to delete correct compliance rules to agree with it. A syntactically broken prompt was promoted as the winner because a silent parser fallback improved the metric. Attempts to fix the judge by rewriting its rubric plateaued; the only reliable gain came from a structural constraint on its output order. In response we describe PROCTOR, a Teacher-Student loop in which a stateful orchestrator holds all tool access, stateless subagents diagnose failures and draft mutations they cannot apply, and a Teacher grades those mutations under five deterministic guardrails: hermetic sandboxes, capability-disjoint roles, acceptance checks that outrank the Teacher, frozen holdouts, and canary cases engineered so that a perfect score is itself evidence of cheating. We report the failures this prevented, and, because the Teacher is itself an LLM judge, the failures it did not.

</details>


### [41] [APEx: Distillation of Agent Procedural Experience for Adaptive Deep Research Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02253)
*Jie Ding,Rui Sun,Xinyuan Zhang,Zeyu Zhang,Xin Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: APEx: A hierarchical framework for deep research agents that organizes experience into trajectory memories and procedural skills, coupled through Executor-Distiller-Planner modules with reward-guided skill distillation and test-time reinforcement learning for self-improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep research agent methods either use verbose task-specific traces that burden decision-making or distill procedural skills that remain decoupled from downstream policy adaptation, lacking effective experience utilization for continual improvement.

Method: APEx organizes interaction history into instance-level trajectory memories and category-level procedural skills, coupled through a closed-loop architecture of Executor, Distiller, and Planner modules. These are optimized via a three-stage alternating GRPO training paradigm for reward-guided skill distillation rather than fixed-prompt generation.

Result: APEx achieves state-of-the-art performance on 7 benchmarks, surpassing GPT-5.4 by 14.7 points and the strongest memory-augmented baseline by 3.0 points.

Conclusion: The hierarchical experience utilization framework with closed-loop architecture and reward-guided skill distillation enables effective continual improvement for deep research agents, allowing ground-truth-free self-improvement through test-time reinforcement learning while preventing policy drift.

Abstract: Deep research agents augment large language models with external tools to answer complex, long-horizon questions through multi-turn reasoning. Learning from prior experience is crucial for continual improvement, yet existing methods either retrieve verbose task-specific traces that burden decision-making, or distill procedural skills that remain decoupled from downstream policy adaptation. We propose APEx, a hierarchical experience utilization framework that organizes interaction history into instance-level trajectory memories and category-level procedural skills, and couples them through a closed-loop architecture of Executor, Distiller, and Planner. The three modules are optimized via a three-stage alternating GRPO training paradigm, enabling reward-guided skill distillation rather than fixed-prompt generation. At test time, distilled skills serve as procedural priors for online Planner adaptation through skill-guided test-time reinforcement learning, allowing ground-truth-free self-improvement with skill-alignment regularization to prevent policy drift. Experiments on 7 benchmarks demonstrate that APEx achieves state-of-the-art performance, surpassing GPT-5.4 by 14.7 points and the strongest memory-augmented baseline by 3.0 points.

</details>


### [42] [CoMerge: Conflict-Driven Preference Optimization for Multi-Task Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02273)
*Mingjie Zheng,Zihao Chen,Wenqing Chen,Weile Yuan,Zhixuan Chu,Jianxing Yu,Zibin Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoMerge: A conflict-driven preference optimization framework that reformulates model merging as a preference optimization problem using naive merging defects as hard negative samples to learn optimal merging coefficients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model merging for multi-task LLMs faces parameter interference problems. Existing methods aim to preserve individual expert capabilities but don't directly learn from the degraded behaviors that emerge from naive merging, missing opportunities to optimize merging strategies based on observed conflicts.

Method: CoMerge reformulates model merging as preference optimization using a self-supervised, conflict-driven strategy. It leverages defects from naive merging methods (like task arithmetic) as hard negative samples to construct preference pairs without external annotations. The approach refines lightweight, tensor-wise merging coefficients through preference optimization to mitigate parameter-space conflicts while preserving task-specific capabilities.

Result: CoMerge achieves average normalized performance of 0.9968 on MergeBench, outperforming all data-free and data-driven model-merging baselines. On Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, it shows marked improvements on conflict-sensitive tasks like instruction following and safety, while remaining competitive with full-parameter fine-tuning despite optimizing only 1,445 scalar coefficients (vs full parameter sets).

Conclusion: CoMerge successfully addresses parameter interference in model merging by framing it as preference optimization that directly learns from merging defects. The method's efficiency (optimizing only scalar coefficients rather than full parameters) combined with strong performance improvements makes it a practical and effective solution for constructing multi-task LLMs without full retraining.

Abstract: Model merging provides an efficient paradigm for constructing multi-task large language models (LLMs) without full model retraining, yet it remains challenged by parameter interference. While existing methods aim to preserve the capabilities of individual expert models and mitigate interference, they generally do not directly learn from the potentially degraded behaviors exposed by naive merging. In this paper, we propose a conflict-driven preference optimization framework for model merging (CoMerge), which reformulates model merging as a preference optimization problem. The approach utilizes a self-supervised, conflict-driven strategy that leverages the defects of naive merging methods (e.g., task arithmetic) as hard negative samples to construct preference pairs without external annotations. By applying preference optimization to refine lightweight, tensor-wise merging coefficients, CoMerge enables the model to mitigate parameter-space conflicts while preserving task-specific capabilities. Extensive experiments show that CoMerge achieves an average normalized performance of 0.9968 on MergeBench, outperforming all evaluated data-free and data-driven model-merging baselines. Furthermore, on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, CoMerge yields marked improvements on conflict-sensitive tasks such as instruction following and safety, while remaining highly competitive with full-parameter fine-tuning despite optimizing only 1,445 scalar coefficients.

</details>


### [43] [SCX Router: Streaming Zero-Shot Model Selection with a Decoder-KV Classifier and a Real-World Task Ontology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02292)
*Ihor Stepanov,Aleksandr Smechov,Mykhailo Shtopko,Dmytro Vodianytskyi,Oleksandr Lukashov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A lightweight routing system called Router that intelligently selects the most suitable LLM for each task by evaluating model labels without costly generation, achieving better speed-cost-quality trade-offs than fixed model selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The proliferation of diverse LLMs with varying quality, price, latency, and capabilities makes manual model selection difficult and suboptimal. Current approaches struggle to maintain consistent speed-cost-quality trade-offs across heterogeneous inference endpoints.

Method: A 0.6B-parameter GLiClass-based router that assigns suitability scores to inference-time model labels without autoregressive generation. It uses a Qwen3 decoder with bidirectional scoring, preserves text-only KV cache across sessions, and encodes only new dialogue turns. Built using a comprehensive task ontology with 23 families, 115 task types, 345 routable subtypes, and 150,000+ synthetic tasks, with explicit separation of request prediction from per-task policies.

Result: The router outperforms mean candidate models across six LiveBench subsets. On a 1,000-task subset, it achieves top-1 score of 0.707 vs. 0.696 for the strongest fixed model, with benchmark-dependent performance gains. It also predicts task type, difficulty, reasoning mode, expected output length, and supports custom zero-shot labels.

Conclusion: Router provides an effective lightweight solution for intelligent model selection that consistently achieves favorable speed-cost-quality trade-offs by dynamically routing tasks to the most suitable LLM, overcoming the limitations of manual heuristics and fixed model selection in heterogeneous inference environments.

Abstract: The rapid proliferation of large language models (LLMs) and the growing diversity of their applications presents a unique optimization opportunity: selecting the right model for the task, while optimizing for speed, cost, and quality at a per-task level. However, inference endpoints can vary widely in quality, price, latency, context support, tool use, domain expertise, and reasoning behavior. This heterogeneity makes manual heuristics difficult to maintain and unlikely to achieve consistently favorable speed--cost--quality trade-offs on their own. We introduce \router{}, a lightweight GLiClass-based router that assigns a suitability score to each inference-time model label without autoregressive generation. The released 0.6B-parameter checkpoint combines a Qwen3 decoder with a shallow bidirectional scorer. Its decoder-KV execution path preserves a text-only key--value cache across a session, encodes only new dialogue turns, and evaluates transient candidate-label tokens without adding them to the persistent cache. The same checkpoint also predicts task type, difficulty, reasoning mode, and expected output length, and supports custom zero-shot labels. For task generation, we construct a task ontology with 23 families, 115 task types, 345 routable subtypes, 1,173 synthetic examples, and an orthogonal axis of 30 domains. Using this structure, we generate 150,000 verifier-scored tasks and 15,000 open-ended tasks. We then train the Qwen3 decoder on these tasks, while explicitly separating learned request prediction from per-task policies for attributes such as eligibility, cost, cache reuse, safety, and sovereignty. Across six LiveBench subsets, the router outperforms the mean candidate; on the selected 1,000-task subset, it achieves an aggregate top-1 score of 0.707 versus 0.696 for the strongest fixed model, with benchmark-dependent gains.

</details>


### [44] [Improving Evaluation Realism with Inference-Time Compute and Deployment Scaffolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02302)
*Axel Ahlqvist,Richard Guan,Juan-Pablo Rivera,Adeline Kassler,Dmitrii Troitskii,Alexandra Souly,Kai Fronsdal,Robert Kirk,John Hughes*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes two techniques (critique refinement and DISH) to make alignment evaluations harder for AI models to distinguish from real deployments, improving evaluation realism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is evaluation awareness - capable AI models can tell when they're being tested vs. deployed, which weakens the conclusions of safety evaluations. Current alignment evaluations may not accurately reflect how models would behave in real-world deployment scenarios.

Method: Two complementary techniques: 1) Critique refinement - spends extra compute to generate multiple candidate actions, refines them using feedback from the target model on how to make them more realistic, then uses the most deployment-like candidate. 2) DISH (Deployment-Imitating SWE-Agent Harness) - wraps the target model in an agent harness to reduce the gap between simulated and real deployment environments in coding settings.

Result: The techniques were tested on multiple target models and found to compose effectively: applying both yields larger realism gains than either alone. Automated approaches can improve alignment evaluation realism, and these improvements use additional compute more effectively than simply making audits longer.

Conclusion: Automated techniques can enhance the realism of alignment evaluations by making them harder for models to distinguish from real deployments. The two proposed methods (critique refinement and DISH) work synergistically and use compute more efficiently than simply extending evaluation length.

Abstract: A core obstacle to alignment evaluation is evaluation awareness: capable models can tell when they are being tested rather than deployed, weakening the conclusions a safety evaluation can support. We present two techniques that make simulated alignment evaluations harder to distinguish from real deployments. Our first technique, critique refinement, spends additional inference-time compute on each simulator action: the simulator generates multiple candidate actions, refines them using feedback from an instance of the target model on how to make them more realistic, and continues the evaluation with the most deployment-like candidate. Our second technique, DISH (Deployment-Imitating SWE-Agent Harness), wraps the target in an agent harness, reducing the gap between simulated and real deployment environments in coding settings. We test the techniques on multiple target models and find that they compose: applying both yields larger realism gains than either alone. Our results show that automated approaches can improve the realism of alignment evaluations, and that these improvements use additional compute more effectively than making the audits longer.

</details>


### [45] [SALA: Semantic-Aware Logical Alignment for Complex Reasoning in In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02336)
*Zhao Ji,Wenqing Chen,Zhixuan Chu,Jianxing Yu,Jingping Liu,Shanhe Zhao,Zibin Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SALA is a Semantic-Aware Logical Alignment framework that improves in-context learning for reasoning tasks by automatically learning task-specific reasoning operations and aligning reasoning sequences using dynamic time warping for flexible matching of problem-solving logic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional retrieval methods for demonstration selection in in-context learning rely on surface similarity and fail to capture problem-solving logic. Recent logic-based methods use rigid predefined reasoning steps and exact-match criteria, which are inadequate for handling flexible or diverse reasoning processes.

Method: SALA automatically learns task-specific reasoning operations instead of using fixed predefined steps, embeds these operations into a continuous semantic space, and uses dynamic time warping (DTW) to align reasoning sequences for soft, flexible matching of reasoning logic.

Result: Experiments across four reasoning benchmarks and three LLMs show that SALA outperforms existing demonstration selection methods. Further analysis confirms the effectiveness of operation induction and logical semantic alignment components.

Conclusion: SALA provides a superior approach to demonstration selection for in-context learning by enabling semantic-aware logical alignment that captures underlying reasoning processes while maintaining interpretability and flexibility.

Abstract: Effective in-context learning (ICL) for complex reasoning relies on selecting the right demonstrations. Traditional retrieval methods based on surface similarity fail to capture the underlying problem-solving logic. Recent logic-based methods address this by matching predefined reasoning steps, but the rigid rules and exact-match criteria is improper to handle flexible or diverse reasoning processes. To address the problem, we propose SALA, a Semantic-Aware Logical Alignment framework. Instead of relying on a fixed inventory, SALA automatically learns task-specific reasoning operations. It then embeds these operations into a continuous semantic space and uses dynamic time warping (DTW) to align the reasoning sequences. This approach allows for soft, flexible matching of reasoning logic while remaining highly interpretable. Experiments across four reasoning benchmarks and three LLMs demonstrate that SALA outperforms existing demonstration selection methods. Further analysis confirms the roles of the operation induction and the logical semantic alignment.

</details>


### [46] [Diagnosing with Insights: Structured Analysis of Agent Failures via Behavioral Abstractions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02371)
*Jiayi Bi,Yanjie Gao,Yuanmin Xie,Liqun Li,Tianyin Xu,Fan Yang,Mao Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AGENTSCOPE is a neuro-symbolic approach for diagnosing LLM agent failures by converting agent trajectories into structured representations and using neural invariants with LLM-guided reasoning to pinpoint failure steps and types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agents become more widespread, there's a need for reliable failure diagnosis methods. Manual analysis of complex agent trajectories is impractical, traditional software debugging techniques don't work well for LLM agents, and relying solely on LLMs as judges yields unreliable results.

Method: AGENTSCOPE uses a neuro-symbolic approach that abstracts agent behavior from trajectories into structured representations, introduces neural invariants to specify agent behavior properties, and employs LLM-guided reasoning atop these structured representations against neural invariants to identify failures.

Result: AGENTSCOPE significantly outperforms current state-of-the-art methods on public datasets (Who&When) and a newly created comprehensive dataset (AgentErrata) in terms of fault localization and attribution accuracy.

Conclusion: Integrating structured abstractions with LLM-guided reasoning enables effective, reliable, and interpretable diagnosis for agent failures, demonstrating the value of neuro-symbolic approaches in this domain.

Abstract: With the proliferation of LLM agents, the ability to understand and diagnose failures in agents is essential to achieving superior effectiveness and trustworthiness. As agent failures often manifest via long and complex trajectories, manually finding the needles in the haystack is untenable. However, traditional diagnosis techniques for software bugs can hardly address LLM agent failures, while completely relying on LLMs as the judge yields unreliable diagnosis results. To overcome these challenges, this paper presents AGENTSCOPE, a new neuro-symbolic approach for agent failure mode diagnosis. The key principle of AGENTSCOPE is to abstract agent behavior, based on its trajectories, into structured representations. Furthermore, AGENTSCOPE introduces the concept of neural invariants to specify agent behavior properties. AGENTSCOPE leverages LLM-guided reasoning atop the structured representation against neural invariants to pinpoint both the failure step and its type in the trajectory. We show the effectiveness of AGENTSCOPE on publicly available agent failure datasets (Who&When) and a more comprehensive dataset created by us (AgentErrata), where AGENTSCOPE significantly outperforms the current state of the art in fault localization and attribution accuracy. Our work shows that integrating structured abstractions with LLM-guided reasoning enables effective, reliable, and interpretable diagnosis for agent failures.

</details>


### [47] [Contrastive Explanations in Quantitative Bipolar Argumentation Frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02399)
*Xiang Yin,Nico Potyka,Antonio Rago,Francesca Toni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces contrastive explanations for Quantitative Bipolar Argumentation Frameworks (QBAFs) that explain differences between two topic arguments rather than just explaining a single argument's outcome.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Argumentation frameworks provide explainability for AI models in classification tasks, but existing QBAF explanations only address single topic arguments, lacking explanations for differences between arguments.

Method: Introduced a general form of contrastive attribution functions (CAFs) with established properties. Developed specific CAFs based on three approaches: removal, gradients, and Shapley-values, and analyzed their properties.

Result: Demonstrated practical usefulness of contrastive explanations in two real-world settings: healthcare applications and bias identification scenarios.

Conclusion: Contrastive explanations enhance the explanatory power of QBAFs by enabling comparison between arguments, providing more nuanced understanding of AI model reasoning, particularly valuable in sensitive domains like healthcare and bias detection.

Abstract: Argumentation frameworks are useful tools for representing and reasoning with information in a variety of settings, e.g. in supplementing AI models as they perform classification tasks, with a notable benefit of providing additional explainability. In this paper, we introduce contrastive explanations for Quantitative Bipolar Argumentation Frameworks (QBAFs), one such formalism. Unlike most existing explanations for QBAFs, which explain the reasoning outcome of a single argument of interest (i.e. a topic argument), contrastive explanations explain the difference between two topic arguments. We introduce a general form of contrastive attribution functions (CAFs) and establish a set of general properties they should satisfy. We introduce CAFs based on removal, gradients and Shapley-values, and study their properties. Finally, to illustrate contrastive explanations, we demonstrate their usefulness in healthcare and bias identification settings.

</details>


### [48] [UTP-Bench: Uncertainty-aware Travel Planning Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02421)
*Etcharla Revanth Rao,Priyanshu Karmakar,Shubhojit Mallick,Manish Gupta,Shreya Ghosh,Abhik Jana*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UTP-Bench is a benchmark for evaluating uncertainty-aware travel planning in LLMs, addressing stochastic disruptions like delays and crowds that existing benchmarks ignore.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current travel planning benchmarks assume deterministic environments and only evaluate static constraint satisfaction, ignoring real-world uncertainties like transportation delays, crowd fluctuations, and stochastic disruptions that frequently invalidate otherwise feasible schedules.

Method: 1) Introduce UTP-Bench dataset with real-world travel data spanning 504 Indian cities including attractions, restaurants, accommodations, and multi-modal transportation networks. 2) Incorporate empirical delay distributions and crowd-density patterns from major cities. 3) Propose three evaluation metrics: Buffer Adequacy Score (BAS), Crowd-Aware Timing Score (CATS), and Transport Delay Absorption Score (TDAS) to quantify itinerary robustness.

Result: Experiments with state-of-the-art LLMs (GPT-5, Qwen3, Mistral, Phi-4) reveal substantial gaps between model-generated and human-authored plans in temporal buffering, delay-aware transportation scheduling, and crowd-sensitive planning.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that current LLMs have significant deficiencies in handling uncertainty in travel planning, highlighting the need for uncertainty-aware benchmarks like UTP-Bench to advance robust travel itinerary generation systems.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently demonstrated strong capabilities in automated travel itinerary generation. However, real- world travel planning is inherently uncertain: transportation delays, crowd fluctuations, and unexpected stochastic delays frequently inval- idate otherwise feasible schedules. Existing benchmarks like TravelPlanner and TripCraft assume deterministic environments, evaluating only static constraint satisfaction and ignoring whether generated plans remain robust when such uncertainties arise. To address this limitation, we introduce UTP-Bench1 , a large-scale benchmark for uncertainty-aware travel planning. The dataset integrates real-world travel data spanning 504 cities of India, including attractions, restau- rants, accommodations, and multi-modal trans- portation networks. To model realistic disrup- tions, UTP-Bench incorporates empirical delay distributions and crowd-density patterns col- lected from major cities, enabling evaluation of travel plans under stochastic conditions. We further propose three evaluation metrics, namely Buffer Adequacy Score (BAS), Crowd- Aware Timing Score (CATS), and Transport Delay Absorption Score (TDAS), which quan- tify the ability of generated itineraries to main- tain robustness against transit delays and crowd variability. Experiments with state-of-the-art LLMs like GPT-5, Qwen3, Mistral and Phi-4 re- veal substantial gaps between model-generated and human-authored plans, particularly in tem- poral buffering, delay-aware transportation scheduling, and crowd-sensitive planning.

</details>


### [49] [CivBench: A Long-Horizon Benchmark for Tool-Mediated Agents in Civilization VI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02459)
*Austin Tudor David Andrews,Liam Wilkinson,Jamie Heagerty,Harry Coppock,Jakob Nicolaus Foerster,Rui Ponte Costa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CivBench: A benchmark for evaluating LLM agents in long-horizon, tool-mediated Civilization 6 environments using Model Context Protocol, revealing consistent patterns of under-monitoring strategic state and poor execution of stated commitments despite guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks lack the ability to evaluate language model agents in complex, long-horizon environments with extensive tool usage and partial observability. There's a need to understand how agents behave in sustained planning scenarios with hundreds of turns and thousands of tool calls.

Method: Developed CivBench, an open-source benchmark using Model Context Protocol (MCP) with Civilization 6 game environment. It exposes 76 MCP tools and converts visual game state to structured text. Evaluated four model families in 23 runs, measuring Proactive Monitoring Rate (PMR) and RAG@10 (whether commitments in planning reflections are executed within 10 turns).

Result: Two consistent patterns observed: 1) Agents significantly under-monitor strategically relevant state (query victory progress every 30-75 turns vs. guidance to query every 20 turns), with 7 of 20 detectable defeats having no query within warning window; 2) Poor execution of near-term commitments from planning reflections (RAG@10 between 48.2%-65.8%). Aggregate outcomes didn't reliably discriminate models.

Conclusion: LLM agents exhibit systematic deviations from guidance rather than capability absences, particularly in state monitoring and commitment execution. The benchmark provides measurable interface-level metrics that capture behavioral patterns beyond aggregate success rates, offering insights for improving long-horizon agent performance.

Abstract: We present CivBench, an open-source benchmark for evaluating language model agents in long-horizon, tool-mediated environments through the Model Context Protocol (MCP). A single episode spans 300+ turns and produces thousands of tool calls over a large action space, requiring sustained planning, state monitoring, and execution under partial observability. The environment exposes 76 MCP tools and a narration layer that converts visual game state into structured text.
  We use CivBench to characterise agent behaviour across four model families in 23 admissible runs. The sample is a pilot, not a model ranking: aggregate outcomes do not reliably discriminate models at this scale. Instead, we introduce two interface-level metrics that the environment makes measurable: Proactive Monitoring Rate (PMR), capturing whether agents actively query latent strategic state, and RAG@10, capturing whether commitments stated in structured planning reflections are executed within ten subsequent turns.
  Across runs we observe two consistent patterns under a shared playbook protocol. Agents under-monitor strategically relevant state that is available but requires explicit querying: despite playbook guidance to query victory progress every 20 turns, agents do so only every 30 to 75 turns, and in 7 of 20 detectable defeats they failed to query within the 20 turn warning window before game end. Agents also frequently fail to execute near-term commitments stated in their own planning reflections (RAG@10 between 48.2% and 65.8% across models). Both patterns arise despite tool access and explicit guidance, and we interpret them as deviations under instruction rather than absences of capability.
  We release the environment, scenarios, logs, metrics, and analysis pipeline at https://github.com/lmwilki/civ6-mcp

</details>


### [50] [Loom: Weaving Diagnostic Strands into Free-Text Consensus via Embedding-Space Reweighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02649)
*Ron Begleiter,Katya Egert Berg,Gilad Saban,Gil Shabat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Loom is a generative consensus framework that efficiently aggregates open-form textual hypotheses from modular heuristics using embedding projections and iterative reweighting, achieving Pareto-optimal accuracy-efficiency trade-offs for real-world Root Cause Analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monolithic LLM agents for tasks like Root Cause Analysis suffer from context limits, hallucinations, and high latency, while traditional weak supervision methods are limited to discrete classes. There's a need to bridge these paradigms for reliable consensus aggregation in industrial NLP settings.

Method: Loom aggregates open-form hypotheses from modular heuristics (diagnostic templates with episode-specific entities) by projecting them into continuous embedding space, using iterative centroid-based reweighting to resolve conflicts, then grounding a single lightweight LLM synthesis step with the consensus weights.

Result: On OpenRCA benchmark, Loom matches state-of-the-art autonomous agents on Bank and Market-2 datasets, slightly trails on Market-1 and Telecom, while using ~26x fewer LLM calls (single call per incident) and achieving ~33x speedup with an 8B-parameter synthesizer, occupying the accuracy-efficiency Pareto frontier.
