Conclusion: Loom successfully bridges generative and weak supervision paradigms for practical Root Cause Analysis, demonstrating superior efficiency while maintaining competitive accuracy. The deployment experience revealed insights about agentic depth vs. latency trade-offs, limitations in redundancy detection, and how deterministic consensus builds trust with Subject Matter Experts.

Abstract: Aggregating noisy, conflicting textual hypotheses into a reliable consensus is a fundamental challenge when deploying NLP systems in real-world industrial settings. While monolithic Large Language Model (LLM) agents offer unbounded expressivity for tasks like Root Cause Analysis (RCA), they suffer from context limits, compounding hallucinations, and prohibitive inference latency. Traditional weak supervision offers statistical rigor but is mathematically restricted to discrete classes. We present Loom, a generative consensus framework deployed for real-world RCA that bridges these paradigms. Loom aggregates open-form hypotheses emitted by modular heuristics (diagnostic templates dynamically populated with episode-specific entities, times, and metrics) by projecting them into a continuous embedding space, and resolves conflicting signals with an iterative centroid-based reweighting algorithm. The resulting consensus weights ground a single lightweight LLM synthesis step. Evaluated on the OpenRCA benchmark, Loom occupies the accuracy--efficiency Pareto frontier: it matches a state-of-the-art autonomous agent on Bank and Market-2 and trails on Market-1 and Telecom, while using a single LLM call per incident on all four datasets ($\sim$26$\times$ faster; $\sim$33$\times$ with an 8B-parameter synthesizer). We discuss our deployment experience, highlighting lessons learned regarding the trade-offs between agentic depth and inference latency, negative results in redundancy detection, and how deterministic consensus fosters trust among Subject Matter Experts~(SMEs).

</details>


### [51] [Door-in-the-Face Requests and Refusal Behaviour in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02707)
*Til Jordan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The door-in-the-face technique (starting with a large request that gets refused, then making a smaller request) works differently on various language models - it increases compliance on Anthropic's Opus but decreases compliance on OpenAI and Google models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The researchers wanted to test whether psychological influence techniques like door-in-the-face (DITF) that work on humans also transfer to language models. This explores how susceptible AI models are to human persuasion strategies.

Method: Tested nine production models from three providers. Each model was given a large request it would refuse, followed by a smaller version of the same request. Compliance was compared to asking the smaller request directly. Also tested control conditions with unrelated requests and analyzed request formulation.

Result: Anthropic's Opus showed increased compliance (65.8% vs 29.3% direct), but OpenAI and Google models, plus Anthropic's Haiku, showed decreased compliance (15.5-23.0 point reductions). The effect was specific to related requests. Refusal removal was achieved by reformulating refused requests as explanation requests rather than instruction requests.

Conclusion: Human influence techniques like door-in-the-face transfer to language models but differently across model families. The success depends on model architecture and how requests are formulated - explanation requests are much less likely to be refused than instruction requests on the same topic.

Abstract: Does the door-in-the-face technique work on language models? In humans, a large request that is refused makes a smaller follow-up request more likely to be granted. We test this on nine production models from three providers: each model refuses a large request, then receives a smaller version of the same request, and we compare its compliance with asking directly. The answer depends on the model. On Anthropic's frontier models the technique works: Opus 5 answers the smaller request 65.8% of the time after refusing the larger one, against 29.3% when asked directly. On the frontier models of OpenAI and Google, and on Haiku 4.5, it backfires, lowering compliance by 15.5 to 23.0 points. A control locates the effect: a refused large request on an unrelated topic does less than the related one on all nine models, so the concession itself matters everywhere, while the reaction to having just refused something differs by model family. The technique does not transfer to refusals drawn from public benchmarks. What decides whether a retreat can work is what the request asks for: rewriting 265 refused requests for usable instructions into requests for explanations of the same topic removed the refusal in 263 cases. Human influence techniques port to language models one model family at a time.

</details>


### [52] [Repo-To-Skill: Distilling GitHub Repositories Into AI4AI Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02749)
*Jianlyu Chen,Yuyang Hu,Hongjin Qian,Jiawei Liu,Wenqing Wei,Xiaolong Chen,Defu Lian,Zhicheng Dou,Chaozhuo Li,Qiwei Ye,Zheng Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DisCo is a skill-powered research agent that distills operational knowledge from ML repositories and papers into compact, verified skills that dramatically improve AI research performance across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current autonomous ML research agents lack operational knowledge - the practical know-how that separates knowing a method from making it work. This knowledge exists in repositories and papers but is in formats for human readers and too large to load during tasks, requiring agents to rediscover it each time.

Method: DisCo distills operational knowledge into skills through two complementary approaches: 1) Task-agnostic distillation that condenses widely used ML repositories into reusable skills, creating the AREX-Skill Library with 5,000+ verified skills from 1,000 repositories, and 2) Task-oriented distillation that produces skills specifically needed for concrete research tasks.

Result: With GPT-5.5 backbone and fixed execution budget, the skill-equipped agent shows massive improvements: 134.3% higher on MLE-bench, 34.4% higher on PaperBench, 9.2% higher on FrontierCS, and 14.0% higher on PassNet compared to the same agent without skills.

Conclusion: Distilling operational knowledge into compact, verified skills that can be reused across research tasks significantly enhances autonomous research agent performance, demonstrating that the missing operational knowledge layer is crucial for effective ML research automation.

Abstract: Autonomous agents are beginning to carry out machine-learning (ML) research end to end. These agents combine a model backbone with a harness for planning, execution, memory, and verification, but this architecture still leaves domain-specific know-how outside the agent. We call this missing layer operational knowledge, the know-how that separates knowing a method from making it work. That knowledge is not absent from the field. It appears in repositories and papers, but in forms written for human readers and too large to load during a task. Once distilled into compact, verified skills, this knowledge can be reused across tasks rather than rediscovered during each run.
  We present DisCo, a skill-powered research agent that creates skills and uses them during research. Its distillation runs in two complementary forms: task-agnostic, condensing the field's widely used repositories into reusable skills, and task-oriented, producing the skills a concrete task calls for. The former, applied across the open ecosystem, yields the AREX-Skill Library, with 5,000+ verified skills distilled from 1,000 widely used ML repositories and organized into 20 areas and 178 capability families. With the GPT-5.5 backbone, research harness, and downstream execution budget held fixed, the skill-equipped research agent scores 134.3% higher on MLE-bench, 34.4% higher on PaperBench, 9.2% higher on FrontierCS, and 14.0% higher on PassNet than the same agent without skills. These gains come from adding distilled operating context under that fixed setup.

</details>


### [53] [Bilevel Coordinated Reflection: A Game-Theoretic Approach to Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02750)
*Yihang Chen,Yuxiang Chen,Yuxuan Huang,Meng Fang,Weilin Luo,Jun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a game-theoretic framework for analyzing multi-agent LLM systems, showing that orchestrator-worker interactions form a bilevel coordination game with reflection as stochastic memory movement. It proves information-theoretic limits of text-only gates, introduces a grounded evaluation method (SRMA) with convergence guarantees, and demonstrates competitive performance on SWE-bench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multi-agent LLM systems lack a unified theoretical account of coordination, memory improvement, and the role of external verification. While orchestration-based systems show strong empirical results, there's insufficient understanding of their fundamental properties and limitations.

Method: Models orchestrator-worker interaction as a bilevel coordination game, analyzes reflection as stochastic movement over semantic memory states, proves impossibility results for text-only gates, and introduces Stochastic Reflective Memory Ascent (SRMA) with grounded evaluation gates and convergence guarantees.

Result: Theoretical results: equilibrium slack controlled by decomposition quality, finite-time upper bound for free-form reflection, information-theoretic impossibility for text-only gates. Experimental results: SRMA achieves 72.2% resolution on 500 SWE-bench instances vs 70.8% baseline, demonstrating the predicted coordination and drift laws.

Conclusion: The paper provides a unified theoretical framework for understanding multi-agent LLM coordination, establishes fundamental limitations of text-only reflection systems, and introduces a principled grounded evaluation approach (SRMA) with provable convergence properties that outperforms existing methods in practice.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems commonly use an orchestrator to decompose a task for a team of workers and then improve through textual reflection. Despite strong empirical results, these systems lack a unified account of coordination, memory improvement, and the role of external verification. We model orchestrator-worker interaction as a bilevel coordination game: under bounded coupling, the workers' local-update game is an approximate potential game whose equilibrium slack is controlled by decomposition quality. We then analyse reflection as stochastic movement over semantic memory states. For free-form reflection, we derive a finite-time upper bound, prove worst-case tightness, and give a positive lower bound under a falsifiable persistent-harm condition. We further prove an information-theoretic impossibility result: no gate that observes only the generated transcript can improve uniformly over text-indistinguishable environments, whereas an environment-grounded gate can. Motivated by this separation, we introduce Stochastic Reflective Memory Ascent (SRMA), which accepts a candidate memory only after a grounded evaluation risk strictly decreases. Under calibration and non-degenerate corrective mass, SRMA converges exactly, geometrically or polynomially; matching constructions show that both rate regimes are order-tight. We also provide confidence gating for stochastic evaluation and re-anchoring guarantees for piecewise-stationary environments. Experiments instantiate these objects with environment-grounded metrics and test the predicted coordination and drift laws. On 500 SWE-bench instances, the complete Kimi-based system resolves 72.2% versus a 70.8% public mini-SWE-agent reference. Code: https://github.com/YihangChen9/Bilevel-Coordinated-Reflection

</details>


### [54] [Measurement-Driven Sub-Network Selection for On-Premise Retrieval-Augmented Factory Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02760)
*Vasileios Rizeakos,Georgios Paisios,Alexandros Machairas,Michael Birbas,Athanasios Bachoumis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Factory AI assistants need to run on edge devices with limited hardware. The paper shows that model size doesn't predict quality after compression and retrieval adaptation, and proposes a deployment selection approach using weight-shared supernetworks and distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-premise AI assistants for factory workers need conversational access to machine documentation, but capable models rarely fit shop-floor hardware constraints. There's a need to deploy effective AI assistants on resource-limited edge devices in manufacturing environments.

Method: Use structural compression and retrieval-grounded adaptation, then treat deployment as post-adaptation selection problem. Implement weight-shared supernetwork trained with sandwich-style in-place distillation to keep selection inexpensive. Balance multiple constraints: general capability floor, memory budget, answer quality, and on-device throughput.

Result: After compression, model size no longer reliably predicts answer quality. Extraction costs 13.7% of unpruned model's quality, but retrieval-grounded distillation recovers to within 4.6% (recovering two-thirds of loss). Same assistant runs across three heterogeneous edge tiers at 1.3-5 watts standby.

Conclusion: Deploying factory AI assistants requires considering multiple optimization criteria beyond just model size. A systematic approach with weight-shared supernetworks and distillation enables efficient deployment across heterogeneous edge hardware while maintaining quality and throughput.

Abstract: On-premise assistants can give factory workers conversational access to machine documentation, but models capable of the task rarely fit shop-floor hardware. We show that after structural compression and retrieval-grounded adaptation, model size is no longer a reliable predictor of adapted answer quality: general capability falls almost linearly with parameter count, while judged retrieval-augmented answer quality does not. We therefore treat deployment as a post-adaptation selection problem, committing one sub-network per device on judged answer quality and measured on-device throughput under a configurable general-capability floor and memory budget; rules that optimize size, speed, or quality alone each give up capability or throughput. A weight-shared supernetwork trained with sandwich-style in-place distillation keeps this selection inexpensive. In a manufacturing-manual case study, extraction costs 13.7 percent of the unpruned model's judged quality and retrieval-grounded distillation returns it to within 4.6 percent, recovering two thirds of the loss, and the same assistant runs across three heterogeneous edge tiers at 1.3 to 5 watts standby.

</details>


### [55] [SafeEvolve: Harness-Policy Co-Evolution from Agent Experience for Safety Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02786)
*Qinghua Mao,Wanying Qu,Dadi Guo,Leitao Yuan,Qingyu Liu,Yu Li,Guanxu Chen,Yanwei Fu,Xi Lin,Xia Hu,Dongrui Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SafeEvolve is a framework for aligning LLM-based agents with safety through harness-policy co-evolution using safety experience from on-policy trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents face safety risks in both harmful final responses and multi-step execution trajectories. Existing safety alignment methods either use external harness updates or policy optimization in isolation, failing to bridge runtime control with intrinsic safety.

Method: SafeEvolve uses safety experience from on-policy trajectories to drive continual harness-policy co-evolution. On harness side: converts trajectory-level safety evidence into bounded component-level updates across safety prompts and hierarchical skills. On policy side: two-stage SFT-RL paradigm where harness-use SFT bootstraps policy to use evolved harness artifacts, and harness-augmented RL shapes autonomous safety behaviors during multi-step exploration using verifier-decomposed rewards.

Result: SafeEvolve achieves stronger safety-utility tradeoff than existing baselines. For Qwen3.5-4B, it achieves 3× ASR reduction on AgentDojo while improving benign utility from 59.79% to 61.86%.

Conclusion: SafeEvolve successfully bridges the gap between runtime control and intrinsic safety through harness-policy co-evolution, converting safety experience into both evolved runtime harness and improved policy behavior, providing a comprehensive solution for agent safety alignment.

Abstract: The performance of LLM-based agents is jointly shaped by the base model and the harness used when interacting with the environment. This exposes them to safety risks in both harmful final responses and multi-step execution trajectories. Existing safety alignment mechanisms often rely on either external harness updates or policy optimization, yet applying either paradigm in isolation fails to bridge runtime control with intrinsic safety. We propose SafeEvolve, an experience-driven self-evolving framework for agent safety alignment. SafeEvolve leverages safety experience from completed on-policy trajectories to drive a continual loop of harness-policy co-evolution. On the harness side, SafeEvolve converts trajectory-level safety evidence into bounded, component-level updates across safety prompt and hierarchical skills, yielding auditable and reversible harness artifacts. On the policy side, SafeEvolve follows a two-stage SFT-RL paradigm, where harness-use SFT bootstraps the policy to actively leverage evolved harness artifacts, and harness-augmented RL further shapes autonomous safety behaviors during multi-step exploration via verifier-decomposed rewards. Through harness-policy co-evolution, SafeEvolve converts safety experience into an evolved runtime harness and improved policy behavior. Experiments on agentic safety benchmarks show that SafeEvolve achieves a stronger safety-utility tradeoff than existing baselines. For Qwen3.5-4B, SafeEvolve achieves a $3\times$ ASR reduction on AgentDojo while improving benign utility from 59.79% to 61.86%.

</details>


### [56] [Large Language Models (LLMs) for Telecom Root Cause Analysis (RCA): A Structured Reasoning Framework for Evidence-Grounded Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02805)
*Hao Zhou,Mandar Kulkarni,Hao Chen,Yan Xin,Charlie,Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a structured reasoning framework for LLM-based root cause analysis in telecom networks that addresses hallucination and reasoning issues by organizing network telemetry and enforcing decision-path reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diagnosing performance degradations in modern 5G and emerging 6G networks is challenging due to complex cross-layer dependencies. While LLMs offer reasoning capabilities, directly applying them to telecom RCA leads to hallucination, unstable reasoning, and poor alignment with structured network evidence.

Method: The paper proposes a structured reasoning framework that: (1) organizes heterogeneous network telemetry into canonical contexts, (2) enforces decision-path reasoning during diagnosis, and (3) generates evidence-grounded explanations for reliable fault identification.

Result: Experimental results on two 5G RCA datasets (TeleLogs and TelecomTS) demonstrate that the proposed framework consistently improves diagnostic accuracy and decision consistency compared with baseline techniques.

Conclusion: The cross-dataset results highlight the importance of structured reasoning design for practical LLM-based RCA systems in next-generation telecom networks, showing that properly aligning LLM reasoning with telecom-specific evidence and domain knowledge is crucial for reliable fault diagnosis.

Abstract: Root cause analysis (RCA) is a critical task in telecom network operations, but diagnosing performance degradations in modern 5G and emerging 6G networks remains challenging due to complex cross-layer dependencies. While large language models (LLMs) offer promising capabilities for reasoning and knowledge integration, directly applying vanilla LLMs to telecom RCA often leads to hallucination, unstable reasoning, and poor alignment with structured network evidence. This work first reviews the evolution of telecom RCA from rule-based and machine learning (ML) approaches to emerging LLM-enabled techniques, and provides an overview of recent paradigms, including structured reasoning, retrieval-augmented knowledge grounding, agentic orchestration, and verifiable reasoning. Building upon these insights, we propose a structured reasoning framework for LLM-enabled telecom RCA that aligns diagnostic reasoning with telecom-specific evidence and domain knowledge. The proposed approach first organizes heterogeneous network telemetry into canonical contexts, and then enforces decision-path reasoning during diagnosis, and finally generates evidence-grounded explanations for reliable fault identification. Experimental results on two 5G RCA datasets, TeleLogs and TelecomTS, demonstrate that the proposed framework consistently improves diagnostic accuracy and decision consistency compared with baseline techniques. These cross-dataset results highlight the importance of structured reasoning design for practical LLM-based RCA systems in next-generation telecom networks.

</details>


### [57] [AI Contextual Measurement for Recovering Individual and Group-Level Effects: Validation Against Survey Measures and an Occupational Application](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02821)
*Wenxin Jiang,Xuyang Wang,Yuxiao Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AICOME framework validates AI-generated respondent-level measures for contextual models, testing if AI measures can recover group-level and individual effects, with validation showing success when rich data is available but limitations with restricted information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers increasingly use AI to measure social/organizational characteristics missing from surveys, but need evaluation frameworks to determine if AI-derived measures can properly capture contextual model effects beyond simple response prediction.

Method: Proposed AICOME framework evaluates AI-derived respondent-level measures by decomposing them into group-level aggregates and individual deviations to estimate both between-group and within-group associations. Validated using 2022 China Family Panel Studies (CFPS) with occupations as grouping structure and job-related survey variables as benchmarks, comparing survey vs AI measures across multiple validation types.

Result: AI contextual measurement recovers much contextual-model information from observed survey variables when rich respondent/job characteristics are available (strongest validation for weekly hours), but performance deteriorates with restricted information (only occupation/demographics) and when multiple related concepts are treated as simultaneously unobserved.

Conclusion: AICOME is most useful for recovering limited number of theoretically important constructs from rich existing datasets, providing a framework to evaluate whether AI measures can properly capture contextual model effects beyond simple prediction tasks.

Abstract: Researchers increasingly use artificial intelligence to construct measures of social, organizational, and occupational characteristics that are absent from conventional surveys. We propose AICOME, AI COntextual MEasurement, a framework for evaluating whether AI-derived respondent-level measures can recover individual and group-level effects in contextual models. The key idea is that an AI measure constructed at the respondent level can be used to derive its group-level aggregate and its individual deviation, allowing researchers to estimate both between-group and within-group associations rather than treating AI measurement as response prediction alone.
  We validate the framework using the 2022 China Family Panel Studies (CFPS), where occupations provide the empirical grouping structure and several job-related survey variables provide validation benchmarks. For computer use, foreign-language use, weekly hours, and management responsibilities, we compare survey measures with AI-derived measures in response-level, model-level, contextual, and boundary-condition validations. The results show that AI contextual measurement can recover much of the contextual-model information contained in observed survey variables when rich respondent and job characteristics are available. Weekly hours provides the strongest validation case, with AI-derived measures reproducing the large negative between- and within-occupation associations with satisfaction observed in CFPS. The framework also identifies clear boundary conditions: performance deteriorates when information is restricted to occupation and basic demographics, and recovery is weaker when several related concepts are treated as simultaneously unobserved. The findings suggest that AICOME is most useful for recovering a limited number of theoretically important constructs from rich existing datasets.

</details>


### [58] [Discriminative World Models for Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02885)
*Kelvin Li,Dhruv Pendharkar,Anish Pahilajani,Chuyi Shang,Leon Oks,Leonid Karlinsky,Rogerio Feris,Trevor Darrell,Roei Herzig*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new training objective called "predicted-state matching" for web agent world models improves action selection by ensuring predicted states are discriminative for ranking alternative actions, outperforming traditional supervised next-state prediction methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current web agent world models are trained via supervised next-state prediction to generate fixed representations (HTML or AXTree snapshots), but this objective is misaligned with downstream rankers that need predicted states to be discriminative across candidate actions for accurate scoring.

Method: Introduces predicted-state matching objective where predicted representations must distinguish true resulting states from those reached by alternative actions. Models are trained using a branching web-agent dataset derived from WebArena Go-Browse trajectories containing multiple alternative actions and their resulting states at each decision point.

Result: Outperforms world models trained with supervised next-state prediction on held-out predicted-state matching benchmark. Improves PRM-style action ranking on WebPRMBench compared to action-only PRMs and PRMs augmented with supervised-next-state world models. Enhances end-to-end task success on WebArena-Lite when used for test-time action selection.

Conclusion: Predicted-state matching provides better alignment between world model training and downstream ranking objectives, leading to improved web agent performance across multiple benchmarks by ensuring predicted states are discriminative for action selection.

Abstract: Recent web agents use world models for test-time action selection by sampling candidate actions, predicting the resulting web states, and ranking them with a ranker model or a Process Reward Model (PRM). These world models are typically trained via supervised next-state prediction to generate fixed representations like HTML or AXTree snapshots. However, this objective is misaligned with the downstream ranker, which relies on predicted states being discriminative across candidates to accurately score them. To address this, we introduce predicted-state matching, a training objective where the predicted representation must distinguish the true resulting state from those reached by alternative actions. We train these models using a branching web-agent dataset derived from WebArena Go-Browse trajectories, where every decision point contains multiple alternative actions and their resulting states. Experiments on our held-out predicted-state matching benchmark show that our approach outperforms world models trained with supervised next-state prediction. We further show that our approach improves PRM-style action ranking on WebPRMBench compared with action-only PRMs and PRMs augmented with supervised-next-state world models. Finally, on WebArena-Lite, using our world model for test-time action selection improves end-to-end task success. Our project page is available at: https://dhruvpendharkar.github.io/dwm/.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [59] [WMLLM: Self-Evolving Optimization Agents via Predict-Then-Act World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01608)
*Zhongzheng Li,Qingsong Ran,Shikun Feng,Nian Ran,Wenhao Li,Xiaoyuan Zhang,Yue Wang,Xiaoguang Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: WMLLM: A self-evolving optimization-agent framework using LLM-based world modeling for improved black-box optimization efficiency, achieving SOTA on multi-objective molecular optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Black-box optimization faces challenges with large, weakly structured, high-dimensional search spaces, leading to poor sample efficiency in existing methods that rely on direct candidate generation or trial-and-error refinement.

Method: Proposes WMLLM, a self-evolving optimization-agent framework using predict-then-act world modeling where agents first predict promising directions then generate candidates, combined with multi-turn refinement, population-based search, and reinforcement learning to refine both world model and optimization strategy.

Result: WMLLM improves sample efficiency and final optimization performance on black-box optimization tasks, achieving state-of-the-art results on multi-objective molecular optimization benchmark under limited evaluation budget.

Conclusion: Large language models can effectively predict optimization outcomes using implicit knowledge, and the WMLLM framework successfully leverages this through world modeling and self-evolving agentic architecture to solve challenging black-box optimization problems more efficiently.

Abstract: Black-box optimization problems remain challenging because of large, weakly structured, and high-dimensional search spaces. Existing methods often suffer from poor sample efficiency because they rely on direct candidate generation or trial-and-error refinement. A natural way to improve search efficiency is to use world modeling, which can help identify promising optimization directions before costly evaluation. Large language models can predict the outcomes of these candidates with nontrivial accuracy because of their implicit knowledge. Motivated by this observation, we propose WMLLM, a self-evolving optimization-agent framework based on predict-then-act world modeling. The agent first predicts promising directions and then acts to generate candidates. Combined with agentic multi-turn refinement, population-based search, and reinforcement learning, WMLLM refines both its implicit world model and its optimization strategy during search. Experiments on black-box optimization tasks, especially multi-objective molecular optimization, show that WMLLM improves sample efficiency and final optimization performance. On the multi-objective molecular optimization benchmark, WMLLM achieves state-of-the-art results under a limited evaluation budget.

</details>


### [60] [DiDrive: A Risk-Aware Hierarchical Diffusion Framework for Safe Offline Reinforcement Learning in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01609)
*Qisong Guo,Jingtang Chen,Zhilin Chen,Pei Xu,Mingjian Fu,Wenxi Liu,Yuanlong Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DiDrive: A distribution-guided offline diffusion framework for autonomous driving that combines risk-aware hierarchical diffusion with policy optimization to address distribution shift and OOD actions, achieving 85% success rate in complex traffic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models capture multimodal behavioral priors well, but offline RL policies still suffer from distribution shift, heavy-tailed risk signals, OOD action generation, and high-dimensional state redundancy in autonomous driving applications.

Method: DiDrive framework with two components: 1) Risk-Aware Hierarchical Diffusion (RHDif) uses low-level risk-gated encoder and high-level contextual modulator to filter environmental redundancy and focus on safety-critical threats, and 2) 3DICE policy optimization paradigm with in-sample calibrated guidance, spatiotemporal optimization, and ensemble-based candidate ranking to mitigate OOD overestimation and gradient oscillation.

Result: On CARLA benchmark, DiDrive outperforms baselines (IQL, CQL, Diffusion-QL), especially in complex high-density traffic with 60 vehicles, achieving 85% success rate and 4295.68 average reward.

Conclusion: DiDrive provides a robust pathway for safe autonomous driving decision-making by effectively addressing key challenges in offline RL for driving through distribution-guided diffusion and risk-aware optimization.

Abstract: While diffusion models effectively capture multimodal behavioral priors for autonomous driving, offline reinforcement learning (RL) policies remain susceptible to distribution shift, heavy-tailed risk signals, out-of-distribution (OOD) action generation, and high-dimensional state redundancy. To address these challenges, we propose DiDrive, a distribution-guided offline diffusion framework featuring two synergistic components: the Risk-Aware Hierarchical Diffusion (RHDif) architecture and the 3DICE policy optimization paradigm. In the state space, RHDif utilizes a low-level risk-gated encoder and a high-level contextual modulator to filter environmental redundancy and focus on safety-critical threats. In the action space, 3DICE mitigates OOD overestimation and gradient oscillation through in-sample calibrated guidance, spatiotemporal optimization, and ensemble-based candidate ranking. Evaluations on the CARLA benchmark demonstrate DiDrive's superiority over baselines like IQL, CQL, and Diffusion-QL, particularly in complex, high-density traffic scenarios with 60 vehicles, where it achieves an 85% success rate and a 4295.68 average reward, providing a robust pathway for safe autonomous driving decision-making.

</details>


### [61] [Prompt-Space Meta-Learning Does Not Transfer Across Users: A Frozen-LLM Negative Result](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01615)
*Liam Byrne,David Dylan,Orla Fitzgerald,Eoin Doyle,Ciara Nolan,Padraig Lynch,Sinead Gallagher*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Muse is a meta-learning approach for personalizing frozen LLMs to users via shared prompt evolution, but fails to achieve meaningful cross-user adaptation due to meta-objective collapse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to personalize frozen large language models to individual users using meta-learning in prompt space, where each user represents a task and the goal is to learn a shared natural-language adaptation policy that can configure the model for new users with minimal labeled examples.

Method: The method, called Muse (Meta-learned User-adaptation via Shared Evolution), evolves a single shared adaptation prompt over a meta-train user population using reflective prompt evolution. The prompt is then frozen and applied zero-shot to held-out users. The approach includes carefully matched controls to isolate learning from confounds like phrasing and selection.

Result: On two standard personalization benchmarks (LaMP-2 categorization and LaMP-3 rating) with 200 held-out users each, Muse does not significantly improve over its own un-evolved seed prompt or structure-broken controls that meta-train on mismatched user-support pairs. It performs worse than plain few-shot retrieval on the rating task (Delta MAE +0.175, p < 0.001).

Conclusion: The poor performance is attributed to meta-objective collapse: the meta-validation objective is statistically invariant to whether user-support correspondences are genuine, preventing optimization of transferable adaptation. Instead, it rewards instruction polish and validation overfitting. The proposed controls (seed-prompt, wrong-support, and invariance-oracle) form a reusable protocol for separating learned adaptation from confounds.

Abstract: Personalizing a frozen large language model (LLM) to individual users is often framed as a meta-learning problem in prompt space: each user is a task, and one seeks a shared natural-language adaptation policy that, given a handful of the user's labeled interactions, configures the frozen model for that user. The framing is attractive because it is backbone-agnostic and reuses the machinery of prompt optimization, yet the field rarely tests whether the optimized meta-objective encodes transferable cross-user adaptation rather than generic instruction quality. We study this question with Muse (Meta-learned User-adaptation via Shared Evolution), which evolves a single shared adaptation prompt over a meta-train user population by reflective prompt evolution, freezes it, and applies it zero-shot to held-out users; matched controls isolate learning from confounds of phrasing and selection. On two standard personalization benchmarks (LaMP-2 categorization and LaMP-3 rating) over 200 held-out users each, Muse does not significantly improve on its own un-evolved seed prompt or on a structure-broken control that meta-trains on mismatched user-support pairs, and is dominated by plain few-shot retrieval on the rating task (Delta MAE +0.175, p < 0.001). We attribute these outcomes to a single mechanism, meta-objective collapse: the meta-validation objective is statistically invariant to whether the user-support correspondence is genuine (p=0.555 on LaMP-2, p=0.622 on LaMP-3), so it cannot be optimized into transferable adaptation and instead rewards instruction polish and validation overfitting. The seed-prompt, wrong-support, and invariance-oracle controls form a reusable protocol that separates learned adaptation from these confounds.

</details>


### [62] [Efficient Context-Limited Telescope Bibliography Classification for the WASP-2025 Shared Task Using SciBERT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01647)
*Madhusudhana Naidu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SciBERT-based approach achieves state-of-the-art performance (macro F1 0.89) for automatic telescope paper classification despite token constraints, demonstrating efficient scientific text curation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual creation of telescope bibliographies is resource-intensive but crucial for assessing observatory impact and ensuring reproducibility in astronomy, highlighting the need for automated solutions.

Method: Developed a SciBERT-based approach for classifying papers into four categories (science, instrumentation, mention, not telescope) while handling strict 512-token context limits and limited computational resources.

Result: Achieved macro F1 score of 0.89, ranking top on WASP-2025 leaderboard; analysis showed SciBERT's domain alignment enables robust classification even with half samples exceeding token limit through effective truncation.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates efficient scientific text curation and provides insights into the trade-offs between truncation, chunking, and long-context models for astronomy bibliography automation.

Abstract: The creation of telescope bibliographies is a crucial part of assessing the scientific impact of observatories and ensuring reproducibility in astronomy. This task involves identifying, categorizing, and linking scientific publications that reference or use specific telescopes. However, this process remains largely manual and resource intensive. In this work, we present an efficient SciBERT-based approach for automatic classification of scientific papers into four categories - science, instrumentation, mention, and not telescope. Despite strict context-length constraints (maximum 512 tokens) and limited compute resources, our approach achieved a macro F1 score of 0.89, ranking at the top of the WASP-2025 leaderboard. We analyze the effect of truncation and show that even with half the samples exceeding the token limit, SciBERT's domain alignment enables robust classification. We discuss trade-offs between truncation, chunking, and long-context models, providing insights into the efficiency frontier for scientific text curation.

</details>


### [63] [CliffRank: A Dual-Branch Framework for Activity-Cliff Ranking Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01673)
*Kewei Li,Rongying Zhang,Peiyu Yang,Zhongjian Wang,Qiuchen Zhao,Lan Huang,Fengfeng Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CliffRank combines absolute-activity regression with ranking-consistency learning using pairwise preference consistency to improve activity-cliff ranking, achieving state-of-the-art performance on antimicrobial peptide and small-molecule datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Activity-cliff ranking is challenging due to local structural changes causing large activity differences and limited high-quality data that resolve underlying mechanisms, necessitating more effective use of available activity labels.

Method: CliffRank trains two parallel predictors using mean squared error, thresholded listwise loss, and Pairwise Preference Consistency (PPC) that aligns relative ordering in preference-probability space, with variations in PPC activation timing and architecture combinations (ESM2-t12 for peptides, PNA for small molecules).

Result: On antimicrobial peptide datasets, CliffRank with ESM2-t12 achieved highest mean Spearman correlation (0.5393) and mean Recall@50 (21.4). On small-molecule datasets, CliffRank with PNA achieved highest mean Spearman correlation (0.6890) with mean Recall@50 (30.4) matching ACANet-PNA. PPC results define practical limits, with asymmetric initialization and delayed PPC showing mixed benefits.

Conclusion: Future work should evaluate more targets and antimicrobial peptide systems, develop adaptive PPC schedules, and incorporate protein or membrane context when available.

Abstract: Activity-cliff ranking remains difficult because local structural changes can cause large activity differences, while high-quality data that resolve the underlying mechanisms remain limited. To use available activity labels more effectively, we combine absolute-activity regression with ranking-consistency learning. CliffRank trains two parallel predictors with mean squared error, a thresholded listwise loss, and Pairwise Preference Consistency (PPC), which aligns relative ordering in the preference-probability space. On three antimicrobial peptide datasets, CliffRank with ESM2-t12 achieved the highest mean Spearman correlation of 0.5393 and mean Recall@50 of 21.4, although the leading method varied across individual datasets. On three small-molecule datasets, CliffRank with PNA, where PPC was activated after 120 epochs, achieved the highest mean Spearman correlation of 0.6890, while its mean Recall@50 of 30.4 matched that of ACANet-PNA. The PPC results also define its practical limits. Asymmetric initialization improved the MolCLR-GIN averages but did not improve every target. For PNA without pretrained weights, delayed PPC improved selected metrics, but no schedule was best for both mean Spearman correlation and mean Recall@50. Future work should evaluate more targets and antimicrobial peptide systems, develop adaptive PPC schedules, and incorporate protein or membrane context when available.

</details>


### [64] [Sim2Signal: Sim-to-Real Benchmarks for Traffic Signal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01676)
*Ferdous Al Rafi,Susrik Mukherjee,Latika Liladhar Dekate,Jennifer Yawa Lavoe,Huaiyuan Yao,Shlok Mohanty,Longchao Da,Xuesong Zhou,Hua Wei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sim2Signal is a benchmark that systematically decomposes and measures the Sim-to-Real gap in traffic signal control RL, evaluating 18 mitigation methods across 33 gap settings in 10 real-world networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL policies for traffic signal control fail when deployed from simulation to reality (Sim-to-Real gap). Current research lacks understanding of relative impacts of different gap sources, reliability of mitigation methods, and standardized benchmarks for systematic evaluation.

Method: Developed Sim2Signal benchmark that decomposes Sim-to-Real gap into observation, action, transition, and reward gaps (corresponding to MDP components). Induces each gap in isolation under shared protocol. Evaluated 18 mitigation methods on 2 base controllers across 33 gap settings in 10 calibrated networks from 5 real-world locations.

Result: Direct transfer consistently degrades performance across all gap sources. Severity of degradation doesn't predict mitigation effectiveness. Mitigation effectiveness depends strongly on network and gap setting: methods that help in one case may fail in another. Most effective methods estimate gap changes rather than making policies insensitive through domain randomization or invariant representations.

Conclusion: Sim2Signal provides a standardized benchmark for systematic evaluation of Sim-to-Real gaps in traffic signal control. Mitigation effectiveness is context-dependent, suggesting need for adaptive approaches rather than one-size-fits-all solutions. Methods that actively estimate and compensate for gap changes outperform those that try to make policies gap-insensitive.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning achieves strong traffic signal control performance in simulation, yet policies trained in simulators often fail once deployed in the real world, a failure known as the Sim-to-Real gap. When RL is applied to traffic signal control, this gap arises from several sources: sensing, action execution, traffic dynamics, and the control objective. Their relative impact and the reliability of existing Sim-to-Real mitigation methods remain insufficiently understood, and the field lacks a standard benchmark for systematically measuring the gap and evaluating mitigation methods. We present Sim2Signal, a benchmark that decomposes the Sim-to-Real gap into observation, action, transition, and reward gaps, corresponding to mismatches in the four components of the underlying MDP, and induces each gap in isolation under a shared protocol. We evaluate 18 mitigation methods on 2 base controllers, across 33 gap settings and 10 calibrated networks built from 5 real-world locations. We find that direct transfer consistently degrades performance across all four gap sources, but the severity of the degradation does not predict the effectiveness of mitigation. Instead, mitigation effectiveness depends strongly on the network and gap setting: outside the action gap, a method that helps in one case may fail in another. The most effective methods generally estimate what the gap changes, rather than make the policy insensitive through domain randomization or invariant representations. Our code is available at https://github.com/Red-Pheonix/Sim2RealTSCBenchMark

</details>


### [65] [A Survey on Self-Improving Test-Time Intelligence: Feedback-Driven Adapting, Learning, and Scaling at Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01679)
*Shuaicheng Niu,Guohao Chen,Yaofo Chen,Zhiquan Wen,Jinwu Hu,Zeshuai Deng,Deyu Chen,Shuhai Zhang,Renjie Chen,Zihao Lian,Shoukai Xu,Gang Dai,Yunbei Zhang,Wei Luo,Yifan Zhang,Mingkui Tan,Cheng Deng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Survey paper presenting Feedback-driven Test-Time Intelligence (TTI) as a unified framework for understanding AI systems that improve their behavior during deployment by using test-time information and additional computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems increasingly need to improve during deployment, but current approaches to test-time refinement have evolved along two separate directions (modifying model state vs. using extra inference resources) in different communities with different terminology, making connections hard to see.

Method: The paper proposes a unified perspective called feedback-driven Test-Time Intelligence (TTI) that connects test-time adaptation, test-time learning, and test-time scaling, highlighting both their distinctions and growing overlap in hybrid systems.

Result: Creates a coherent conceptual framework that helps connect previously fragmented ideas and provides clearer foundation for studying inference-time self-improvement, with review of major methodological paradigms, applications across multiple domains, and open challenges.

Conclusion: The unified TTI framework provides a coherent foundation and research roadmap for the study of self-improving AI systems at test time, connecting previously isolated approaches and offering clearer conceptual understanding of deployment-time improvement.

Abstract: The ability of AI systems to improve their behavior during deployment is becoming increasingly important. As inference moves beyond the static execution of a fixed trained model, a growing body of work studies how models can refine their behavior on the fly by exploiting test-time information and additional computation. These developments have largely evolved along two directions: methods that modify the model's state using test-time signals, and methods that improve predictions through extra inference-time resources such as more sampling and tool use. However, these directions are often studied in separate communities with different terminology, making their connections harder to see. In this survey, we present feedback-driven Test-Time Intelligence (TTI) as a unified perspective for understanding such deployment-time improvement. We use this view to relate test-time adaptation, test-time learning, and test-time scaling, highlighting both their distinctions and their growing overlap in hybrid systems. This unified framework helps connect previously fragmented ideas and provides a clearer conceptual foundation for studying inference-time self-improvement. We review major methodological paradigms, representative applications, and open challenges across vision, language, multimodal learning, generative models, robotics, and healthcare. Our goal is to provide a coherent foundation and research roadmap for the study of self-improving AI systems at test time.

</details>


### [66] [Reinforcement Learning and Rule-Based Peer-to-Peer Pricing in Residential PV-BES Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01680)
*Pablo Benalcazar,Maciej Kalka,Wilian Guamán,Jacek Kamiński*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Rule-based pricing mechanisms outperform reinforcement learning approaches in P2P electricity trading for PV communities, but adding battery storage improves RL performance substantially.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to compare rule-based and learning-based pricing mechanisms for peer-to-peer electricity trading in residential photovoltaic communities, understanding which approach yields better community savings and operational outcomes.

Method: The study compares rule-based benchmarks (bill-sharing, mid-market rate, supply-demand-ratio pricing) against reinforcement learning approaches using Deep Q-Network with multiplier-based and SDR-shaped pricing. Performance is evaluated using community savings and other financial/operational indicators across PV-only and PV+battery configurations.

Result: In PV-only configurations, rule-based benchmarks outperformed the best RL policy. With battery storage added, the best RL policy improved community savings from €734.23 to €978.52. SDR-shaped pricing consistently outperformed multiplier-based parameterization across learning modes. Rule-based pricing remained highly competitive when directly compared.

Conclusion: Rule-based pricing mechanisms are still highly effective compared to learning-based approaches in P2P electricity trading, though battery storage significantly enhances RL performance. The distribution of benefits across households remains heterogeneous, and SDR-shaped pricing is superior to multiplier-based approaches within learning-based methods.

Abstract: This paper compares rule-based and learning-based pricing mechanisms for peer-to-peer (P2P) electricity trading in residential photovoltaic communities. The rule-based benchmarks comprise bill-sharing as an ex post allocation mechanism, the mid-market rate, and supply-demand-ratio pricing. The reinforcement-learning (RL) formulation is implemented through a Deep Q-Network and evaluated under multiplier-based and learnable SDR-shaped pricing, with a fixed-parameter SDR variant as a non-learning control. Performance is assessed through community savings together with complementary financial and operational indicators. In the base PV-only configuration, the rule-based benchmarks outperform the best RL policy. With battery energy storage, evaluated for the RL policies only, community savings under the best RL policy increase from EUR 734.23 to EUR 978.52. Across the learning-based modes and in both configurations, SDR-shaped pricing outperforms the multiplier-based parameterization considered. The results indicate that rule-based pricing remains highly competitive wherever the two families are compared directly, and that storage substantially improves the learning-based outcomes under this accounting, while the distribution of benefits remains heterogeneous across households.

</details>


### [67] [Post-Training Language Models for Gold-Medal Performance in Coding Competitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02849)
*Aleksander Ficek,Sean Narenthiran,Mehrzad Samadi,Somshubra Majumdar,Boris Ginsburg*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A specialized AI system achieves superhuman performance on the International Olympiad in Informatics (IOI) through a combination of supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and a test-time refinement strategy called GenCorrect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Competitive programming competitions like IOI and ICPC represent the most challenging test settings for large language model reasoning capabilities, serving as a benchmark for advanced AI reasoning.

Method: The authors developed an end-to-end specialization pipeline featuring: 1) Large-scale problem curation (22,000 problems), 2) Synthetic reasoning traces generation, 3) Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) for both 30B and 550B models, 4) Reinforcement learning (RL) for the 30B model, and 5) GenCorrect - a feedback-driven test-time compute strategy that iteratively generates, evaluates, and refines diverse solutions.

Result: On IOI 2025: Nano-CC improved from 130 points to 291 after post-training, then to 468 with GenCorrect (exceeding gold threshold of 438.3). Ultra-CC reached 502 points. For IOI 2026 prospective evaluation: The competition-specific Ultra-CC system scored 535.4/600 under the same constraints as human contestants, exceeding both the gold threshold (361.12) and top human score (498.27).

Conclusion: This represents the first AI system to outscore the highest-scoring human contestant on an IOI problem set, demonstrating superhuman performance in competitive programming through specialized training and test-time refinement techniques.

Abstract: Competitive programming has become a key test of large language model reasoning, with international competitions such as IOI and ICPC representing its most challenging settings. We present an end-to-end specialization pipeline combining large-scale problem curation, synthetic reasoning traces, supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and reinforcement learning (RL). Using 22,000 curated problems, we train Nemotron-3-Nano-CC (30B-A3B) with SFT and RL and Nemotron-3-Ultra-CC (550B-A55B) with SFT alone. We further introduce GenCorrect, a feedback-driven test-time compute strategy that iteratively generates, evaluates, and refines diverse solutions. On IOI 2025, Nano-CC improves from 130 points to 291 after post-training and to 468 with GenCorrect, exceeding the gold threshold of 438.3 while Ultra-CC reaches 502. Guided by these results, we develop a competition-specific Ultra-CC system and evaluate it prospectively during IOI 2026. Under the same time, internet-access, and submission constraints as human contestants, it scores 535.4 out of 600, exceeding both the gold threshold of 361.12 and the top human score of 498.27. To our knowledge, this is the first AI system to outscore the highest-scoring human contestant on an IOI problem set.

</details>


### [68] [Median-of-Means as an Extremal Convex Estimator and a Nonconvex Route to the Trimmed Oracle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01689)
*Angshul Majumdar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces nonconvex block-Lp estimators that achieve near-oracle robustness for learning with heavy-tailed and adversarially corrupted data, improving upon classical convex methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Median-of-means estimation faces limitations in robust learning with heavy-tailed and adversarially corrupted data. Convex estimators cannot achieve the optimal trimmed-block oracle constant, creating a gap between practical methods and theoretical performance limits.

Method: Develops a family of block-Lp estimators (for 0<p<1) that are nonconvex. Analyzes their deterministic robustness bounds, establishes that global minimizers approach the oracle constant as p decreases, proves benign optimization landscape (no bad local minima), and combines with block-level concentration techniques.

Result: Convex block M-estimators have worst-case robustness constant ≥1/(1-2ε), matching median-of-means but falling short of oracle constant 1/(1-ε). Nonconvex block-Lp estimators approach the oracle constant as p→0, with global minimizers coinciding with oracle under mild conditions. Achieves sub-Gaussian deviation bounds under finite (2+δ) moments, with extensions to high-dimensional robust mean estimation and sparse regression.

Conclusion: Nonconvex block-Lp estimators bridge the gap between convex methods and oracle performance, providing theoretically optimal robustness for learning with heavy-tailed and corrupted data while maintaining tractable optimization landscapes.

Abstract: We revisit median-of-means estimation from a deterministic optimization viewpoint and develop a family of block-Lp estimators for robust learning with heavy-tailed and adversarially corrupted data. In a block contamination model with at least a fraction 1 minus epsilon of good blocks, we first show that every convex block M-estimator has worst-case robustness constant at least 1 divided by 1 minus 2 epsilon. This matches the classical median-of-means bound and proves that the trimmed-block oracle constant 1 divided by 1 minus epsilon cannot be attained within the convex class. We then introduce a nonconvex block-Lp family for p between 0 and 1 and derive finite-sample deterministic robustness bounds for all global minimizers. As p decreases from 1 toward 0, these bounds continuously approach the trimmed-block oracle constant. For sufficiently small p, the global minimizers coincide with those of the oracle under a mild separation condition. We also show that the block-Lp objectives have a benign landscape, with all local minima remaining close to the truth and no bad basins. Combining these results with block-level concentration yields sub-Gaussian deviation bounds under finite 2 plus delta moments and high-dimensional extensions to robust mean estimation and sparse regression.

</details>


### [69] [Tri-Band Channel Measurement-Enabled Multi-Layer Digital Twin for Terahertz Wireless Data Centers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01699)
*Mingjie Zhu,Ziming Yu,Guangjian Wang,Chong Han*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a measurement-driven digital twin framework for THz wireless data centers with physical, channel, evaluation, and manipulation layers for efficient planning and optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid growth of AI computing demands flexible, high-capacity data-center interconnections. THz communication offers ultra-wide bandwidth and high spatial reuse, while digital twins enable efficient wireless planning and real-time optimization for future wireless data centers.

Method: Proposed a multi-layer DT framework: 1) Extensive channel measurements at 140, 220, and 300 GHz; 2) Measurement-calibrated physical twin with optimized geometry, material, antenna, and hybrid propagation models; 3) LoS-aware implicit neural field for AI channel twin; 4) System-level evaluation layer for coverage and interference analysis.

Result: AI twin achieves lower power reconstruction error than existing neural-field baselines with real-time inference capability. Ceiling-mounted AP deployment achieves over 90% coverage under 10 dB SINR threshold for both AP-to-rack and rack-to-rack communications.

Conclusion: The proposed DT framework effectively enables THz wireless data-center planning and optimization, demonstrating practical feasibility through measurement-driven calibration and AI-powered channel reconstruction.

Abstract: The rapid growth of AI computing has driven increasing demands for flexible and high-capacity data-center interconnections. Owing to its ultra-wide bandwidth and high spatial reuse capability, terahertz (THz) communication has emerged as a promising solution for future wireless data centers, while digital twins (DTs) enable efficient wireless planning and real-time optimization. In this work, a measurement-driven multi-layer DT framework is proposed for THz wireless data centers, where the physical, channel, evaluation, and manipulation layers are progressively constructed from bottom to top. First, extensive channel measurements are conducted at 140, 220, and 300 GHz to characterize frequency-dependent propagation behaviors. Based on the tri-band measurements, a measurement-calibrated physical twin is established by jointly optimizing the geometry, material, antenna, and hybrid propagation models. On top of the physical twin, a line-of-sight (LoS)-aware implicit neural field is developed to construct an AI channel twin for efficient channel reconstruction. The proposed AI twin learns location-dependent channel statistics from the calibrated twin, enabling real-time prediction of received power and LoS probability. Building upon the reconstructed channel field, a system-level evaluation layer is derived to analyze coverage and interference for both AP-to-rack and rack-to-rack communications. Experimental results show that the proposed AI twin achieves lower power reconstruction error than existing neural-field baselines while maintaining real-time inference capability. Moreover, the ceiling-mounted AP deployment achieves over 90% coverage under a 10 dB signal-to-interference-plus-noise ratio (SINR) threshold, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed DT framework for THz wireless data-center planning and optimization.

</details>


### [70] [Generative Diffusion Surrogates with Analytical Variance Schedule](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01705)
*Patrick Reichherzer,Gianluca Gregori,David N. Hosking,Subir Sarkar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Generative diffusion models are adapted to simulate stochastic transport processes by using known macroscopic variance scaling as the noise schedule, enabling calibrated emulation of non-Gaussian particle distributions without heuristic tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative diffusion models for stochastic transport applications use heuristic noise schedules that lack physical meaning. Image and audio generation have no inherent physical time scale, but transport systems often have known macroscopic variance scaling from theory or experiments even when full distributions are unknown.

Method: The authors prescribe the forward noising rate in diffusion models as the time derivative of the known variance scaling from transport theory. This turns generative time into a calibrated transport clock. The method learns the score field representing how non-Gaussian structure from initial data is smoothed along this physically-calibrated variance path, requiring no intermediate-time physical transport data.

Result: For ballistic-to-diffusive transport in turbulent plasmas, the surrogate model matches test-particle distributions, reproduces laboratory-measured variance scaling, and tracks simulated kurtosis evolution without schedule tuning. The approach enables calibrated emulation and likelihood-based inference for stochastic transport systems.

Conclusion: By linking diffusion model noise schedules to known macroscopic variance scaling from transport theory, the method creates physically-calibrated generative models that accurately simulate stochastic transport processes and capture non-Gaussian distributional structure, overcoming limitations of heuristic schedule selection.

Abstract: Stochastic transport describes physical systems in which an initially structured distribution spreads under unresolved forcing, scattering, or heterogeneous media. Useful surrogates for such systems should be probabilistic, time-resolved, and able to represent non-Gaussian distributional structure. Generative diffusion models, which corrupt data with Gaussian noise and learn a reverse flow back to structured states, have these properties. Their noise schedules, however, are usually chosen heuristically: image and audio generation---the canonical use cases---provide no physical clock. In transport, by contrast, the variance, or mean-square displacement, is often known from macroscopic theory or empirical scaling even when the full distribution is not. Here we prescribe the forward noising rate as the time derivative of this variance, turning generative time into a calibrated transport clock. The variance path is enforced by construction, while the learned score field represents how non-Gaussian structure inherited from entrance data is smoothed along that path, requiring no intermediate-time physical transport data. For ballistic-to-diffusive transport in turbulent plasmas, the surrogate matches test-particle distributions, reproduces the laboratory-measured variance scale, and tracks the simulated kurtosis evolution without schedule tuning, enabling calibrated emulation and likelihood-based inference.

</details>


### [71] [RecKAN: Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks with a Learnable Recursive Polynomial Basis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01729)
*Amirhosein Azarpour*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RecKAN improves on traditional KANs by learning polynomial basis functions through a second-order recurrence relation rather than using fixed classical bases, achieving better performance across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) still rely on fixed basis functions (B-splines, Chebyshev polynomials, etc.) with only combination weights learned. The authors aim to move beyond this limitation by learning the basis functions themselves through a recurrence relation.

Method: RecKAN defines basis functions using a second-order polynomial recurrence: R_{n+1}(x) = (ax^2+bx+c)R_n(x) + (dx+e)R_{n-1}(x), where the five coefficients (a,b,c,d,e) are learned jointly with the network. This recurrence can recover classical polynomial families as special cases.

Result: RecKAN outperforms parameter-matched KAN baselines (Chebyshev, Jacobi, and spline) on all classification tasks across image, text, biomedical time series, and achieves lowest MSE on ETTh1 forecasting. It also beats standard MLP heads when used as classifier heads. The learned recurrence coefficients are interpretable and adapt to task requirements.

Conclusion: Learning basis functions through recurrence relations provides a more flexible and powerful approach than fixed classical bases for KANs, enabling better performance across diverse tasks while maintaining interpretability of the learned mathematical structure.

Abstract: Kolmogorov--Arnold Networks (KANs) replace the fixed scalar weights of a standard network with learnable univariate functions on each edge, but existing variants still fix the \emph{basis} that those functions are built from: B-splines, Chebyshev polynomials, wavelets, or Jacobi polynomials, and learn only the combination weights over it. We introduce RecKAN, which instead defines the basis itself by a second order polynomial recurrence, $R_{n+1}(x) = (ax^2+bx+c)R_n(x) + (dx+e)R_{n-1}(x)$, whose five coefficients are learned jointly with the network. We show this recurrence recovers several classical polynomial families including both kinds of Chebyshev polynomials, Fibonacci, Pell, and Jacobsthal polynomials as special cases, and prove that its degree grows linearly in $n$ exactly on the sub-family containing all of them, giving a concrete sense in which the learned basis can move beyond any fixed classical choice. Across multiple benchmark datasets spanning image, text, biomedical time series classification, and time series forecasting, RecKAN outperforms three parameter-matched KAN baselines (Chebyshev, Jacobi, and spline based) on all classification tasks and achieves the lowest MSE on the ETTh1 forecasting benchmark. Additionally, when used as a classifier head with a convolutional backbone, RecKAN achieves higher accuracy than standard MLP heads on Fashion MNIST, CIFAR-10, and SVHN. On a synthetic function fitting benchmark it tracks a sharply oscillatory target that a parameter comparable MLP under fits. We further show that the learned recurrence coefficients are interpretable: on the task requiring the most local structure, training moves the basis away from the linear degree growth regime that contains every classical family we identify, consistent with our theoretical analysis of what that structural shift enables.

</details>


### [72] [CAT-Flow: Curvature-Adaptive sTeps for Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01746)
*Qinchan Li,Pedro Cisneros-Velarde,Keru Fu,Samuel Antunes Miranda,Sharan Vaswani,Hao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAT-OV and CAT-OT: Training-free step-size adaptation algorithms for Flow Matching that reduce sampling steps by up to 40% by leveraging curvature information from vector fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow Matching has become a leading generative modeling framework but suffers from efficiency bottlenecks due to its iterative ODE-based sampling. Current models require 20-30 steps for good quality, and sample quality is highly sensitive to step-size choices, creating a fundamental efficiency limitation.

Method: Two lightweight, training-free algorithms: CAT-OT (estimates curvature over time via finite-difference approximation of vector field's time-derivative) and CAT-OV (approximates curvature over state space via gradient of vector field). Both adapt step-sizes at inference time based on novel connection between Flow Matching sampling and gradient flow, requiring no additional neural function evaluations.

Result: Both methods have truncation error bounds of constant order under suitable conditions. Empirically, CAT-OV and CAT-OT outperform existing step-size heuristics across four text-to-image Flow Matching models, reducing the number of generation steps required for comparable quality by up to 40% while maintaining computational efficiency.

Conclusion: The proposed CAT-OV and CAT-OT algorithms provide effective, training-free solutions to the step-size adaptation problem in Flow Matching, significantly improving sampling efficiency without compromising quality, making Flow Matching models more practical for real-world applications.

Abstract: Flow Matching has emerged as a leading framework for generative modeling, powering state-of-the-art systems such as FLUX and Stable Diffusion 3.5. However, the iterative nature of its ODE-based sampling process creates a fundamental efficiency bottleneck: the quality of generated samples is highly sensitive to the choice of step-sizes, and current models typically require 20 to 30 steps for good quality. In this work, we propose two lightweight, training-free algorithms, CAT-OV and CAT-OT that adapt step-sizes at inference time based on a novel connection between Flow Matching sampling and gradient flow. Our algorithms are computed efficiently by not requiring additional neural function evaluations. Specifically, CAT-OT estimates curvature over time via a finite-difference approximation of the time-derivative of the vector field, while CAT-OV approximates curvature over the state space via a gradient of the vector field. Under suitable conditions, both methods have truncation error bounds of constant order. Empirically, CAT-OV and CAT-OT outperform existing step-size heuristics in image quality metrics across four text- to-image Flow Matching models, reducing the number of generation steps required to reach comparable quality by up to 40%.

</details>


### [73] [A Study of Conditional Diffusion Models for Open-Loop Control under Dry Friction and Stiction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01756)
*Eric Aislan Antonelo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Action Diffusion uses a diffusion model as a generative prior for open-loop control in point-mass systems with dry friction, outperforming traditional methods by generating temporally coherent control sequences that overcome stiction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the challenge of generating effective control sequences for systems with dry friction and stiction, where motion only occurs when applied input exceeds static-friction thresholds. This creates a small, temporally structured subset of the action-space that traditional methods struggle to sample effectively.

Method: The authors propose Action Diffusion, a conditional diffusion model formulation for generating action sequences. They use a compact conditional 1D U-Net that generates bounded control sequences conditioned on initial and target states, serving as an open-loop proposal distribution.

Result: Action Diffusion outperforms uniform random shooting, random shooting from structured dataset prior, and Cross-Entropy Method (CEM), especially in low-sample regimes. It reduces terminal error and stuck steps, indicating better ability to overcome stiction.

Conclusion: Conditional diffusion models provide an effective mechanism for generating temporally coherent control sequences that can overcome stiction by conditioning on and recombining structured control primitives from training data, making them promising for state-to-state open-loop control tasks.

Abstract: Diffusion models have recently emerged as expressive generative priors for planning and control. This paper studies Action Diffusion, an action-sequence diffusion formulation used as an open-loop proposal distribution for a point-mass system with dry friction and stiction. In this benchmark, motion starts only when the applied input exceeds a static-friction threshold, so effective controls occupy a small and temporally structured subset of the action-sequence space. A compact conditional 1D U-Net generates bounded control sequences conditioned on initial and target states. We compare it with uniform random shooting, random shooting from the same structured dataset prior, and the Cross-Entropy Method (CEM). Results show that Action Diffusion reduces terminal error and stuck steps, especially in low-sample regimes. These results indicate that conditional diffusion provides an effective mechanism for generating temporally coherent control sequences that overcome stiction by conditioning and recombining structured control primitives from the training prior for state-to-state open-loop control.

</details>


### [74] [Toward Explainable and Policy-Aware AI for Carbon Credit Price Prediction: A Research Framework for Emerging Carbon Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01765)
*Summaiya Unnisa Begum,Mohammed Nadeem Ullah,Mohammed Abdul Ghani Khan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A policy-aware neural network (EPA-CarbonNet) for carbon price prediction underperforms random walk on 5-day RMSE but has better directional accuracy; policy attention doesn't align with actual regulatory events.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Carbon prices are hard to forecast, existing work focuses on EU/Chinese schemes, uses simple sentiment scores, lacks calibration and explanation stability, and has ten identified research gaps.

Method: EPA-CarbonNet: six-layer architecture fusing market series with policy text via cross-attention, producing calibrated intervals and policy-attributed explanations; tested on 11 years of daily S&P carbon index data.

Result: Random walk beats model on 5-day RMSE (0.0365 vs 0.0475); SHAP agreement at rho=0.54; policy attention doesn't coincide with regulatory events; directional accuracy 58.6% (best among baselines).

Conclusion: Despite innovative architecture, model fails to outperform simple benchmarks and policy attention lacks interpretability; results highlight difficulty of carbon price prediction and need for better policy-text modeling.

Abstract: Carbon markets put a price on emissions, yet that price remains hard to forecast. Work in this area clusters on the EU and Chinese schemes, compresses regulatory text into a sentiment score, and reports accuracy without calibration or explanation stability. We distil ten recurring gaps into an impact-feasibility matrix and propose EPA-CarbonNet, a six-layer architecture that fuses market series with policy text by cross-attention and calibrated intervals alongside policy-attributed explanations. We then build and test it on eleven years of daily S and P carbon index data. The findings are largely negative, and reported as measured: a random walk beats the model on five-day RMSE (0.0365 against 0.0475), SHAP rankings agree at rho = 0.54 across resampled backgrounds, and policy attention never coincides with documented regulatory events. Directional accuracy, at 58.6 percent, leads every baseline. Code, data documentation and all result artifacts are available at https://github.com/Kimalice/Toward-Explainable-and-Policy-Aware-AI-for-Carbon-Credit-Price-Prediction

</details>


### [75] [Emergence of Fibrations, Compression, and Symmetry Breaking in Artificial Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01768)
*Osvaldo M Velarde,Lucas C Parra,Alireza Hashemi,Hernan A Makse*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep neural networks develop mathematical symmetries (fibrations/coverings) during learning, enabling dramatic compression and solving plasticity loss in continual learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Artificial neural networks are powerful but opaque "black boxes" that lack interpretability and suffer from issues like inefficient inference and loss of plasticity in continual learning.

Method: Theoretical analysis showing learning in deep neural networks generates local symmetries from graph theory (fibrations/coverings). Proved covering symmetries are stable attractors of stochastic gradient descent. Experimental validation across major architectures: multilayer, convolutional, recurrent, and transformer networks.

Result: Covering symmetries emerge universally across architectures. Exploiting symmetries enables drastic model compression (reducing to 17% of original size without performance loss).
