Controlled symmetry breaking overcomes loss of plasticity, achieving state-of-the-art performance in continual learning.

Conclusion: Symmetries provide a new foundation for AI systems that converts black boxes into interpretable colored graphs and enables more efficient inference and lifelong learning through systematic symmetry analysis and manipulation.

Abstract: Artificial neural networks are often regarded as powerful yet opaque black boxes. Here, we demonstrate that learning in deep neural networks generates local symmetries known in graph theory as fibrations and coverings. We prove that covering symmetries are stable attractors of stochastic gradient descent. Consistent with this theory, we report the emergence of covering symmetries across major network architectures, including multilayer, convolutional, recurrent, and transformer networks. Exploiting these symmetries enables drastic model compression - reducing networks to 17% of their original size without sacrificing performance. Furthermore, controlled breaking of covering symmetry overcomes the loss of plasticity, achieving state-of-the-art performance in continual learning. The theoretical results provide a new foundation for AI systems based on symmetries that convert black boxes into interpretable colored graphs and enable more efficient inference and lifelong learning.

</details>


### [76] [D-FROST: Decentralized Federated pRompt-tuning via Optimal tranSporT for Non-IID and Imbalanced Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01802)
*Quan Minh Nguyen,Hoang M. Ngo,Trong Nghia Hoang,My T. Thai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First study of prompt tuning in decentralized federated learning (DFL) proposes D-FROST, an optimal-transport-based algorithm that addresses prompt misalignment issues in decentralized settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompt tuning is parameter-efficient for adapting foundation models, making it suitable for DFL where exchanging full models is expensive. However, standard decentralized averaging fails because prompts learned from heterogeneous local data are not index-wise aligned, and there's a need for theoretical guarantees of consensus and convergence.

Method: Formulates decentralized prompt tuning as a Wasserstein-based optimization problem over prompt measures to capture set-valued prompt structure. Proposes D-FROST algorithm that merges neighborhood prompts into compact representative sets through transportation-based matching (optimal transport).

Result: Provides theoretical analysis: bounds Wasserstein consensus error across clients and establishes convergence of network-level prompt barycenter to a neighborhood of stationarity. Experimental results show effectiveness under heterogeneous client data.

Conclusion: D-FROST successfully addresses challenges of prompt tuning in decentralized federated learning through optimal transport techniques, providing theoretical guarantees and practical effectiveness for parameter-efficient adaptation in decentralized settings.

Abstract: Prompt tuning provides a parameter-efficient way to adapt foundation models (FMs) by freezing the pretrained backbone and updating only a small set of learnable prompts. This property makes prompt tuning especially suitable for decentralized federated learning (DFL), where exchanging full-model updates can be prohibitively expensive. However, prompt tuning in DFL introduces new challenges. Prompt sets learned from heterogeneous local data may not be index-wise aligned, making standard decentralized averaging unsuitable. In addition, the algorithm should be theoretically guaranteed to achieve consensus and make progress toward the shared objective. In this work, we provide the first study of prompt tuning in DFL. We formulate decentralized prompt tuning as a Wasserstein-based optimization problem over prompt measures, which captures the set-valued structure of prompts. We then propose D-FROST, an optimal-transport-based (OT-based) decentralized prompt-tuning algorithm that merges neighborhood prompts into compact representative prompt sets through transportation-based matching. We further analyze D-FROST by bounding the Wasserstein consensus error across clients, and establishing convergence of the network-level prompt barycenter to a neighborhood of stationarity. Experiments under heterogeneous client data demonstrate the effectiveness of D-FROST for decentralized prompt tuning.

</details>


### [77] [hLLM: Single Pass Decoding for Generative Reranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01807)
*Emil Laftchiev,Prachi Agrawal,Moe Kayali,Bixing Yan,Qi Xu,Zijie Lei,Chen Qiu,Zhi Hua,Ke Li,Luke Simon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: hLLM (Hungarian LLM) introduces an O(1) decoding method for LLM-based ranking that uses the Hungarian algorithm on precomputed item-position scores, achieving 64× speedup while maintaining ranking quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based ranking suffers from inefficient autoregressive decoding (one sequential forward pass per token) even though ranking only needs to output N ordinal values in a permutation-structured format.

Method: Proposes hLLM with specialized format decoding: 1) Read N×K item-position score matrix from LLM's prefill hidden states using lightweight self-attention, 2) Decode ordinals as optimal bipartite assignment via Hungarian algorithm, 3) Fine-tune with LoRA and teacher ranking distillation.

Result: Achieves 28 ms end-to-end inference (64× speedup) while maintaining ranking quality on par with teacher model. Complete ablation study shows contributions of architecture, training signal, and backbone adaptation.

Conclusion: Connects generative ranking to combinatorial optimization, opening path toward other O(1)-decode mechanisms for real-time ranking. The approach yields valid permutations by construction rather than repair.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) achieve state-of-the-art generative ranking quality, but the ranking they produce must be decoded, and autoregressive decoding spends one sequential forward pass per emitted token. We observe that the only tokens a ranker must emit are the $N$ ordinal values naming the items in ranked order, and that this narrow, permutation-structured output format admits decoding strategies which are much more efficient than left-to-right generation. We introduce hLLM (Hungarian LLM), a format-specialized decoding strategy that decodes all $N$ ordinals in $O(1)$ forward passes. hLLM reads an $N \times K$ item-position score matrix off the LLM's prefill hidden states with a lightweight self-attention head, then decodes the ordinals as the optimal bipartite assignment of that matrix via the Hungarian algorithm, yielding a valid permutation by construction rather than by repair. Through a systematic study of training signals and backbone adaptation, we show that LoRA-based fine-tuning combined with teacher ranking distillation reaches 28 ms end-to-end inference, a speed-up of $64\times$ while maintaining ranking quality on par with the teacher. We provide a complete ablation decomposing the contributions of architecture, training signal, and backbone adaptation. Our framework connects generative ranking to combinatorial optimization, opening a path toward other $O(1)$-decode mechanisms for real-time ranking.

</details>


### [78] [Import What You Need: Learning When and How to Augment EHR Graphs with External Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01839)
*Chen Chen,Mohsen Nayebi Kerdabadi,Dongjie Wang,Mei Liu,Zijun Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReTA is a reinforcement learning framework that dynamically augments knowledge graphs for EHR-based predictions, choosing per-visit actions to balance accuracy and computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Longitudinal prediction from EHRs is challenging due to sparse, irregular patient trajectories. Existing methods use fixed, context-agnostic KG augmentation that adds the same nodes/edges regardless of patient state, which is suboptimal.

Method: ReTA uses reinforcement learning for dynamic topology augmentation: 1) Builds offline refined pool of KG-grounded templates, 2) Learns per-visit policy choosing among three actions (Soft Import - enrich node features, Hard Import - graft KG subgraph, Skip - no augmentation), 3) Employs decoupled encoder with separate semantic/structural channels fused via adaptive gating for stable learning.

Result: Outperforms baselines on MIMIC-III/IV for diagnosis prediction, mortality, and readmission tasks. Shows efficient performance, transfers across datasets and KGs, yields interpretable augmentation patterns, and achieves robust gains under sparse supervision.

Conclusion: ReTA's dynamic knowledge import decision-making provides superior accuracy while controlling computational costs, offering a promising approach for knowledge-augmented EHR predictions that adapts to patient state.

Abstract: Longitudinal prediction from electronic health records (EHRs) is limited by the sparsity and irregularity in patient trajectories, and knowledge augmentation with external knowledge graphs (KGs) offers a promising way to alleviate these issues. However, most existing methods perform fixed, context-agnostic topology augmentation by adding the same KG nodes and edges regardless of a patient's evolving state. We propose ReTA, a Reinforcement learning-based dynamic Topology Augmentation framework that casts KG import as a per-visit, budget-aware policy. ReTA first constructs an offline refined pool of KG-grounded templates, then learns a policy to select one augment action per visit from three options: Soft Import, which enriches node features without modifying graph topology, Hard Import, which grafts a compact KG subgraph onto the visit graph to create message-passing shortcuts, and Skip, which leaves the visit unaugmented when the base encoder is already confident. To stabilize learning, ReTA employs a decoupled encoder that processes semantic and structural signals in separate channels and fuses them via adaptive gating. Experiments on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV across diagnosis prediction, mortality, and readmission show that ReTA consistently outperforms strong baselines while remaining efficient, transfers across datasets and knowledge graphs, and yields interpretable augmentation patterns. The robust gains under sparse supervision highlight the advantage of ReTA's dynamic decision to import knowledge, boosting accuracy while curbing costs.

</details>


### [79] [OutageDiT: A Generative Foundation Model for Power Outage Forecasting and Scenario Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01896)
*Yunqin Zhu,Feng Qiu,Yao Xie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OutageDiT is a foundation model that generates realistic 7-day power outage trajectories with quarter-hour resolution using deep generative modeling to address data scarcity for rare severe events.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Power outage planning requires scenario generation before events occur, but severe events are rare and regional data contains few examples of extreme outage and restoration patterns, making uncertainty representation challenging.

Method: Introduces OutageDiT, a foundation model trained on outage and weather records across the US, using a condition encoder to process historical context and future covariates once per forecast, and a shallow flow decoder to reuse horizon-aligned states to generate complete 7-day trajectories at quarter-hour resolution.

Result: OutageDiT improves forecast accuracy and scenario quality over strong baselines, supports zero-shot transfer to held-out regions, and enables point forecasting, uncertainty quantification, and conditional event simulation within a single deep generative model.

Conclusion: Conditional outage simulation serves as a bridge from outage forecasting to operational planning under uncertainty, positioning OutageDiT as a valuable tool for power grid resilience planning.

Abstract: Power-outage planning requires scenarios before an event occurs. These scenarios must represent uncertainty in magnitude, timing, and duration while preserving temporal dependence. However, severe events are rare, and data from any single region contain few examples of extreme outage and restoration patterns. To address this challenge, we introduce OutageDiT, a foundation model for generating seven-day outage trajectories at quarter-hour resolution, trained on outage and weather records across the United States. Specifically, a condition encoder processes the historical context and known future covariates once per forecast, and a shallow flow decoder reuses the resulting horizon-aligned states to generate complete trajectories. The resulting samples support point forecasting, uncertainty quantification, and conditional event simulation within one deep generative model. Across outage forecasting benchmarks, OutageDiT improves forecast accuracy and scenario quality over strong baselines and supports zero-shot transfer to held-out regions. Together, these results position conditional outage simulation as a bridge from outage forecasting to operational planning under uncertainty.

</details>


### [80] [CRISP: Cliff-awaRe Input-adaptive Sparse Prefilling with Structural-Mass-Motivated Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01925)
*Huu Huy Nguyen,Chien Van Nguyen,Franck Dernoncourt,Ryan A. Rossi,Linh Ngo Van,Jieyang Chen,Thien Huu Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CRISP is a new dynamic sparse attention method that addresses quadratic scaling in long-context LLM prefilling by using structural proxy routing and sink-aware thresholding to eliminate O(n) noise while preserving attention quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The attention prefilling phase in long-context LLM inference has quadratic scaling, making it a computational bottleneck. Existing sparse attention methods either use fixed patterns (lacking adaptability) or dynamic routing with indirect proxies (overlooking post-softmax mass hierarchy and accumulating background noise).

Method: CRISP introduces two key innovations: 1) C_struct - a structural proxy that directly measures mass at Vertical-Slash compatible positions, replacing JSD routing while eliminating pooled matmul and KL divergence overhead; 2) Sink-aware thresholding that navigates the post-softmax mass cliff by grounding thresholds in the noise floor, preventing O(n) background noise accumulation.

Result: CRISP achieves up to 5.30x attention speedup at 512k tokens and recovers up to +28.0 percentage points on retrieval tasks over baselines. It matches or exceeds exact dense attention on retrieval-heavy benchmarks across InfiniteBench, RULER, and LongBench on two model families.

Conclusion: CRISP effectively addresses structural challenges in dynamic sparse attention, delivering both computational efficiency and preserved accuracy for long-context LLM inference through its novel structural proxy routing and noise-aware thresholding approach.

Abstract: The attention prefilling phase of long-context LLM inference scales quadratically, making self-attention a severe computational bottleneck. Traditional sparse attention methods mitigate this through fixed patterns or offline profiling, but lack the flexibility to adapt to input-dependent attention structure. Recent dynamic methods address this by routing heads to sparse patterns in real-time, but rely on indirect routing proxies with overhead and budget allocation mechanisms that overlook the post-softmax mass hierarchy. We present CRISP (Cliff-awaRe Input-adaptive Sparse Prefilling), which identifies and addresses two structural challenges in this dynamic routing paradigm. First, we show that the routing decision can be read directly off the structure of the proxy attention map. We replace the Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) routing with C_struct, a structural proxy that measures mass at Vertical-Slash compatible positions and reproduces JSD's routing decisions while eliminating both the pooled matmul and subsequent KL divergence overhead. Second, we formalize the post-softmax mass cliff and demonstrate theoretically that strictly cumulative coverage thresholds accumulate O(n) background noise at long contexts. CRISP navigates this via a sink-aware threshold grounded in the noise floor. Empirically, across InfiniteBench, RULER and LongBench on two model families, CRISP is the strongest sparse method overall and matches or exceeds exact dense attention on retrieval-heavy benchmarks, recovering up to +28.0 pp on retrieval tasks over baselines and achieving up to a 5.30x attention speedup at 512k tokens, driven primarily by our O(n) noise elimination during selection while preserving structural integrity.

</details>


### [81] [OR-Transformer: Scaling Real-Time Decision-Making to 1,000 Items](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01933)
*Shuze Daniel Liu,David Simchi-Levi,Claire Chen,Chutong Gao,Shangtong Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OR-Transformer uses a permutation-equivariant Transformer architecture with pathwise-gradient training to solve large-scale joint replenishment problems with thousands of items, outperforming MILP and RL baselines while dramatically reducing decision time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern supply chains require coordinating thousands of heterogeneous items under correlated stochastic demand, heterogeneous lead times, and shared fixed costs, creating high-dimensional problems where traditional MILPs are too slow and standard RL struggles with credit assignment in such large action spaces.

Method: OR-Transformer is a deep RL framework with an item-permutation-equivariant Transformer architecture and pathwise-gradient training through inventory dynamics. This approach enables efficient learning in high-dimensional spaces while respecting the permutation symmetry of item ordering problems.

Result: The method outperforms learning-based and rolling-horizon MILP baselines across problem sizes up to 1,024 items, with performance gains increasing at larger scales. It reduces online decision time by over 4 million times compared to MILP solvers, enabling real-time operation.

Conclusion: OR-Transformer demonstrates that deep RL with appropriate architectural inductive biases (permutation equivariance) and training methods (pathwise gradients) can effectively solve large-scale joint replenishment problems, enabling real-time decision-making in supply chain operations that was previously infeasible with traditional optimization methods.

Abstract: Modern supply chain operations can require coordinating replenishment across thousands of heterogeneous items under correlated stochastic demand, heterogeneous lead times, and shared fixed ordering costs, yielding observation spaces exceeding $10^4$ dimensions. At this scale, rolling-horizon stochastic mixed-integer linear programs (MILPs) become prohibitively slow, while standard reinforcement learning (RL) methods face increasingly challenging credit assignment in high-dimensional action spaces. We introduce OR-Transformer, a deep reinforcement learning framework for joint replenishment under stochastic demand, with an item-permutation-equivariant Transformer architecture and pathwise-gradient training through the inventory dynamics. Across problem sizes up to 1,024 inventory items, OR-Transformer increasingly outperforms learning-based and rolling-horizon MILP baselines as scale grows. It also reduces online decision-making time by over 4 million times relative to MILP solvers, enabling real-time, large-scale deep RL in supply chain operations.

</details>


### [82] [Refining Heuristic-Based Bitcoin Address Clustering with Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01942)
*Hugo Schnoering,Roman Bresson,Michalis Vazirgiannis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a graph neural network-based method to refine heuristic Bitcoin address clustering by using contrastive embeddings and hierarchical clustering to identify suspicious merges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bitcoin's pseudonymous nature makes user-level analysis difficult as single users control multiple addresses. Existing heuristic-based clustering methods produce flat clusters with limited modularity and are prone to errors like merging different users together.

Method: Proposes refining heuristic-obtained clusters using contrastive embeddings from graph neural networks. Includes: releasing a Bitcoin transaction graph dataset, learning address embeddings consistent with heuristics (with theoretical backing), and applying hierarchical clustering for finer analysis.

Result: The method enables finer analysis of heuristic clusters and provides a quantitative criterion for flagging suspicious merges between different users' addresses.

Conclusion: The proposed approach improves upon traditional heuristic-based Bitcoin address clustering by leveraging graph neural networks to create more accurate and modular cluster assignments, addressing the limitations of existing methods.

Abstract: Bitcoin's pseudonymous nature makes it challenging to analyze user-level activity, since a single user may control multiple identifiers (addresses). Existing heuristic-based methods attempt to identify addresses belonging to the same user, but they often produce flat cluster assignments with limited modularity and are prone to errors such as merging different users together. In this work, we propose a method for refining heuristic-obtained clusters by grounding our clustering on contrastive embeddings yielded by graph neural networks. Our contributions are threefold: (i) we release a publicly available dataset of Bitcoin transaction graphs containing a substantial number of clusters; (ii) we propose a methodology for learning address embeddings consistent with heuristics, and back it up with theoretical guiding intuitions; (iii) through hierarchical clustering, we enable a finer analysis of heuristic clusters and provide a quantitative criterion for flagging suspicious merges.

</details>


### [83] [On-Policy Distillation Meets Off-Policy GRPO: Training Compact Instruction-Following Rerankers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01947)
*Vignesh Prabhakar,Jialing Pan,Anil Babu Ankisettipalli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A two-stage reinforcement learning framework for distilling compact rerankers where a small student model learns by exploring rankings and receiving teacher-derived rewards, outperforming traditional offline distillation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional distillation pipelines for compact instruction-following rerankers use offline imitation of teacher outputs on fixed examples, limiting supervision to the teacher's observed ranking space. The authors aim to overcome this limitation by applying reinforcement learning principles to reranker distillation.

Method: Two-stage framework: Stage 1 strengthens a 4B teacher reranker with off-policy GRPO using LLM-judge feedback on 88K instruction-following examples. Stage 2 trains a compact 1B student that samples rankings from its own policy and receives soft teacher-derived rewards on those rankings, enabling student exploration coupled with knowledge transfer.

Result: Significant improvements under distribution shift: On MAIR-11 (869 queries), achieves 0.7670 nDCG@6, outperforming offline listwise KD by +4.6 points. Outperforms offline pairwise RankNet KD and on-policy GKD. On MAIR-Full (126 tasks, 9,356 queries), reaches 0.6808 nDCG@6 and 0.7865 MRR@6. The 1B reranker achieves 0.7624 nDCG@6 on validation benchmark while maintaining favorable quality-efficiency tradeoff.

Conclusion: Reward-based on-policy distillation over student-sampled rankings significantly outperforms traditional offline distillation methods, especially under distribution shift. The approach enables better student exploration and knowledge transfer, creating more effective compact rerankers that can even surpass larger RL-trained models while maintaining efficiency advantages.

Abstract: Compact instruction-following rerankers are attractive for deployment, but conventional distillation pipelines typically train students by offline imitation of teacher outputs on a fixed set of examples, constraining supervision to the teacher's observed ranking space. We revisit reranker distillation through the lens of reinforcement learning.
  We propose a two-stage framework combining off-policy teacher optimization with on-policy student distillation. In Stage 1, a 4B teacher reranker is strengthened with off-policy GRPO using LLM-judge feedback on 88K instruction-following examples. In Stage 2, a compact 1B student samples rankings from its own policy and receives soft teacher-derived rewards on those rankings, coupling student exploration with knowledge transfer.
  Our strongest gains appear under distribution shift. On MAIR-11, the original 11-subset, 869-query evaluation, the proposed student reaches 0.7670 nDCG@6, outperforming offline listwise KD by +4.6 points. Controlled comparisons against offline pairwise RankNet KD and on-policy GKD show that neither changing the offline distillation objective nor moving teacher-distribution matching on-policy reproduces the performance of reward-based on-policy distillation over student-sampled rankings. The advantage persists on MAIR-Full: across all 126 tasks and 9,356 queries, the proposed method obtains the highest task-macro point estimates among the evaluated distillation variants, reaching 0.6808 nDCG@6 and 0.7865 MRR@6. It also exceeds two released 7B RL-trained rerankers on the comparable MAIR-11 evaluation, while the same Stage 2 training procedure consistently improves three architecturally distinct alternative student backbones.
  On the 9,861-query validation benchmark, the resulting 1B reranker achieves 0.7624 nDCG@6 while providing a favorable quality-efficiency tradeoff relative to larger alternatives.

</details>


### [84] [Convergence Theory of Knowledge Distillation in Asynchronous P2P Gossip Learning Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01952)
*Lucas Qingyang Fang,Tiyao Liu,Jinhao Jing,Zeji Li,Kaijie Chen,Harikrishna Kuttivelil,Katia Obraczka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decentralized knowledge distillation enables heterogeneous model training by exchanging predictions instead of weights, with provable convergence in function space for peer-to-peer networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decentralized learning on heterogeneous devices faces a fundamental obstacle: models with different architectures (different parameter counts) cannot be averaged using standard decentralized SGD. Knowledge distillation offers a solution by exchanging predictions rather than weights, but lacks theoretical convergence guarantees for fully decentralized, asynchronous peer-to-peer settings.

Method: Relocate consensus from parameter space to function (output) space by analyzing knowledge distillation as a geometric contraction operator in logit space. The analysis uses Hilbert space of predictions on a reference measure, with assumptions including smoothness/variance, realizability bridging parameter SGD to functional steps, and restricted task/KD alignment control.

Result: Prove convergence at rate O(1/(ηT)) to a neighborhood of size O(η)+O(B_f²)+O(ζ_f²), where η is step size, B_f is distance from task optimum to peers' reachable classes, and ζ_f measures persistent local-task heterogeneity. Experiments show knowledge distillation contracts function disagreement by 40-61× across heterogeneous networks while isolated training does not.

Conclusion: Knowledge distillation provides a theoretically sound approach for decentralized heterogeneous learning, with proven convergence in function space and practical effectiveness demonstrated across various network configurations, solving the fundamental problem of parameter averaging in heterogeneous federated/distributed learning.

Abstract: Decentralized, serverless learning increasingly connects devices running different architectures, where the standard tool, decentralized SGD, is undefined as models with different parameter counts cannot be averaged. Knowledge distillation (KD) exchanges soft predictions rather than weights and sidesteps this obstacle, yet convergence theory for fully decentralized, asynchronous peer-to-peer (P2P) KD is lacking. We provide one, relocating consensus from parameter space to function (output) space: a KD event is a geometric contraction operator in logit space on the peers' predictive distributions, which we analyse in the Hilbert space of predictions on a reference measure. Under standard smoothness/variance assumptions and two realizability assumptions, one bridging parameter SGD to the functional step and one controlling restricted task/KD alignment, the time-averaged functional stationarity and function-space disagreement converge at rate $O(1/(ηT))$ to an $O(η)+O(B_f^2)+O(ζ_f^2)$ neighbourhood. Here $B_f$ is the distance from the task optimum to the peers' reachable classes and $ζ_f$ measures persistent local-task heterogeneity. Across homogeneous, width-heterogeneous, and mixed-family networks of the experiments, KD contracts function disagreement by $40-61\times$, while isolated training does not. The sampled stationarity diagnostic has late transient exponents $0.99-1.90$ on the shared-skeleton main runs, and the four-point step-size sweep exhibits the predicted transient: neighbourhood tradeoff.

</details>


### [85] [FlashKAN: B-Spline KANs via Truncated Power Form](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01956)
*Naveen Mysore*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlashKAN accelerates KANs by replacing recursive B-spline evaluation with truncated power form, fused into single GPU kernel via torch.compile, preventing catastrophic cancellation, providing drop-in replacement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard Cox-de Boor recursion for evaluating B-spline activations in KANs consumes over 90% of forward-pass time, making it computationally expensive and inefficient.

Method: FlashKAN replaces recursive B-spline evaluation with the classical truncated power form, expresses each uniform cubic B-spline as five (x)_+^3 terms at shifted knot positions, and implements three key improvements: (1) torch.compile-fused single GPU kernel eliminating recursion, span lookup, and scatter-gather; (2) bounded-coordinate stabilization clamping normalized input to [0, k+1]; (3) production-ready open-source package.

Result: The approach significantly accelerates KAN forward passes by eliminating the recursive bottleneck, providing a production-ready implementation that serves as a drop-in replacement for existing KAN layers.

Conclusion: FlashKAN successfully addresses the computational inefficiency of KANs by leveraging classical approximation theory results and modern GPU optimization techniques, making KANs more practical for real-world applications.

Abstract: Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) place learnable B-spline activations on network edges rather than fixed activations on nodes. The standard Cox-de Boor recursion evaluates these activations through k sequential passes for degree-k splines, consuming over 90% of forward-pass time. FlashKAN replaces this recursion with the truncated power form, a classical result from approximation theory that expresses each uniform cubic B-spline as five (x)_+^3 terms at shifted knot positions. This paper makes three contributions: (1) a torch.compile-fused implementation that collapses these operations into a single GPU kernel, eliminating all recursion, span lookup, and scatter-gather operations; (2) a bounded-coordinate stabilization that clamps the normalized input to [0, k+1], preventing the catastrophic cancellation that historically motivated the Cox-de Boor recursion; and (3) a production-ready, open-source package (pip install flashkan) that serves as a drop-in replacement for existing KAN layers.

</details>


### [86] [A Unified Particle Filter LSTM for Data-Driven Process Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01967)
*Parvin Malekzadeh,Opher Baron,Dmitry Krass*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Unified PF-LSTM uses particle filtering with LSTM to handle partial observability in event logs, maintaining multiple latent state hypotheses for more accurate process simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Event logs provide only partial views of underlying process states (e.g., recording activity completions without service-start times), causing the same observed history to be consistent with multiple plausible latent conditions, while standard recurrent models compress each process prefix into a single deterministic state.

Method: Proposed Unified Particle Filter LSTM maintains and sequentially updates a weighted set of recurrent-state hypotheses, summarizes particle belief using weighted mean and learned features based on moment-generating function, and predicts next activity distribution and conditional quantiles of sojourn time.

Result: Framework consistently outperforms data-driven baselines across three real-world emergency department datasets in reproducing routing, duration, and system-level behavior, with particularly strong gains in settings with complex, partially observed process dynamics.

Conclusion: The Unified PF-LSTM effectively addresses partial observability in event logs through particle filtering, enabling more realistic data-driven process simulation by maintaining multiple latent state hypotheses rather than relying on single deterministic representations.

Abstract: Data-driven process simulation aims to generate realistic case trajectories from historical event logs without requiring an explicitly specified model of the underlying dynamics. Deep sequence models can capture complex temporal dependencies through next-activity probabilities and conditional time distributions. However, event logs provide only a partial view of the underlying process state, often recording activity completions without the corresponding service-start times. Consequently, the same observed process history may be consistent with multiple plausible latent process conditions, whereas standard recurrent models compress each process prefix into a single deterministic recurrent state. We propose a Unified Particle Filter LSTM (Unified PF-LSTM) that maintains and sequentially updates a weighted set of recurrent-state hypotheses. We summarize this particle belief using its weighted mean and learned features based on the moment-generating function. The resulting representation is used to predict a categorical distribution over the next activity and conditional quantiles of the current activity's sojourn time. The framework is trained end-to-end from event-log data and evaluated on three real-world emergency department datasets. The results show that the proposed framework consistently outperforms the considered data-driven baselines in reproducing routing, duration, and system-level behavior across all datasets, with particularly strong gains in settings where complex process dynamics are only partially reflected in the available event logs.

</details>


### [87] [CAHR-Net: Condition-Adaptive Hysteresis Reconstruction for Compact and Interpretable Magnetic Core Loss Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01991)
*Chunye Gong,Cong Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAHR-Net: A condition-adaptive hysteresis reconstruction network that injects operating conditions (frequency, temperature, waveform) into intermediate hysteresis representation using feature-wise linear modulation, achieving state-of-the-art magnetic core loss prediction with minimal parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing models treat operating conditions as terminal scalars or append them to features, losing the ability to reshape hysteresis loops which is where these conditions physically act. There's a need for models that preserve the interpretable chain from flux density to field reconstruction while properly accounting for condition effects.

Method: Proposes CAHR-Net with feature-wise linear modulation to inject frequency, temperature, and waveform statistics into intermediate reconstruction representation. Uses a matched large-batch training protocol with AdamW, cosine scheduling, and staged reconstruction-to-power-loss objective. Preserves physical interpretability through flux density → magnetic field reconstruction → loop-area integration → power loss estimation.

Result: Achieves average p95 relative error of 6.89% with only 1874 parameters, lowest among compared methods. Reduces backbone error from 7.47% to 6.89%, and most difficult material D from 16.40% to 14.87%. Outperforms black-box solutions with 48x fewer parameters. Shows lower worst-material p95 than strongest black-box approach.

Conclusion: The improvement stems from coupling physical loop reconstruction, structured condition modulation, and matched optimization trajectory. CAHR-Net successfully injects operating conditions where they physically act in hysteresis reconstruction, achieving superior accuracy with extreme parameter efficiency while maintaining physical interpretability.

Abstract: Magnetic core loss originates in the hysteresis loop: the energy dissipated per excitation cycle equals the loop area, and frequency, temperature, and waveform shape set the loss by reshaping the loop geometry. Most existing models let these conditions act only on a terminal scalar - empirical equations fold them into fitted exponents, and data-driven predictors append them to encoded features - so no intermediate hysteresis representation remains for the conditions to reshape. This paper proposes CAHR-Net, a condition-adaptive hysteresis reconstruction network that injects the operating conditions where they physically act. It preserves the interpretable chain from flux density waveform to magnetic field reconstruction, loop-area integration, and power loss estimation, and uses feature-wise linear modulation to inject frequency, temperature, and waveform statistics into the intermediate reconstruction representation. A matched large-batch training protocol based on AdamW, cosine scheduling, and a staged reconstruction-to-power-loss objective is also reported, because the modulation pathway takes effect only within it. On the MagNet final A-E material protocol, CAHR-Net attains an average p95 relative error of 6.89% with only 1874 parameters, the lowest among all compared methods, together with a lower worst-material p95 than the strongest black-box solution at about 48x fewer parameters; it reduces the average p95 of the physical reconstruction backbone from 7.47% to 6.89% and the p95 of material D, the most difficult material, from 16.40% to 14.87%. Ablation and condition-slice analyses attribute the improvement to the coupling of physical loop reconstruction, structured condition modulation, and the matched optimization trajectory.

</details>


### [88] [Train What You Deploy: Closing the MLP Reachability Gap in Low-Rank Clone Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02006)
*Wenhui Chen,Zhifeng Li,Jie Zhou,Navan Preet Singh,Madalina Ciobanu,Chenghua Wang,Qingqing Mao,Ritankar Das*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method called Dense-LRC/CORE-LRC fixes the limitation in Low-Rank Clone (LRC) distillation by ensuring training matches deployed weights, recovering stranded capacity and improving student performance without changing inference costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current weight-inheritance distillation methods like LRC create a mismatch: the deployed inference weights have more capacity than what's trainable during distillation, leaving significant linear degrees of freedom unused and untrainable.

Method: The authors propose "train what you deploy" principle with two implementations: Dense-LRC and CORE-LRC. Both start from identical LRC warm starts but train the entire deployed matrix rather than just the teacher-induced slice, collapsing to a single deployed weight without changing inference parameters or FLOPs.

Result: Significant performance gains: +2.36 to +10.45 Avg9 improvements across three teacher models, with 2x token efficiency on Qwen teacher. A 1.5B student matches its 9T-token teacher's average performance with only ~10B distillation tokens, and a 2.7B student beats Meta's official Llama3.1-8B compression at ~900x fewer tokens.

Conclusion: Training the full deployed weight matrix rather than just a teacher-induced slice recovers stranded capacity in distillation, enabling more efficient knowledge transfer and better student performance without additional inference costs or parameter increases.

Abstract: A compressed student has two shapes that need not agree: the weight it deploys at inference and the weight family its training can reach. We show that a state-of-the-art weight-inheritance distiller, Low-Rank Clone (LRC), deploys a full-width student MLP but ties training to a teacher-induced slice, leaving 62.5-81.4% of each deployed matrix's independent linear degrees of freedom unreachable-paid for at inference, never trainable. Our principle is one line: train what you deploy. From the identical LRC warm start, we make the training object the entire deployed matrix, with no change in deployed shape, deployed parameter count, or inference FLOPs, via two mergeable realizations (Dense-LRC and CORE-LRC) that both collapse to one deployed weight. This recovers stranded capacity: taking the stronger realization per teacher, +2.36/+2.71/+10.45 Avg9 over matched-budget plain-LRC baselines across three teachers (Llama3.2-3B, Llama3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-3B), with the largest gain on the widest teacher (Qwen), where it reaches the original recipe's approx. 20B-token accuracy at 10B tokens (2x token efficiency); there the strictly same-lineage arm still recovers +6.39, the fully controlled figure. Controls strongly support attributing the gain to the enlarged reachable set, rather than to added parameters or the recipe. From approx. 10B distillation tokens plus a short SFT, a half-parameter 1.5B student matches its approx. 9T-token teacher's 9-task macro-average, within evaluation noise and with a residual MMLU deficit, and a 2.7B student beats Meta's own official compression of Llama3.1-8B at ~900x fewer compression tokens (a token count under unmatched recipes, not a compute claim). All results are from single-seed runs on the LRC backbone.

</details>


### [89] [Source-Free Class Relearning: Diagnosing Forgetting in Class Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02018)
*Zahra Dehghani,Pablo Piantanida,Mohammadhadi Shateri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether forget classes removed through machine unlearning can be recovered without access to original data, showing that several unlearning methods retain recoverable structure despite appearing to successfully forget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current class unlearning evaluation focuses on low forget accuracy, but this doesn't guarantee erasure of class structure. Approximate unlearning can leave recoverable information in representations, and existing recovery methods require original data, auxiliary data, or reference models.

Method: Proposes Source-Free Relearning Audit (SFRA) using white-box approach: generates candidate embeddings in representation space, applies model-guided confidence filtering to create high-confidence retain probes and low-confidence boundary-adjacent probes relabeled as forget class, then performs classifier-head update with synthetic probes.

Result: Several unlearning methods show substantial source-free recoverability. For some methods, recoverability exceeds that of retrained reference models. Introduces Relearning Score (RS) to jointly measure forget-class recovery and retain-accuracy preservation.

Conclusion: Low forget accuracy doesn't guarantee successful unlearning - recoverable structure persists. Source-free relearning reveals limitations of current unlearning methods and provides more rigorous evaluation of whether class structure is truly erased.

Abstract: Class unlearning aims to remove a model's ability to recognize designated forget classes while preserving performance on retain classes. However, low forget accuracy after unlearning does not necessarily mean the class structure has been erased. Approximate unlearning methods can alter classifier decision boundaries while leaving recoverable structure in the representation. Prior work has shown that forget classes can be recovered, but existing approaches require real forget or retain samples, auxiliary data, or reference checkpoints. We study class relearning in a strictly source-free setting, asking whether a forget class can be recovered through a classifier-head update using only the unlearned model. Our approach rests on a theoretical analysis establishing a sufficient alignment condition under which a single gradient step on a synthetic probe set increases the expected logit margin of the forget class. Building on this, we propose a white-box Source-Free Relearning Audit (SFRA), which generates candidate embeddings in representation space and uses model-guided confidence filtering to construct high-confidence retain probes and low-confidence boundary-adjacent probes that are relabelled as the forget class. Gaussian sampling and Softmax confidence are used by default, while ablations with alternative proposal distributions and uncertainty criteria show that recoverability is not specific to these choices. To quantify recoverability, we introduce the Relearning Score (RS), which jointly measures forget-class recovery and retain-accuracy preservation, and report class-matched $Δ$RS relative to a retrained reference. Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and TinyImageNet with ResNet-18, ViT-B/16, and Swin-T show that several unlearning methods exhibit substantial source-free recoverability, and that for a subset of methods this recoverability exceeds the matched retrained reference.

</details>


### [90] [Act More, Decide Less: Skill-Guided Adaptive Action Chunking for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02042)
*Yanting Yang,Can Jin,Jinman Zhao,Jiahao Wu,Yang Zhou,Zhepeng Wang,Zhendong Wang,Mu Zhou,Dimitris N. Metaxas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SPACE improves LLM agent efficiency for long-horizon tasks by learning to emit variable-length action chunks instead of one action per round, using programmatic skills distillation to learn proper chunk boundaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ReAct-style LLM agents are inefficient for long-horizon tasks because they issue only one primitive action per LLM round, spending many rounds on routine action sequences. Training agents to emit variable-length action chunks with standard RL fails - agents either collapse to single-action behavior or over-commit to excessively long sequences due to inability to learn chunk boundaries.

Method: SPACE distills chunk-boundary supervision from trajectory-induced programmatic skills. It induces two-level programmatic skills from successful trajectories, where subskill boundaries serve as direct chunk-boundary supervision. This temporal structure is then distilled into a primitive-chunk policy via hybrid on-/off-policy optimization with chunk-aware credit assignment.

Result: Experiments on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld show SPACE improves success rates by 7.0%-31.3% over the strongest baseline in each setting while reducing average LLM decision rounds by up to 78.9%.

Conclusion: SPACE successfully addresses the chunk boundary learning problem in LLM agents for long-horizon tasks, enabling more efficient execution through variable-length action chunks while maintaining or improving task success rates, representing a significant step toward more efficient interactive AI systems.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents for long-horizon interactive tasks typically follow a ReAct-style protocol, issuing one primitive action per LLM round. While this enables frequent replanning, it is inefficient for long-horizon tasks where many rounds are spent on routine action sequences. A natural alternative is to let the agent emit variable-length action chunks. However, naively training such policies with standard reinforcement learning fails: the agent either collapses to single-action behavior or over-commits to excessively long sequences. Both failures share a common root cause: the inability to learn chunk boundaries. We propose SPACE, which addresses this challenge by distilling chunk-boundary supervision from trajectory-induced programmatic skills. We induce two-level programmatic skills from successful trajectories, where subskill boundaries serve as direct chunk-boundary supervision. This temporal structure is then distilled into a primitive-chunk policy via hybrid on-/off-policy optimization with chunk-aware credit assignment. Experiments on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld show that SPACE improves success rates by 7.0%-31.3% over the strongest baseline in each setting while reducing average LLM decision rounds by up to 78.9%.

</details>


### [91] [The Dynamics of Continuous Mixture Collapse in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02049)
*Ali Backour*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transformers and training amplify geometric distortion of latent mixture states, causing failure to preserve multiple reasoning directions through softmax-autoregressive dynamics and context-dependent scaling needs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs use continuous latent states (weighted token mixtures) to maintain multiple reasoning possibilities, but pretrained models fail to preserve these mixtures. Understanding why this failure occurs is crucial for improving latent-state reasoning methods.

Method: Combination of theoretical analysis and controlled empirical investigations on various models. Three distinct failure sources are examined: architecture-induced geometric distortion, softmax-autoregressive dynamical system effects, and context-dependent correction requirements for multi-component mixtures.

Result: Identified three independent failure sources: 1) Transformers distort mixture geometry, amplified by training; 2) Softmax readout and autoregressive feedback form dynamical systems that either amplify differences or contract mixtures; 3) Exact preservation of multi-component mixtures requires context-dependent correction with dimensionality growing with component count. Empirical verification shows transition near theoretical thresholds, with pretrained models predominantly on amplifying side.

Conclusion: The failure to preserve latent mixture states stems from fundamental architectural and training limitations, including geometric distortion, dynamical system effects in the readout-feedback loop, and scaling challenges for multi-component mixtures. These insights reveal intrinsic limitations of current transformer-based approaches for maintaining multiple reasoning directions.

Abstract: LLMs latent-state reasoning methods replace discrete intermediate tokens with continuous states, such as weighted mixtures of token embeddings, to retain multiple possible reasoning directions rather than committing to one. Yet pretrained language models often fail to preserve these mixtures. We study why through a combination of theoretical analysis and controlled empirical investigations on a variety of models. We identify three independent, distinct sources of failure. First, transformer architectures already distort mixture geometry, and training substantially amplifies this effect. Moreover, the failure can occur even if the model transports mixtures perfectly linearly: the softmax readout and autoregressive feedback form a dynamical system that either amplifies small differences until one component of the mixture dominates or contracts different mixtures until they become indistinguishable. We verify this theoretical prediction empirically: the observed transition between contraction and amplification occurs near the theoretical threshold derived by our analysis, and pretrained-model rollouts lie predominantly on the amplifying side. Finally, we generalize to mixtures of many components and show that exact preservation generally requires context-dependent correction, whose required dimensionality can grow with the number of components.

</details>


### [92] [DynG-Diff: A State-Aware Dynamic Guidance Diffusion Framework for Probabilistic Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02068)
*Zhente Zhang,Zhengwei Ni,Wei Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DynG-Diff is a variable-sensitive dynamic guidance diffusion framework for probabilistic multivariate time-series forecasting that adaptively adjusts guidance based on variable reliability to handle information heterogeneity across variables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion-based methods for multivariate time series forecasting rely on task-specific conditional paradigms that lack flexibility and struggle with "information heterogeneity"—the significantly varying noise levels and evolutionary patterns across different variables in complex dynamical systems.

Method: The framework uses: (1) two-stage separated training with an unconditional diffusion backbone to model joint distribution; (2) a lightweight state-aware policy network that infers variable reliability from real-time noisy states and denoising estimates, outputting a dynamic guidance strength matrix; (3) mathematical formulation of dynamic weights as local precision of observation distribution.

Result: Extensive experiments on real-world benchmarks demonstrate competitive probabilistic forecasting performance against state-of-the-art conditional diffusion models and improved robustness under severe observation corruption.

Conclusion: DynG-Diff effectively addresses information heterogeneity in multivariate time series forecasting through adaptive dynamic guidance, outperforming existing methods while providing better handling of variable reliability and noise interference.

Abstract: Probabilistic multivariate time series (MTS) forecasting is crucial for modeling complex dynamical systems. However, existing diffusion-based methods rely on task-specific conditional paradigms that lack flexibility and struggle with inherent "information heterogeneity"--the significantly varying noise levels and evolutionary patterns across variables. To address this, we propose DynG-Diff, a variable-sensitive dynamic guidance diffusion framework for probabilistic multivariate time-series forecasting: (1) DynG-Diff adopts a two-stage separated training strategy and uses an unconditional diffusion backbone to model the joint distribution of multivariate time series. (2) DynG-Diff introduces a lightweight state-aware policy network that adaptively infers variable reliability from real-time noisy states and one-step denoising estimates, outputting a dynamic guidance strength matrix. (3) DynG-Diff mathematically formulates this dynamic weight as the local precision of the observation distribution, enabling precise guidance for high-confidence variables during inference while filtering out interference from anomalous noise. Extensive experiments on real-world benchmarks demonstrate competitive probabilistic forecasting performance against state-of-the-art conditional diffusion models and improved robustness under severe observation corruption.The implementation code is available at: https://github.com/TT-20011031/DynG-Diff

</details>


### [93] [XMerge: Cross-Axis Selection and Reconstructive Layer Merging for LLM Depth Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02083)
*Jundong Hu,Shekar Ramachandran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: XMerge is a post-training method for compressing transformer models by removing layers without task labels or fine-tuning, using cross-axis selection to identify removable blocks and local boundary reconstruction to preserve model quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing depth-compression methods for removing transformer layers often cause substantial and unpredictable quality loss across different models, creating a need for a more reliable compression approach.

Method: XMerge has two components: 1) Cross-axis selection identifies blocks with low relative-magnitude and angular hidden-state change, and 2) Local boundary reconstruction re-fits the adjacent surviving block to match the original two-block output. It requires no task labels, fine-tuning, architectural changes, or additional inference parameters.

Result: Across seven Llama and Qwen models (0.5B-8B), five baselines, and three removal levels, XMerge outperforms baselines, especially at aggressive removal (k=4). It ranks first on 6/7 backbones on CORE and MMLU, avoids large perplexity increases, never collapses across 14 (model, regime) cells, and shows best calibration on tested probes.

Conclusion: XMerge provides an effective post-training method for transformer depth compression that maintains quality better than existing approaches, with local reconstruction providing most gains and cross-axis fusion helping when selection axes disagree. The construction cost is recovered after tens of thousands of requests.

Abstract: Removing complete transformer layers preserves a standard serving architecture, but existing depth-compression methods can lose substantial quality, and the loss varies unpredictably across models. We introduce XMerge, a post-training method with two components. Cross-axis selection identifies a block with low relative-magnitude and angular hidden-state change, and local boundary reconstruction re-fits the adjacent surviving block to match the original two-block output. XMerge uses no task labels or end-to-end fine-tuning, and it introduces neither architectural changes nor additional inference-time parameters. Across seven Llama and Qwen backbones (0.5B-8B), five published baselines, and three layer-reduction levels, its advantage over baselines is largest at the most aggressive removal: at k=4 it ranks first on six of seven backbones on CORE (a 22-task aggregate) and, separately, on six of seven on MMLU (five of seven on both at once), while avoiding the large perplexity increases of several competing operators. In a task-level bootstrap, the 95% confidence intervals for the three largest CORE margins exclude zero; the remaining margins are consistent with ties. Across the 14 (model, regime) cells it is also the only evaluated operator that never collapses, ranking top-2 in both zero-shot and in-context regimes; on a first calibration probe (one backbone) it is the best-calibrated operator. Ablations show that local reconstruction provides most of the gain, while cross-axis fusion helps when the two selection axes disagree. The additional construction cost is recovered through per-token decode savings after roughly tens of thousands of requests.

</details>


### [94] [TC-Next: Zero-Shot Multimodal Cyclone Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02085)
*Zhe Wang,Sijie Chen,Yiming Luo,Daehyun Kim,Chien-Yi Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TC-Next is a multimodal AI model that improves tropical cyclone forecasting by combining GraphCast weather forecasts with satellite imagery, achieving better accuracy than conventional methods across multiple forecast systems without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the need for improved tropical cyclone forecasting by leveraging foundation models and multimodal data (weather forecasts + satellite imagery) to overcome limitations of conventional rule-based trackers and achieve better accuracy with lower computational cost.

Method: Developed TC-Next, a multimodal deep learning model combining: 1) Atmospheric forecast fields from foundation models (GraphCast, Pangu-Weather, IFS HRES) for kinematic/thermodynamic data, and 2) GridSat infrared satellite imagery. The model was trained only on Western Pacific GraphCast forecasts but designed for cross-model compatibility.

Result: TC-Next reduces track error by 15-44% and intensity error by 3-6× compared to conventional TempestExtremes. It performs well across different forecast systems (GraphCast, Pangu-Weather, IFS HRES) without retraining, and outperforms specialized direct trackers in intensity prediction at all lead times (6-24h). Multimodal approach improves tracking at all leads and intensity at longer leads.

Conclusion: TC-Next demonstrates that multimodal AI combining foundation weather models with satellite imagery can significantly improve tropical cyclone forecasting accuracy, shows strong generalization across different forecast systems, and offers a promising approach for operational forecasting with lower computational requirements.

Abstract: We present TropicalCycloneNext (TC-Next), a multimodal deep learning model that forecasts tropical cyclone track and intensity at $6$-$24$ h leads by leveraging a foundation model's forecast fields of atmospheric kinematic and thermodynamic fields and GridSat infrared satellite imagery. Trained only on GraphCast forecasts over the Western Pacific (WP), yet reliant only on generic atmospheric variables, TC-Next on GraphCast lowers track error by $15$-$44\%$ and intensity error by a factor of $3$-$6$ relative to a conventional, rule-based tracker, TempestExtremes; applied without retraining to the forecast fields of Pangu-Weather and IFS HRES, it stays ahead of TempestExtremes on both. Applied zero-shot to the generic weather fields of WeatherNext Cyclones on the 2025 WP season, TC-Next attains lower intensity error at every lead time, and lower or comparable track error, compared to that model's specialized direct tracker in a deterministic comparison. Our ablation studies show that our multimodal model is able to utilize the additional modality to improve performance in tracking errors at every lead time and in intensity prediction at longer lead times.

</details>


### [95] [Compositional Spectral Prompts for LLM-based Online Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02093)
*Seungyoon Choi,Hyunchul Kim,Jae-Gil Lee,Chanyoung Park*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoSPOT: A novel LLM-based online time series forecasting framework that uses frozen LLMs with compositional spectral prompts for efficient adaptation to non-stationary environments, achieving superior performance in extended online phases and cross-dataset settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory buffer-based retrieval strategies for Online Time Series Forecasting (OTSF) struggle with long-term adaptation and fail to generalize to unseen patterns in non-stationary environments.

Method: Uses pre-trained LLM as backbone online forecaster (kept frozen), employs compositional spectral prompts grounded in frequency-domain bases to guide the model with input distribution. Decomposes time series into frequency bases and composes corresponding spectral basis prompts based on amplitudes, allowing unseen patterns to be represented as combinations of learned basis prompts.

Result: Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate superiority and practicality across challenging online scenarios, including extended online phases and cross-dataset settings with substantial distribution shifts.

Conclusion: CoSPOT provides an effective LLM-based framework for online time series forecasting that overcomes limitations of existing approaches by using compositional spectral prompts for efficient adaptation while maintaining strong generalization to unseen patterns.

Abstract: To address the sequential and evolving nature of time series, the Online Time Series Forecasting (OTSF) task has been extensively studied in multiple domains. Existing research focuses on adapting to non-stationary environments by employing memory buffer-based retrieval strategies. However, we observe that such frameworks struggle with long-term adaptation and fail to generalize to unseen patterns. To this end, we introduce CoSPOT, an LLM-based online time series forecasting framework that leverages a pre-trained LLM as the backbone online forecaster, motivated by its strong few-shot capabilities. For efficient online adaptation, CoSPOT keeps the LLM frozen and employs compositional spectral prompts grounded in frequency-domain bases to guide the model with the overall distribution of the input, thereby substantially reducing the number of parameters updated during the online phase. Specifically, CoSPOT decomposes time series into frequency bases and composes the corresponding spectral basis prompts according to their amplitudes, allowing unseen patterns to be represented as new combinations of learned basis prompts. Our extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate the superiority and practicality of CoSPOT across challenging online scenarios, including extended online phases and cross-dataset settings with substantial distribution shifts. Our code is available at https://github.com/seungyoon-Choi/CoSPOT.

</details>


### [96] [Federated LoRA Adaptation of BiomedCLIP Across Four International Chest X-Ray Cohorts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02101)
*Sanjaya Poudel,Nirajan Kunwor,Manish Dhakal,Debesh Jha,Sunil Kumar Gaire*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Federated LoRA adaptation improves BiomedCLIP for chest radiograph classification across diverse international cohorts, achieving near-centralized performance without data sharing and demonstrating that specialized aggregation methods are crucial for success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical imaging faces challenges due to patient data privacy regulations, institutional heterogeneity in scanners/protocols/compute, and the need to leverage multimodal vision-language models. Federated learning with LoRA offers a practical solution by enabling collaborative model adaptation without data exchange.

Method: Benchmarked federated PEFT of BiomedCLIP for chest radiograph classification across four public cohorts in USA, Vietnam, and Spain. Used LoRA for parameter-efficient updates and tested different aggregation methods: SVD-based product-space aggregation (FlexLoRA) vs naive factor averaging, and compared FedAvg with FedProx optimizer.

Result: Federated LoRA adaptation improved shared-class AUC from mean 0.687 (unadapted backbone) to 0.802 across all cohorts. FlexLoRA's SVD-based aggregation was essential (naive averaging dropped AUC by 0.097). Federation improved weaker cohorts while preserving strong ones, approaching centralized performance (0.812). FedProx showed no benefit over FedAvg, as LoRA's low-rank updates already limit client drift.

Conclusion: Biomedical vision-language models can be effectively adapted collaboratively across heterogeneous, geographically distributed institutions using federated LoRA with specialized aggregation methods, achieving near-centralized performance without compromising data privacy.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) lets institutions train a shared model without exchanging data, and Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) makes this practical at scale by communicating only compact low-rank updates. Biomedical imaging is a compelling setting for this combination: patient data are archived behind privacy regulations, and institutions differ widely in scanners, protocols, and compute. Such heterogeneity raises the question of how federated LoRA updates should be aggregated, increasingly pressing as multimodal vision-language models become central to medical image analysis. We benchmark federated Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) of BiomedCLIP for chest radiograph classification across four public cohorts on three continents (USA, Vietnam, Spain). Federated LoRA adaptation improves shared-class AUC on all four cohorts over the unadapted BiomedCLIP backbone (mean 0.687 to 0.802), showing that the gains come from federated adaptation rather than from the pretrained model's zero-shot ability. Relative to isolated single-cohort training, federation improves the weaker cohorts while largely preserving the strongest and approaches a centralized reference (0.812) that pools all data. The singular value decomposition (SVD)-based product-space aggregation introduced by FlexLoRA is essential to this gain (naive factor averaging drops mean AUC by 0.097), whereas a drift-correcting optimizer (FedProx) shows no benefit over FedAvg in our single-seed runs, consistent with LoRA's low-rank updates already limiting client drift. Biomedical vision-language models can thus be adapted collaboratively across heterogeneous, geographically distributed institutions without centralizing data.

</details>


### [97] [A Unified Rate-Distortion Perspective on Vector, Product, and Scalar Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02107)
*Xianghong Fang,Wenlong Mou,Yuan Yuan,Dehan Kong,Tim G. J. Rudner*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a unified rate-distortion framework for analyzing discrete visual tokenization methods (VQ, PQ, SQ), showing that distortion minimization, not codebook utilization, drives reconstruction fidelity, and establishes fairness conditions for quantizer comparison.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current discrete visual tokenization methods lack a unified conceptual framework for understanding quantization tradeoffs. The field is driven by various quantization approaches (vector, scalar, product) without systematic analysis of their fundamental tradeoffs.

Method: Proposes a rate-distortion perspective treating quantization as lossy compression. Uses token count and codebook size as coding rate, quantization error as distortion. Theoretically and empirically analyzes gradient discrepancy from Straight-Through Estimator (STE). Establishes fairness conditions: controlling latent statistics and enforcing identical coding rates.

Result: Shows distortion minimization is the primary objective for reconstruction fidelity, not codebook utilization. Establishes a fairness framework for quantization comparison. Recovers the VQ-PQ-SQ distortion hierarchy where VQ achieves lowest distortion under fair conditions.

Conclusion: Provides foundational rate-distortion framework for discrete visual tokenization, resolves evaluation ambiguities, offers controlled framework for isolating intrinsic quantization effectiveness under fixed-rate constraints.

Abstract: Discrete visual tokenization, predominantly driven by vector, scalar, and product quantization, lacks a unified conceptual framework for understanding quantization tradeoffs. In this paper, we propose a unified rate--distortion perspective on modern discrete visual tokenization. By viewing quantization as lossy compression, we characterize the nominal fixed-length coding rate through token count and codebook size, and quantization error as the distortion. Within this framework, we resolve three central questions. First, we theoretically and empirically show that minimizing distortion, rather than maximizing codebook utilization, is the primary intrinsic objective for reconstruction fidelity, with a direct connection to the STE-induced gradient discrepancy. Second, we establish two critical fairness conditions for intrinsic quantization comparison: controlling latent feature statistics and enforcing identical coding rates. Third, under these conditions, we recover the VQ--PQ--SQ distortion hierarchy in modern visual tokenization and show empirically that modern VQ methods achieve the lowest distortion. This work provides a foundational rate--distortion reframing of modern discrete visual tokenization, resolves ambiguities in quantizer evaluation, and provides a controlled framework for isolating intrinsic quantization effectiveness under fixed-rate constraints.

</details>


### [98] [A Computational Comparison of Fourier Spectral Differentiation and Spatial Automatic Differentiation in Periodic Physics-Informed Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02110)
*Xilai Liang,Zhao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PINNs using Fourier spectral differentiation instead of automatic differentiation for spatial derivatives achieve 2.9-18.5x training speedup and 68-94% GPU memory reduction on 1D periodic PDEs, with comparable accuracy but requiring uniform structured grids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic differentiation (AD) in PINNs has high computational and memory costs for multiple or high-order spatial derivatives. Need more efficient spatial differentiation methods that maintain accuracy while reducing computational burden.

Method: Replace spatial AD with Fourier spectral differentiation in periodic physical-space PINNs. Network outputs are evaluated on uniform periodic grids, transformed to Fourier space, where spatial derivatives are obtained through spectral multiplication with reuse of Fourier coefficients across derivative orders.

Result: Across five equation-framework settings (Allen-Cahn, KdV with standard PINNs; Allen-Cahn, KdV, Kuramoto-Sivashinsky with Causal PINNs), Fourier differentiation yields 2.90× to 18.52× training speedups and reduces peak GPU memory by 68.7%-94.1%. Accuracy remains comparable (same order of L2 errors) to AD.

Conclusion: Fourier spectral differentiation provides substantially lower training time and memory usage than spatial AD while retaining comparable solution accuracy for 1D periodic PDEs, at the cost of requiring uniform structured spatial grids.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) commonly evaluate the spatial derivatives appearing in partial differential equation residuals using automatic differentiation (AD), whose computational and memory costs can become substantial when multiple or high-order derivatives are required. We perform a controlled comparison of spatial AD and Fourier spectral differentiation in periodic physical-space PINNs. Within each paired experiment, the neural representation, temporal differentiation, optimizer, sampling procedure, and training schedule are held fixed, so that the two cases differ only in the spatial differentiation procedure. For the Fourier variant, network outputs are evaluated on a uniform periodic grid and transformed to Fourier space, where spatial derivatives are obtained through spectral multiplication and the same Fourier coefficients are reused across derivative orders. We compare the two procedures in standard PINNs for the Allen--Cahn and Korteweg--de Vries equations and in Causal PINNs for the Allen--Cahn, Korteweg--de Vries, and Kuramoto--Sivashinsky equations. Across these five equation--framework settings, Fourier differentiation yields mean paired end-to-end training speedups ranging from $2.90\times$ to $18.52\times$ and reduces peak allocated graphics processing unit (GPU) memory by $68.7\%$--$94.1\%$. The final relative $L_2$ errors remain of the same order, with neither differentiation procedure showing a consistent accuracy advantage. For the one-dimensional periodic benchmarks considered here, Fourier spectral differentiation therefore provides substantially lower training time and memory usage than spatial AD while retaining comparable solution error, at the cost of requiring a uniform structured spatial grid.

</details>


### [99] [Scalable Bayesian Optimization of Composite Functions for Image-Based Inverse Problems in Materials Characterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02126)
*Dasol Yoon,Poompol Buathong,Chia-Hao Lee,Yujia Zhang,David A. Muller,Peter I. Frazier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new simulation-efficient method called SBOCF uses Bayesian optimization with composite function structure and patch-level image summaries to estimate critical electron microscopy parameters like thickness and crystal mistilt from PACBED patterns, dramatically reducing required simulations while improving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating physical parameters from electron microscopy images relies on expensive physics-based simulations. Current methods like grid searches scale poorly and neural-network approaches require extensive pretraining that may not transfer to new experimental conditions, creating a need for more efficient and adaptable methods.

Method: SBOCF (Scalable Bayesian Optimization of Composite Functions) exploits the known composite structure of image-matching objectives by representing PACBED images with patch-level summaries and two correction terms. This reduces the number of modeled outputs from 24,649 pixels to just 11 parameters while preserving the original pixel-wise objective function.

Result: Under a 50-simulation budget, SBOCF outperformed standard Bayesian optimization by reducing median final SSE up to 290x for thick samples. On experimental data, it produced consistent parameter estimates without task-specific pretraining, and using SBOCF estimates in downstream ptychographic reconstruction recovered sharp atomic structures that were otherwise blurred.

Conclusion: SBOCF is a promising approach for inverse problems involving expensive simulators and high-dimensional structured outputs, offering simulation-efficient parameter estimation for electron microscopy and potentially other scientific imaging domains.

Abstract: Estimating physical parameters from scientific images is a common inverse problem in materials characterization that often relies on expensive physics-based simulations. In electron microscopy, specimen thickness and crystal mistilt are critical parameters that govern how electrons scatter through the sample, and therefore the accuracy of any atomic-scale structure recovered from it. They are commonly inferred by matching experimental position-averaged convergent-beam electron diffraction (PACBED) patterns to simulated ones, but grid searches scale poorly and neural-network methods require extensive pretraining that may not transfer to new conditions. Here, we propose scalable Bayesian optimization of composite functions (SBOCF), a simulation-efficient method that exploits the known composite structure of the image-matching objective and the intermediate information contained in simulated images. By representing PACBED images with patch-level summaries and two correction terms, SBOCF preserves the original pixel-wise objective while reducing the number of modeled outputs from 24,649 to 11. Under a budget of 50 simulator evaluations, SBOCF outperformed standard Bayesian optimization with expected improvement on synthetic SrTiO3 benchmarks with thick and thin specimens, reducing the median final SSE by up to 290x in the thick-sample case. On experimental data, SBOCF produced parameter estimates consistent with previously reported values without task-specific pretraining. For a simulated mistilted specimen, using the SBOCF estimates in a downstream ptychographic reconstruction recovered sharp atoms that were otherwise blurred. These results establish SBOCF as a promising approach for inverse problems involving expensive simulators and high-dimensional structured outputs.

</details>


### [100] [Online Non-Monotone DR-Submodular Maximization Matching the Offline $0.401$ Factor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02145)
*Vaneet Aggarwal,Yiyang Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper achieves online optimization of non-monotone DR-submodular functions with 0.401 approximation factor, matching the best offline guarantee, using weighted online learners instead of offline construction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
