Motivation: There's a gap between offline (0.401 approximation) and online (1/e ≈ 0.367) guarantees for non-monotone DR-submodular maximization over convex down-closed sets. Online algorithms previously couldn't match the best offline approximation factor.

Method: Replaces offline objective-dependent box step with weighted online learner to cumulatively control residual terms. Uses an exact asymmetric balance theorem to preserve offline coefficients despite adversarial variation. Implements batching strategies for efficiency trade-offs and randomized blocking for bandit feedback.

Result: Achieves 0.401 approximation factor online with O(T^{3/4}) regret and O(dT^{1/4}) oracle calls per round. General batching yields O(T^δ) calls per round with O(T^{4/5-δ/5}) regret, including O(T^{4/5}) regret with one call per round. Under positive-anchor condition, maintains 0.401 factor with O(T^{5/6}) bandit regret.

Conclusion: The paper successfully bridges the gap between offline and online performance for non-monotone DR-submodular optimization, achieving the best known offline approximation factor (0.401) in an online adversarial setting with various efficient implementations.

Abstract: We study online maximization of nonnegative, non-monotone DR-submodular functions over compact convex down-closed subsets of the $d$-dimensional unit cube. The best known constructive offline approximation factor is $0.401$ under the corresponding meta-solvability assumptions, whereas comparable adversarial online guarantees had remained at $1/e$. We show that this factor is also achievable online. In the post-decision full-information value-oracle model, our algorithm attains factor $0.401$ with sublinear approximate regret when oracle feedback is conditionally unbiased and bounded.
  The online algorithm does not run the offline construction on a changing objective. Instead, it replaces the offline objective-dependent box step by a weighted online learner that controls the required residual terms cumulatively. An exact asymmetric balance theorem preserves the offline coefficients despite adversarial variation. The direct implementation has $O(T^{3/4})$ regret and uses $O(dT^{1/4})$ oracle calls per round. More generally, for every $δ\in[0,1/4]$, batching gives $O(T^δ)$ calls per round and $O(T^{4/5-δ/5})$ regret, including a one-call $O(T^{4/5})$ endpoint. Under a positive-anchor condition, randomized blocking retains factor $0.401$ with $O(T^{5/6})$ one-point bandit regret.

</details>


### [101] [Exact Limits of Random Projections for Preserving Geometry: Distance Recovery, Nearest-Neighbor Rankings, and Covariance Shape in Gaussian Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02155)
*Piyush Sao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) lemma is uninformative about retained geometric information in high dimensions - a Gaussian replacement map can satisfy JL bounds while being independent of original data, showing distance preservation doesn't guarantee useful geometry for inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In high dimensions, distances concentrate around a baseline while meaningful geometric information resides in smaller fluctuations. The standard JL bound on distance preservation may be misleading about how much geometric structure is actually retained for downstream tasks like comparison or inference.

Method: Analyze feature recovery from linear sketches under squared-error loss, using optimal conditional expectation decoders. Diagonalize the resulting linear operator in closed form for isotropic Gaussian data, deriving singular values that quantify feature recovery capabilities.

Result: For isotropic Gaussian data: kth singular value scales as (m/d)^(k/2); rank-m sketch retains at most m/d fraction of variance of any feature; Kendall correlation scales as √(m/d); one projection can satisfy JL bound while correlation vanishes when log n ≪ m ≪ d; Haar-averaged retained covariance-shape information is (m/d)².

Conclusion: JL distance preservation doesn't adequately quantify available geometric information for comparison or inference tasks. Formal bounds on feature recovery reveal much weaker geometry retention than distance preservation suggests, especially in high dimensions where distances concentrate.

Abstract: The Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) lemma guarantees that a random projection of $n$ points to
  $m=O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log n)$ dimensions preserves pairwise squared distances within relative
  error $\varepsilon$ with high probability, and this dimension order is asymptotically
  optimal. In high dimensions, however, distances concentrate around a baseline while key
  geometric information lies in much smaller fluctuations. We show that the JL bound can
  therefore be uninformative about retained geometry: an independent Gaussian replacement map
  can satisfy it even though the replacement cloud is independent of the original data.
  We then ask how well any decoder can recover a feature $f(D)$ of a squared distance $D$ from
  a linear sketch. Under squared-error loss, the optimal decoder is conditional expectation, so
  recovery defines a linear operator whose singular values quantify feature recovery. For
  isotropic Gaussian data ($Σ=σ^2 I_d$), we diagonalize this operator in closed form.
  For fixed $k$ with $m,d-m\to\infty$, its $k$th singular value satisfies $\ell_k\approx(m/
  d)^{k/2}$.
  This yields three sharp consequences. A rank-$m$ sketch retains at most an $m/d$ fraction of
  the variance of any feature of one squared distance. If $m\to\infty$ and $m/d\to0$, the
  expected Kendall correlation is $\frac{2}π\sqrt{m/d}(1+o(1))$; for fixed $q$, nearest-
  neighbor agreement tends to $1/q$. Yet one projection can satisfy the JL bound while mean
  Kendall correlation vanishes when $\log n\ll m\ll d$. After removing scale, Haar-averaged
  retained covariance-shape information is $(m/d)^2$. Thus JL distance preservation does not
  quantify the geometry available for comparison or inference.

</details>


### [102] [GeoSPRINT: Geometric Redundancy-Aware Step Pruning for Inference in Diffusion Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02160)
*Arpita Joshi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GeoSPRINT is a training-free method that uses trajectory geometry to create non-uniform sampling schedules for diffusion models, reducing inference steps while maintaining quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models have high sample quality but are expensive at inference due to many sequential neural function evaluations (NFEs). Existing acceleration methods either use fixed schedules, adapt based on local error, or require additional training.

Method: GeoSPRINT detects geometrically redundant steps using a hyperplanarity test in latent space via QR factorization. It converts redundancy profiles into sampling schedules allocating more steps to high-curvature regions. Also introduces trajectory projection score α_traj to quantify trajectory straightness.

Result: Consistent improvements over uniform DDIM schedules across CIFAR-10, LSUN Church, and Stable Diffusion v1.5. On CIFAR-10: 0.7-1.1 FID improvement across 49-89 NFEs, surpasses DPM-Solver++ at NFE≥30. On LSUN Church: reduces FID from 1.48 to 1.26 at 52 steps. On Stable Diffusion v1.5: up to 1.93 FID improvement over DDIM.

Conclusion: Trajectory geometry provides a useful global signal for allocating inference steps, and schedule quality can substantially improve diffusion sampling efficiency without retraining.

Abstract: Diffusion models achieve high sample quality but remain expensive at inference time because sampling requires many sequential neural function evaluations (NFEs). Existing acceleration methods either use fixed step-skipping schedules, adapt step sizes based on local numerical error, or require additional training. We introduce GeoSPRINT (Geometric Step Pruning for Inference in Trajectories), a training-free framework for constructing non-uniform sampling schedules from the geometry of denoising trajectories. GeoSPRINT detects geometrically redundant steps using a hyperplanarity test in latent space, implemented efficiently via QR factorization, and converts the resulting redundancy profile into a sampling schedule that allocates more steps to high-curvature regions of the trajectory. In addition, we introduce the trajectory projection score $α_{\mathrm{traj}}$, a residual-variance metric that quantifies trajectory straightness and serves as a model-free diagnostic for rectified flow quality. Across CIFAR-10 ($32{\times}32$), LSUN Church ($256{\times}256$), and Stable Diffusion v1.5 ($512{\times}512$ latent), GeoSPRINT consistently improves over uniform DDIM (Denoising Diffusion Implicit Models) schedules at matched NFE budgets. On CIFAR-10, GeoSPRINT improves FID (Fréchet Inception Distance) by 0.7-1.1 over DDIM across 49-89 NFEs and surpasses DPM-Solver++ at NFE${\geq}30$ despite using a first-order DDIM solver. On LSUN Church, it reduces FID from 1.48 to 1.26 at 52 steps, and on Stable Diffusion v1.5 it achieves up to 1.93 FID improvement over DDIM. These results show that trajectory geometry provides a useful global signal for allocating inference steps and that schedule quality can substantially improve diffusion sampling efficiency without retraining.

</details>


### [103] [DMRL: Document-Mediated Reinforcement Learning for Skill Optimization in Advertising Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02170)
*Wei Zhang,Hongji Li,Song Sun,Peng Yu,Xue Yang,Lei Zhao,Peng Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DMRL is a reinforcement learning framework for automated skill document evolution in advertising recommendation systems, using dual-level agents and novel optimization methods for credit assignment and long-term outcome prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-assisted skill optimization for advertising recommendation systems is prompt-driven and lacks principled mechanisms for attributing rewards to specific document edits, making the labor-intensive process inefficient and suboptimal.

Method: Document-Mediated Reinforcement Learning (DMRL) with upper-level agent performing structured document edits and frozen lower-level task agent evaluating effects via A/B testing, enhanced by Dual-Relative Policy Optimization (DRPO) for robust advantage estimation and Long-term Reward Predictor (LRP) using disentangled representation learning.

Result: Deployed on a large-scale short-video ads platform, DMRL outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across key advertising metrics, demonstrating practical effectiveness.

Conclusion: DMRL provides a principled framework for skill self-evolution in advertising recommendation systems, effectively addressing credit assignment and long-term outcome challenges while improving both commercial returns and user experience.

Abstract: Advertising recommendation requires continuously tuning complex system parameters while balancing commercial returns and user experience. Recent work has introduced large language models (LLMs) with skill documents to assist this labor-intensive process, but skill optimization remains largely prompt-driven, lacking a principled mechanism to attribute rewards to specific document edits. To address this limitation, we propose Document-Mediated Reinforcement Learning (DMRL), a skill self-evolution framework that models skill document optimization as a sequence of structured editing actions. In DMRL, an upper-level agent performs controlled document edits, while a frozen lower-level task agent evaluates their effects through A/B testing. To address credit assignment and long-term outcomes, we introduce two key components: (1) Dual-Relative Policy Optimization (DRPO), a post-training policy optimization method for robust and risk-aware advantage estimation; and (2) Long-term Reward Predictor (LRP), which estimates long-term outcomes by modeling population heterogeneity with disentangled representation learning and cross-attention transfer. DMRL was deployed on a large-scale short-video ads platform and extensive empirical evaluation shows that DMRL outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across key advertising metrics

</details>


### [104] [Learning the Constitutive Behavior of Materials via Neural Operators and Causal Attention: Case Studies in Plasticity and Damage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02194)
*Rishabh Arora,Lisa Scheunemann,Tim Brepols,Shahed Rezaei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Data-driven constitutive modeling using material operators as functional mappings from strain histories to stress responses, with causal attention mechanisms for path dependence and spectral convolutions for resolution invariance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional constitutive modeling requires postulating internal state variables that are often unmeasurable in experiments, forcing reliance on domain knowledge and calibration against experimental data without direct observation of material internal states.

Method: Proposes a material operator framework treating materials as function-to-function mappings from strain history to stress response. Uses causally masked attention for temporal path dependence while preserving parallelizability, spectral convolutions for frequency-domain discretization-invariant representations, and sinusoidal activations for nonlinear inelastic transitions.

Result: Demonstrates accurate and robust predictions of irreversible deformation mechanisms across multidimensional, rate-independent material models with complex phenomena like nonlinear plasticity and ductile damage accumulation, while achieving resolution invariance and excellent parallel efficiency.

Conclusion: The material operator framework provides an effective data-driven approach for constitutive modeling without requiring measurable internal variables, successfully capturing path dependence through causal attention mechanisms and handling complex inelastic behaviors while maintaining computational efficiency.

Abstract: Classical constitutive modeling of path-dependent inelastic materials relies on internal state variables whose evolution equations must be postulated based on domain knowledge and calibrated against experimental data. However, in many practical settings, the relevant internal variables are typically not measurable in experiments, and the constitutive response must be inferred entirely from measured strain-stress data without any prior knowledge of the material's internal state. We propose a data-driven constitutive modeling framework based on the concept of a material operator, which treats a deforming material as a functional mapping from its entire strain history to the corresponding stress response. In contrast to traditional autoregressive or recurrent formulations, the model is trained directly on full loading paths as function-to-function mappings, predicting complete stress trajectories in a single parallel forward pass. Temporal path dependence is enforced through a causally masked attention mechanism embedded within the operator, which restricts the model's attention to past material states while preserving computational parallelizability. Spectral convolutions provide discretization-invariant representations in the frequency domain, while causal attention captures highly adaptive, non-local history dependence. Furthermore, sinusoidal activation functions are used to resolve the strong nonlinear transitions inherent in inelastic regimes. The framework is evaluated across multidimensional, rate-independent material models exhibiting complex phenomena, with an emphasis on nonlinear plasticity and ductile damage accumulation. The results demonstrate accurate and robust predictions of irreversible deformation mechanisms while simultaneously achieving resolution invariance and excellent parallel efficiency.

</details>


### [105] [SMart: A Multi-source Multi-phase Time Series Representation Transfer Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02203)
*Fang He,Wang-chien Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SMart is a novel multi-source multi-phase time series representation learning framework that improves on existing approaches by using multi-phase recurrence plots recovery tasks and intelligent source dataset selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current TSRL approaches have limitations: transformer-based methods use simple self-supervised recovery tasks, while cross-dataset methods typically use only single source datasets. The authors aim to enhance both aspects for better representation learning.

Method: Proposes SMart framework with two novel mechanisms: 1) multi-phase recurrence plots recovery task in three alternative modes to capture time series dynamics, and 2) a source dataset selector to choose multiple suitable source datasets for pre-training the TSRL encoder.

Result: SMart outperforms state-of-the-art models for time series representation learning, classification and regression on both uni-variate and multi-variate datasets. Achieves up to 19.5% reduction in MAE for regression and up to 1.34% increase in average accuracy for classification.

Conclusion: The proposed SMart framework effectively addresses limitations of existing TSRL approaches by combining enhanced self-supervision with multi-source dataset utilization, demonstrating significant performance improvements across various time series tasks.

Abstract: Time series representation learning (TSRL) has attracted growing research interests in recent years. Two recent explorations in TSRL are: i) exploiting a transformer-based framework to learn time series; ii) instead of using only the targeted dataset, borrowing time series from other datasets to to facilitate representation transfer. While these two explorations are shown effective, the self-supervised time series recovery task in (i) and the single-source dataset used in (ii) are technically simple and thus can be enhanced with new ideas. In this work, we propose a new TSRL framework, namely multi-source multi-phase time series representation transfer (SMart), which has two novel mechanisms to address the aforementioned deficiencies: 1) a multi-phase recurrence plots recovery task, in three alternative modes, for guiding the encoder to embed time series dynamics into the time series representation; and 2) a source dataset selector to select multiple suitable source datasets to supplement the original target dataset for pre-training the TSRL encoder. Experimental results show that SMart outperforms several state-of-the-art models for time series representation learning, classification and regression on both uni-variate and multi-variate time series datasets, reducing mean absolute error up to 19.5% for time series regression, and increasing average accuracy up to 1.34\% for time series classification.

</details>


### [106] [Recursive Value Learning for Long-Horizon Offline Goal-Conditioned RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02237)
*Hyeonseong Jeon,Youngwoon Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DCRL is an offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning method that uses balanced binary trees to decompose trajectories, reducing bootstrap depth from linear to logarithmic for better long-horizon value learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two main challenges in scaling offline GCRL to long-horizon tasks: (1) long-range value learning depends on inaccurate shorter-range estimates, and (2) max-based value backups amplify overestimation through repeated propagation.

Method: DCRL recursively decomposes trajectory segments into balanced binary trees and trains values from leaves to root. Each parent updates only after children using exact factorization of observed routes, then jointly propagates values across trajectories to discover shorter routes.

Result: DCRL reduces worst-case bootstrap depth from linear to logarithmic, slowing error accumulation. It substantially outperforms prior flat offline GCRL methods and achieves best average score of 64 (vs prior 55) on five most challenging OGBench tasks.

Conclusion: The balanced binary tree decomposition approach effectively addresses error propagation issues in long-horizon offline GCRL, outperforming both flat and hierarchical baselines on challenging tasks.

Abstract: Scaling offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning (GCRL) to long-horizon tasks is difficult because (1) long-range value learning depends on shorter-range estimates that may still be inaccurate, and (2) max-based value backups can amplify overestimation through repeated propagation. We propose DCRL (Divide-and-Conquer RL), which recursively decomposes each trajectory segment into a balanced binary tree and trains the values from leaves to root. Each parent is therefore updated only after its children, using an exact factorization of the observed route rather than selecting among noisy alternatives. Since this objective learns values along demonstrated routes that are not necessarily optimal, DCRL jointly propagates values across trajectories to discover shorter routes. Thanks to the balanced binary tree, DCRL reduces worst-case bootstrap depth from linear to logarithmic, and this shorter dependency structure empirically corresponds to much slower error accumulation. Across diverse goal-reaching tasks, DCRL substantially outperforms prior flat offline GCRL methods, and on the five most challenging long-horizon OGBench tasks, it improves the best prior average score from 55 to 64, surpassing all flat and hierarchical baselines.

</details>


### [107] [Similarity-Aware Personalized Federated Learning in Heterogeneous Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02241)
*Arun Kumar A,Sunil Gupta,Dang Ngyuen,Bao Duong,Dat Phan Trong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAPE-FL is a personalized federated learning framework that anchors each client's model to both the global model and a similarity-weighted peer model, using dynamic regularization to balance global knowledge transfer with peer collaboration while filtering out dissimilar clients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In federated learning with distribution mismatch across clients, some clients perform better with their local models alone than with the global model, nullifying the benefits of collaboration. The problem is poor global generalization and degraded local performance in heterogeneous settings.

Method: Proposes SAPE-FL (Similarity-Aware Personalized Federated Learning) - a personalization framework that anchors each client's model to both the global model and a similarity-weighted peer averaged model. Uses dynamic, client-specific regularization based on model similarity and output similarity to adaptively balance global knowledge transfer and peer collaboration while filtering out dissimilar clients.

Result: SAPE-FL outperforms state-of-the-art methods under high statistical heterogeneity and low client data regimes. The approach theoretically establishes convergence guarantees and empirically shows superior performance.

Conclusion: The SAPE-FL framework effectively addresses the challenge of heterogeneous data distributions in federated learning by combining global model knowledge with similarity-weighted peer collaboration, mitigating negative transfer and enhancing robustness in challenging scenarios.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) allows decentralized clients to train models collaboratively while preserving data privacy. However, distribution mismatch across clients often leads to poor global generalization and degraded local client-level performance. In such scenarios, some of the clients with their local models trained solely on local data may perform better than the globally learnt model, thus nullifying the benefits of collaborative federated learning. To address this, we propose SAPE-FL (Similarity-Aware Personalized Federated Learning), a novel personalization framework that anchors each client's model to both the global model and a similarity-weighted peer averaged model. By incorporating dynamic, client-specific regularization based on both model similarity and output similarity, SAPE-FL adaptively balances global knowledge transfer and peer collaboration while filtering out dissimilar clients. This dual anchoring mitigates negative transfer and enhances robustness in heterogeneous settings. We theoretically analyze our algorithm establishing its convergence guarantees and empirically show that SAPE-FL outperforms state-of-the-art methods under high statistical heterogeneity and low client data regimes.

</details>


### [108] [CAPTURE: Disentangling Preference Drift from Memory Poisoning in Personalized LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02265)
*S M Asif Hossain,Ruksat Khan Shayoni,Md Kishor Morol*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAPTURE: A neural differential-equation belief tracker with multi-timescale memory ledger and uncertainty-triggered clarification that improves personalization while defending against memory poisoning attacks in language agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalized language agents that use persistent memory to adapt to users create security vulnerabilities. When new information conflicts with stored preferences, agents must distinguish genuine preference drift from temporary context shifts or adversarial memory poisoning. Current rules based only on recency and provenance are insufficient.

Method: CAPTURE uses: 1) Neural differential-equation belief tracker over latent user state, 2) Multi-timescale memory ledger, 3) Uncertainty-triggered clarification, and 4) Counterfactual auditing of cited memories. The approach formulates the problem as a continuous-time partially observable decision process.

Result: On 480 held-out episodes from 96 users: 71.5% win rate vs 69.3% for supervised baseline and 66.1% for heuristic baseline. Limits fixed-policy poisoning success to 11.5% while accepting 83.5% of genuine preference updates. Adaptive attacker success rises to 24.7%. Zero-shot evaluation on independent benchmark and replay of 40 users' longitudinal histories over 2-3 weeks.

Conclusion: Modeling preference authenticity explicitly can improve both personalization and robustness in memory-augmented LLM agents, but exposes a real adaptation-security tradeoff that adaptive attackers can exploit.

Abstract: Personalized language agents use persistent memory to adapt to users over time, but the same mechanism creates an attack surface. When new information conflicts with stored preferences, an agent must distinguish genuine preference drift from temporary context shifts, ambiguity, or adversarial memory poisoning. We formulate this problem as a continuous-time partially observable decision process over a latent user state and show why rules based only on recency and provenance are insufficient. CAPTURE addresses this ambiguity with a neural differential-equation belief tracker, a multi-timescale memory ledger, uncertainty-triggered clarification, and counterfactual auditing of cited memories. On 480 held-out episodes from 96 users, CAPTURE achieves a 71.5% win rate, compared with 69.3% for an identically supervised baseline and 66.1% for the strongest heuristic baseline. It limits fixed-policy poisoning success to 11.5% while accepting 83.5% of genuine preference updates. Under an adaptive attacker with access to the released weights, attack success rises to 24.7%, exposing a real adaptation-security tradeoff. We further evaluate the frozen system zero-shot on an independently constructed benchmark and replay longitudinal interaction histories from 40 users collected over two to three weeks. These results suggest that modeling preference authenticity explicitly can improve both personalization and robustness in memory-augmented LLM agents.

</details>


### [109] [Entangled Representations Amplify Collateral Damage in Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02285)
*Evžen Wybitul,Tim G. J. Rudner,Christian Schroeder de Witt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: More disentangled neural representations enable better machine unlearning with less collateral damage to retained knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To empirically test the long-standing but untested intuition in interpretability research that representational entanglement (sharing structure between knowledge domains) makes unlearning harder.

Method: Train six 254M-parameter language models on English Wikipedia with graded levels of disentanglement between biology and non-biology knowledge using repurposed Selective Gradient Masking (SGTM). Apply three standard unlearning methods to each model to measure retain-forget trade-offs.

Result: More disentangled models consistently achieve better retain-forget trade-offs: at fixed forgetting levels, the most disentangled models incur 4× lower retain cost under two methods and 1.3× lower under the third method.

Conclusion: Representational entanglement is indeed a cause of collateral damage in unlearning, confirming long-suspected interpretability intuitions. The methodology can be extended to test other structural claims from interpretability research.

Abstract: A long-held intuition in interpretability research is that representational entanglement, the sharing of structure between knowledge domains in a neural network, makes unlearning harder. While the intuition is widespread, it has never been directly tested in a controlled experiment. We present a way to do so: by repurposing Selective Gradient Masking (SGTM), we train a suite of six 254M-parameter language models on English Wikipedia with graded levels of disentanglement between biology and non-biology knowledge. Applying three standard unlearning methods to every model in the suite, we find that more disentangled models consistently achieve better retain-forget trade-offs: at a fixed level of forgetting, the most disentangled models incur roughly $4\times$ lower retain cost under two of the three methods, and $1.3\times$ lower under the third. Because our intervention changes only the model, not the data or the unlearning algorithm, this is direct evidence that representational entanglement is one of the causes of collateral damage in unlearning, as interpretability researchers have long suspected. A similar design could be used to test other structural claims from interpretability.

</details>


### [110] [SEAL: Reinforcing Global Safety in Mixture-of-Experts through Shared Expert ALignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02293)
*Qingyu Meng,Yiwei Zha,Jiahuan Pei,Koen Hindriks,Herbert Bos,Min Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SEAL and SEAL++ are training-time defenses that enhance MoE model safety by creating plug-and-play adapters for shared experts, providing router-independent safety anchors against adversarial attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models are vulnerable to adversarial attacks that manipulate sparse routing to bypass safety mechanisms. Existing defenses focused on hardening routers remain insufficient due to routing's nondeterministic nature, creating a critical security gap in state-of-the-art MoE models.

Method: The authors propose SEAL (Shared Expert-based Adversarial defense) - a training-time parameter-efficient defense that creates plug-and-play adapters attached to shared experts. SEAL++ adds an orthogonal constraint to preserve pre-existing safety subspaces during training, enhancing safety without disrupting original capabilities.

Result: SEAL reduces attack success rate by up to 60% across six attack scenarios combining harmful prompting, jailbreak, and malicious fine-tuning with/without neuron pruning, while maintaining model capability with at most 1.4% degradation on five benchmark average.

Conclusion: Shared experts can serve as router-independent safety anchors in MoE models. The proposed SEAL defenses effectively enhance global safety alignment through parameter-efficient adaptations of shared experts, providing robust protection against diverse adversarial threats while preserving model capabilities.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) is a scaling architecture for large language models that activates only a small subset of expert modules per token, enabling massive parameter growth with nearly constant computation. Recent Hybrid MoE architecture adds \textit{shared experts} to capture consistently useful representations, further improving stability and generalization. MoE now powers many flagship open-source and commercial models, yet remains vulnerable to adversarial attacks. Specifically, sparse routing introduces a structural vulnerability: MoE safety hinges on which experts are activated, and adversaries can subvert this selection through jailbreak prompts, malicious fine-tuning, and weight-level pruning of safety-critical neurons. Existing defenses primarily focus on hardening the router, but an adversary may still manipulate or bypass the routing trajectory due to the routing process's nondeterministic nature, thereby collapsing the defense. To cope with this problem, we first identify theoretically and empirically that shared expert, an always-activated component containing a small proportion of safety-critical neurons, can overcome the uncertainty of sparsely activated routing path and serve as a router-independent anchor to enhance global safety alignment. Based on this insight, we propose SEAL, a training-time parameter-efficient defense that produces a plug-and-play adapter attached to shared expert, and SEAL++, a variant that adds an orthogonal constraint preserving pre-existing safety subspaces during training. We evaluate SEAL and SEAL++ across six attack scenarios that combine three adversarial inputs (harmful prompting, jailbreak, malicious fine-tuning) with and without neuron pruning. SEAL reduces attack success rate (ASR) by up to 60\%, at a capability cost of at most 1.4\% on a five-benchmark average. Additionally, SEAL can seamlessly integrate with router-level ......

</details>


### [111] [Bayes-Optimal BER and AUC: Estimation and Evaluation of Estimators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02304)
*Ryota Ushio,Takashi Ishida,Masashi Sugiyama*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces methods to estimate optimal balanced error rate (BER) and AUC from soft labels, extending beyond traditional accuracy estimation, with a novel evaluation framework to validate these estimators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accuracy is insufficient for imbalanced or noisy classification tasks where BER and AUC are more appropriate metrics. There's a need to estimate the optimal achievable performance for these metrics to understand irreducible error and model improvement potential.

Method: Proposes soft-label-based estimators for optimal BER and AUC in both clean (known soft labels and class prior) and realistic settings (unknown prior, corrupted soft labels). Uses isotonic regression with auxiliary hard labels to recover clean soft labels, estimates class prior via clipped mean, and derives finite-sample error bounds for plug-in estimators. Also extends FeeBee framework for practical evaluation without knowing true optimum.

Result: The proposed estimators effectively estimate optimal BER and AUC in various settings. The extended FeeBee evaluation framework successfully validates estimators without requiring knowledge of true optimum. Experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of both estimation and evaluation methods.

Conclusion: The paper provides practical tools for estimating optimal BER and AUC from soft labels and evaluating such estimators, addressing important gaps in performance estimation for imbalanced and noisy classification problems beyond traditional accuracy metrics.

Abstract: A fundamental quantity in machine learning is the optimal performance achievable by any model on a given task. Estimating this quantity allows us to distinguish the irreducible part of the error from a deficiency of the model, telling us how much room for improvement remains. Recent work has shown that the Bayes error, or equivalently the optimal accuracy, can be estimated from soft labels in binary classification. However, accuracy is often a poor summary of performance in settings with severe class imbalance or noisy annotations, where metrics such as the balanced error rate (BER) and the area under the ROC curve (AUC) are more appropriate. We address this gap with two complementary contributions. (i) Estimation. We propose soft-label-based estimators for the optimal BER and AUC. We first consider the clean setting in which true soft labels and the class prior are known, and then extend the estimators to a more realistic setting in which the class prior is unknown and the observed soft labels are corrupted by an unknown order-preserving transformation, possibly followed by additive noise. In the latter setting, we approximately recover the clean soft labels via isotonic regression with auxiliary hard labels, estimate the class prior with a clipped mean of the hard labels, and derive finite-sample error bounds for the resulting plug-in estimators. (ii) Evaluation. Since the optimum is unobservable on real datasets, evaluating any such estimator is itself nontrivial. We extend the FeeBee framework, originally proposed for evaluating Bayes-error estimators, to the optimal BER and AUC. The resulting procedure provides practical evaluation scores without requiring knowledge of the optimum, and applies to any estimator of the optimal BER or AUC, not only our proposed ones. Experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets validate both the estimators and the evaluation procedure.

</details>


### [112] [What Is Worth Representing? Representational Empowerment for Continual Model Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02322)
*Fei Dai,Hanqi Zhou,Alison Gopnik,Charley Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Representational Empowerment (RepEmp) guides agents to build and reuse representations by scoring how much they expand future modeling and planning capacity, outperforming information-gain approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The fundamental challenge in modeling is deciding what to represent at all, not just estimating parameters or causal structures. Agents need to continually construct models across environments while curating reusable representations under resource constraints.

Method: Proposed Representational Empowerment (RepEmp) that scores candidate representational elements by their capacity to expand future modeling/planning abilities. Implemented as hierarchical Curator-Actor architecture. Tested in three experiments: 1) human causal-learning task comparing abstraction granularities, 2) matched simulations assessing structure recovery and transfer, 3) LLM-augmented Curator in open-vocabulary planning domain.

Result: Human participants favored abstraction levels maximizing goal reachability rather than world fidelity, predicted better by RepEmp than information-gain. Simulations showed RepEmp contributes more to structure recovery and cross-task transfer than exploration alone. LLM-augmented Curator built more compact symbolic libraries with better generalization than baselines, with RepEmp ablation eliminating benefits.

Conclusion: RepEmp is a key principle for continual model construction, guiding what to build, retain, and reuse under bounded resources. It complements classic empowerment by focusing on control over internal representations rather than external states.

Abstract: The first problem of modeling the world is not just estimating the right parameters or causal structure, but deciding what should be represented at all. We frame this problem as continual model construction: an agent maintains an environment-specific model M of an inaccessible world W and curates a persistent library L of reusable representational elements across environments. We propose Representational Empowerment (RepEmp) to score candidate elements by how much they expand the agent's future capacity to model and plan, complementing the classic definition of empowerment, but redefined as control over internal representations instead of external states. We realize the framework as a hierarchical Curator-Actor architecture and test it across three experiments. In a closed-vocabulary causal-learning task, human participants construct causal models at varying abstraction granularities to maximize goal reachability rather than fidelity to the world, a signature better predicted by RepEmp than by information-gain alternatives. Matched simulations reveal that RepEmp-guided construction contributes more than exploration to sufficient structure recovery and cross-task transfer. Finally, in an open-vocabulary planning domain, an LLM-augmented Curator builds more compact symbolic libraries, which also generalize better than baselines. Ablating RepEmp eliminates these benefits. Together, these results identify RepEmp as a key principle for continual model construction: deciding what to build, retain, and reuse under bounded resources.

</details>


### [113] [AGI Maze Prediction Datasets: A Compact Benchmark for Learning World Dynamics with Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02339)
*Alexey Potapov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces AGI Maze Prediction Datasets and Benchmark - a lightweight testbed for evaluating predictive world modeling in Transformers, comparing different memory architectures and finding that structured, task-aligned working memory outperforms unstructured latent capacity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the need for effective world modeling in AI systems, particularly the challenge of maintaining and updating internal states to reason about action consequences. It aims to provide a controlled testbed for studying this capability in predictive models like Transformers, moving beyond mere memorization to learning transferable action-conditioned dynamics.

Method: The authors create the AGI Maze Prediction benchmark from procedurally generated, stateful grid worlds with three prediction tasks: per-step transition prediction, fixed-horizon state prediction, and sequential textual-observation prediction. They use source-maze-disjoint training/validation splits and greedy exact-match evaluation to ensure models learn transferable dynamics rather than memorizing layouts. They test byte-level Transformers, auxiliary latent-memory Transformers, and a novel pseudo-video spatial-memory Transformer that initializes a 2D latent workspace from input maps and updates it from action history.

Result: The pseudo-video spatial-memory Transformer achieves perfect validation accuracy on selected fixed-horizon tasks where byte and unstructured-memory baselines fail, and substantially improves sequential text-trace prediction. Generic auxiliary latent-memory Transformers can fit training data perfectly but don't consistently improve held-out performance. Structured working memory aligned with task requirements proves more effective than additional latent capacity alone.

Conclusion: Structured, task-aligned working memory is more valuable than additional unstructured latent capacity for world modeling tasks. Language grounding may be mediated by persistent data structures and computations over them, and the benchmark provides a compact testbed for architectures coupling textual interfaces to learned structured state.

Abstract: World modeling requires a predictive model to maintain and update an internal state adequate for reasoning about the consequences of actions. We introduce the AGI Maze Prediction Datasets and Benchmark, a lightweight controlled testbed for studying this capability in Transformers and other predictive models. Derived from procedurally generated, stateful grid worlds, the benchmark comprises per-step transition prediction, fixed-horizon state prediction, and sequential textual-observation prediction. Source-maze-disjoint training and validation splits, together with greedy exact-match evaluation, distinguish learning transferable action-conditioned dynamics from memorizing transitions in familiar layouts. We establish from-scratch byte-level Transformer baselines and compare them with two working-memory-augmented architectures. A generic auxiliary latent-memory Transformer can fit some training sets perfectly but does not consistently improve held-out performance. In contrast, a pseudo-video spatial-memory Transformer initializes a two-dimensional latent workspace from the input map and updates it from action history without receiving intermediate maps, positions, or state labels. Under the same data, objectives, and evaluation protocol, this model reaches perfect validation accuracy on selected fixed-horizon tasks where the byte and unstructured-memory baselines do not, and substantially improves sequential text-trace prediction. These results suggest that structured, task-aligned working memory can be more useful than additional latent capacity alone. More broadly, we argue that language grounding is mediated by persistent data structures and computations over them; the benchmark offers a compact setting for testing architectures that couple textual interfaces to learned structured state.

</details>


### [114] [Percolation Dynamics in Optimization : Variance Cascades and Discrete Scale Invariance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02373)
*Sai Niranjan Ramachandran,Suvrit Sra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SGD dynamics in neural networks follow a percolation process where architectural symmetries cause subnetworks to merge in blocks, creating variance spikes resembling phase transitions; this mechanism extends to Adam/AdamW with heavy-tailed noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how SGD steers deep neural networks toward invariant sets/simpler subnetworks over time, as this temporal dynamics remains poorly understood despite known steering behavior.

Method: Model SGD's stochastic gradient flow as a percolation process, where architectural symmetries force subnetworks to merge in discrete simultaneous blocks rather than sequentially, tracking variance spikes in macroscopic order parameters.

Result: SGD dynamics exhibit structural transitions marked by variance spikes that echo physical phase transitions; this trapping mechanism and scaling cascade extend to Adam and AdamW under explicit heavy-tailed noise models.

Conclusion: Neural network training dynamics can be understood through percolation theory and phase transition analogies, providing a unified framework for understanding optimization dynamics across different optimizers under heavy-tailed noise conditions.

Abstract: We study the dynamics of Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD), which is known to steer deep neural networks toward invariant sets that correspond to simpler subnetworks. How this steering unfolds over time remains poorly understood. We answer this by modeling the stochastic gradient flow (SGF) as a percolation process, in which architectural symmetries force subnetworks to merge in discrete simultaneous blocks rather than one at a time. These structural transitions register as variance spikes in a macroscopic order parameter, echoing physical phase transitions. We further show this trapping mechanism and its associated scaling cascade extend to Adam and AdamW under an explicit heavy-tailed noise model.

</details>


### [115] [Evidence for Shared Routing Geometry and Dynamics in Sparse Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02404)
*Kirill Labzin,Stepan Kulibaba,Artem Dzhalilov,Artem Gorokhov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Routing decisions in sparse mixture-of-experts models follow predictable shared dynamics across layers, which can be captured by aligning router control subspaces into a canonical representation that preserves expert choice information better than generic hidden states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work showed routing decisions across different layers can be predicted from earlier signals, suggesting routing is not independent across layers, but the underlying structure behind this predictability was unclear and needed investigation.

Method: The authors isolated the control subspace of each router and aligned these spaces into a shared canonical representation using generalized orthogonal Procrustes analysis. They then compared routing-specific states against generic residual representations using matched-rank comparisons, and tested whether predicted canonical states remain meaningful when substituted for native routing states.

Result: After alignment, a single linear transition achieved R²=0.39-0.71 and retained 79-90% of predictive power of layer-specific dynamics. Router-control states preserve expert choices much more faithfully than generic hidden states. Transported states preserved local routing behavior, with learned state evolution reducing ΔNLL by 15.7% on OLMoE and 6.2% over 10-router horizon on Phi compared to simple persistence.

Conclusion: Routing-relevant states across layers share a common geometric structure obscured by layer-specific coordinate systems, revealing reusable routing dynamics across depth that are separate from generic hidden state evolution, enabling meaningful prediction and substitution of canonical routing states.

Abstract: Sparse mixture-of-experts (MoE) models use an independently parameterized router at each sparse layer to select experts for every token. Prior work has shown that routing decisions across depth can often be predicted from earlier routing signals, suggesting that routing is not fully independent across layers. However, the structure behind this predictability remains unclear. In this work, we provide evidence that routing-relevant states across layers share a common geometric structure that is obscured by layer-specific coordinate systems. We isolate the control subspace of each router and align these spaces into a shared canonical representation using generalized orthogonal Procrustes analysis. After alignment, a single linear transition reaches $R^2=0.39$--$0.71$ and retains 79--90\% of the predictive power of separately fitted layer-specific dynamics, indicating that much of routing-state evolution follows a reusable process across depth. We then ask whether this shared dynamics is specific to routing or simply reflects the smooth evolution of hidden representations. A matched-rank comparison shows that residual representations are often easier to predict across layers, while router-control states preserve the model's expert choices much more faithfully. This separates generic cross-layer predictability from routing-specific information. Finally, we test whether the predicted canonical states remain meaningful when used in place of native routing states. The transported states preserve local routing behavior, while learned state evolution reduces $Δ\mathrm{NLL}$ relative to simple persistence by 15.7\% on OLMoE and 6.2\% over a 10-router horizon on Phi.

</details>


### [116] [Coverage, Not Targeting: A Structural Regime in Multi-Turn Agent Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02417)
*Chenyu Zhou,Qiliang Jiang,Shuning Wu,Xu Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multi-turn RL targeting methods for credit assignment are ineffective in low-verifier-information-density regimes where terminal-state verification collapses observable signals, making uniform reward redistribution the optimal default.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper challenges the current approach of treating credit assignment as a targeting problem in multi-turn agentic RL, where per-turn methods localize credit onto important turns given terminal rewards. The authors identify that this approach fails when verifier information density (fraction of causal chain exposed per turn) is low, which occurs with terminal-state verification.

Method: Introduced the concept of verifier information density (V_d = k/C). Conducted controlled experiments on tau^2-bench that separate reward density from credit geometry, compared continuous dense reward spread uniformly vs sparse binary outcomes. Used synthetic phase boundary analysis and measured V_d on different benchmarks. Performed extensive replication across model families and pre-registered experiments including matched-concentration shuffled controls.

Result: Uniform dense reward distribution consistently outperforms sparse targeting methods in low-V_d regimes. Terminal-state verification collapses observable signals to single final-write turns (k=1 in 98% rollouts) while success requires 5-8 step prerequisite chains. The crossover point is at V_d* ~ 0.8, while measured V_d is ~0.15 on tau^2-bench and ~0.4 on BFCL V3. Uniform redistribution wins across all tested conditions, and targeting methods only become beneficial near full chain coverage.

Conclusion: Uniform redistribution is the zero-information coverage default that per-turn targeting schemes must beat. The paper establishes verifier information density as the structural quantity predicting when targeting is appropriate, and introduces the matched-concentration shuffled control as a necessary validation for any targeting claim.

Abstract: Multi-turn agentic RL increasingly treats credit assignment as a targeting problem: given a terminal verifiable reward, per-turn methods localize credit onto the turns that mattered. We identify the structural quantity that predicts when this is the right move, the verifier information density V_d = k/C (the fraction of an agent's C-step causal chain whose per-turn correctness the verifier exposes), and show that terminal-state verifiers sit deep in a low-V_d regime where targeting is the wrong axis. In controlled shared-rollout comparisons on tau^2-bench that separate reward density from credit geometry, a continuous dense reward spread uniformly beats the sparse binary outcome reward (net-harmful on 4/5 seeds), while concentrating the same advantage on progress turns or on random turns is equally harmful: targeting is second-order. The mechanism is coverage: terminal-state verification collapses the observable signal to a single final-write turn (k=1 in 98% of rollouts) while success requires a 5-8 step chain of prerequisite tool calls. A synthetic phase boundary places the crossover at V_d* ~ 0.8, whereas measured V_d is ~0.15 on tau^2-bench and ~0.4 on BFCL V3; uniform also wins on BFCL, where a matched-concentration shuffled control is negative on 8/8 seeds. The effect reproduces across model families on ToolACE-2-8B (Delta = -0.048 over 32 pre-registered seeds; an independent 20-seed replication is itself significant), and a pre-registered matched-budget breadth sweep traces a monotone dose-response whose deficit vanishes only at full chain coverage, with a reward-to-go arm reaching full-coverage parity. Uniform redistribution is the zero-information coverage default that per-turn schemes must beat; we contribute the matched-concentration shuffled control that any targeting claim should clear.

</details>


### [117] [IFW-BLS: Dual-Robust Broad Learning System with Intuitionistic Fuzzy Wave Loss](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02422)
*Mushir Akhtar,M. Tanveer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IFW-BLS enhances Broad Learning System robustness by combining wave loss (for residual-level protection) with intuitionistic fuzzy credibility weights (for sample-level reliability), optimized using Nesterov accelerated gradient instead of matrix inversion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard BLS training has two vulnerabilities: (1) large residuals from noise/outliers/corrupted labels dominate the objective, and (2) all samples are treated equally reliable even when some lie in ambiguous or locally conflicting regions.

Method: Proposes IFW-BLS with two robustness mechanisms: 1) Residual-level protection using bounded, smooth, asymmetric wave loss; 2) Sample-level credibility control using intuitionistic fuzzy scores combining global class-center consistency with local neighborhood conflict. Uses Nesterov accelerated gradient optimization instead of matrix inversion.

Result: Experiments on UCI benchmark datasets show IFW-BLS superiority over baseline models; additional corruption experiments demonstrate more stable performance than BLS under noise and outlier contamination.

Conclusion: IFW-BLS effectively addresses two sources of fragility in BLS through integrated residual and sample-level robustness mechanisms, providing superior and more stable performance especially in noisy environments.

Abstract: Broad Learning System is an efficient randomized learning model that expands network width through feature and enhancement nodes and estimates the output weights without deep backpropagation. Its standard least-squares training, however, is vulnerable in two different ways: (i) large residuals caused by noise, outliers, or corrupted labels can dominate the objective, and (ii) all samples are treated as equally reliable even when some lie in ambiguous or locally conflicting regions. This paper proposes IFW-BLS, an Intuitionistic Fuzzy Wave Broad Learning System that addresses these two sources of fragility within one optimization model. The first robustness mechanism is residual-level protection, obtained by replacing the squared loss with the bounded, smooth, and asymmetric wave loss. Boundedness prevents extreme residuals from receiving unbounded influence, while asymmetry allows positive and negative deviations to be penalized differently when the dominant error direction varies. The second mechanism is sample-level credibility control, obtained through intuitionistic fuzzy scores that combine global class-center consistency with local neighborhood conflict. The resulting model evaluates the wave loss on credibility-weighted residuals, so unreliable samples are down-weighted before the bounded loss further limits the effect of extreme errors. A Nesterov accelerated gradient based optimizer is used to solve the proposed objective, avoiding the explicit matrix inversion used in conventional BLS. Experiments on UCI benchmark datasets validate the superiority of the proposed IFW-BLS model over the baseline models; additional corruption experiments also show more stable performance than BLS under noise and outlier contamination.

</details>


### [118] [Towards One-for-All Robustness Across a Continuum of Threat Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02440)
*Zhichao Hou,Xiaorui Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TCN: Threat Conditional Network - a single model that achieves strong robustness across a continuum of threat levels by decomposing representation learning into threat-invariant backbone and threat-conditional adaptor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current adversarially robust models overfit to specific attack budgets, requiring multiple specialized models for different threat levels. This becomes intractable as threat spaces grow, creating a need for a single model that can adapt to diverse adversarial environments.

Method: Proposes TCN based on representation factorization: decomposes representation learning into threat-invariant shared backbone and lightweight threat-conditional adaptor. Uses Fourier-based embeddings and channel-wise affine modulation to condition on perturbation levels, trained against a distribution over perturbation budgets.

Result: TCN matches/surpasses full ensemble of budget-specialized models with single parameter set, generalizes to unseen perturbation budgets, transfers robustly under mismatched threat conditions, with only 4.6% parameter overhead on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny-ImageNet.

Conclusion: TCN provides adaptive and generalizable robustness across diverse threat levels with minimal overhead, offering promising direction for handling dynamic adversarial environments efficiently.

Abstract: Adversarially robust models often overfit to a specific attack budget, necessitating multiple specialized models for diverse and dynamic adversarial environments, a strategy that becomes fundamentally intractable as the threat space grows. This raises an open challenge: can we achieve strong robustness across a continuum of threat levels within a single model? We propose the Threat Conditional Network (TCN), grounded in a representation factorization framework that decomposes representation learning into a threat-invariant shared backbone and a lightweight threat-conditional adaptor. TCN conditions a single model on the perturbation level via Fourier-based embeddings and channel-wise affine modulation, and is trained against a distribution over perturbation budgets, enabling flexible and seamless adaptation across an infinite continuum of threat levels during inference. Extensive experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny-ImageNet show that TCN matches or surpasses a full ensemble of budget-specialized models with a single set of parameters, generalizes to unseen perturbation budgets, and transfers robustly under mismatched threat conditions, with only 4.6\% parameter overhead. These contributions chart a promising path toward adaptive and generalizable robustness in dynamic and diverse threat environments.

</details>


### [119] [CACTUS: Mask-Guided Semantic Clean-Label Backdoors in Decentralized Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02450)
*Chao Feng,Burkhard Stiller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CACTUS is a backdoor attack method for decentralized federated learning that uses semantic triggers and counterfactual embedding shifts to propagate backdoors through repeated peer aggregation across various data modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation addresses challenges in backdoor attacks for decentralized federated learning (DFL): semantic triggers are less conspicuous than synthetic patches, sample-dependent trigger placement weakens backdoor implantation across aggregation rounds, and DFL's topology-dependent peer aggregation repeatedly mixes local models, making backdoor propagation difficult.

Method: CACTUS converts label-consistent semantic pairs into target-directed representation shifts. It uses mask-guided, modality-specific operators to: 1) isolate trigger effects, 2) couple them across samples, and 3) apply the shifts counterfactually to clean non-target embeddings before peer aggregation. This enables backdoor propagation through the DFL system.

Result: Experiments covered speech, text, tabular, and image tasks under nine aggregation rules. With 30% malicious nodes, CACTUS achieved a nine-rule mean attack success rate (ASR) of 51.2% on Speech Commands and achieved the highest nine-rule mean ASR among evaluated attacks on three of four modalities. Sensitivity analyses showed ASR varies with network topology and increases with malicious-node ratio.

Conclusion: CACTUS can effectively propagate backdoors through repeated DFL aggregation by using semantic triggers and counterfactual embedding manipulation, demonstrating vulnerability in decentralized federated learning systems across multiple data modalities and aggregation rules.

Abstract: Semantic triggers in federated learning (FL) can be less conspicuous than synthetic patches, but sample-dependent placement may weaken backdoor implantation across aggregation rounds. This challenge is compounded in decentralized FL (DFL), where topology-dependent peer aggregation repeatedly mixes local models. CACTUS converts label-consistent semantic pairs into target-directed representation shifts. Mask-guided, modality-specific operators isolate trigger effects, couple them across samples, and apply the shifts counterfactually to clean non-target embeddings before peer aggregation. Experiments cover speech, text, tabular, and image tasks under nine aggregation rules. With 30\% malicious nodes, CACTUS reaches a nine-rule mean attack success rate (ASR) of 51.2\% on Speech Commands and the highest nine-rule mean ASR among evaluated attacks on three of four modalities. Sensitivity analyses show that ASR varies with network topology and increases with the malicious-node ratio. These results indicate that CACTUS can propagate backdoors through repeated DFL aggregation.

</details>


### [120] [Scalable Kronecker-Fisher Approximation: Efficient Hessian Analysis for Billion-Parameter Language Models Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02451)
*Viacheslav Yusupov,Daria Cherniuk,Evgeny Frolov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Kronecker-based approximation for Hessian analysis in billion-parameter networks, identifies value projection layers as most vulnerable, and demonstrates correlation with performance degradation/recovery for compression and optimization strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full Fisher matrix computation is infeasible for billion-parameter networks, making Hessian analysis impractical despite its importance for understanding model sensitivity and guiding compression/optimization strategies.

Method: Scalable Kronecker-based approximation that captures cross-layer interactions without storing entire Fisher matrix, enabling practical Hessian analysis for large-scale models.

Result: Value projection layers consistently show highest sensitivity and strongest cross-layer correlations across multiple model families; approximation strongly correlates with performance degradation (quantization, sparsification, corruption) and recovery (fine-tuning).

Conclusion: The framework provides practical, theoretically grounded tool for identifying fragile components in large models, enabling new compression/optimization strategies like mixed-precision allocation, layer-wise sparsity, and adaptive low-rank decomposition across layers and weight groups.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a scalable Kronecker-based approximation that captures cross-layer interactions without storing the entire Fisher matrix, enabling practical Hessian analysis for billion-parameter networks where full computation is infeasible. Our approach reveals consistent vulnerability patterns: value projection layers exhibit the highest sensitivity and strongest cross-layer correlations across multiple model families, while other components exhibit architecture-specific behaviors. Through extensive experiments on quantization, sparsification, inter-layer corruption, and post-corruption fine-tuning, we demonstrate that our approximation strongly correlates with both performance degradation and recovery. Our framework provides a practical, theoretically grounded tool for identifying fragile components in large models, opening new avenues for guided compression and optimization strategies, such as mixed-precision allocation, layer-wise sparsity, and adaptive low-rank decomposition across layers and even individual weight groups.

</details>


### [121] [DeepAffinity: Long-Term Aspect Preference Prediction in eCommerce using Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02468)
*Yotam Eshel,Guy Hadad,Guy Feigenblat,Yuri M. Brovman,Matt Gearhart,Bracha Shapira*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeepAffinity: a small language model approach for predicting user preferences for product aspects (brand, size, color) from temporal interaction history, outperforming standard methods and improving eCommerce recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to solve Aspect Affinity - predicting user preferences for specific product aspects like brand, size, and color. This improves customer understanding and enables fine-grained personalization in recommendation, search, and marketing systems.

Method: The authors propose DeepAffinity, which frames Aspect Affinity as a temporal prediction task forecasting future aspect choices from time-ordered interaction history. The method leverages Small Language Models (SLMs) with structured prompts and specialized prediction heads fine-tuned specifically for this task.

Result: DeepAffinity outperforms standard generative fine-tuning methods. General-purpose open-source LLMs perform poorly without task-specific tuning, highlighting their limitations in modeling nuanced user behavior. The approach enhances recommendation quality on a large-scale multinational eCommerce platform.

Conclusion: Task-specific tuning of small language models with structured prompts is effective for predicting evolving user preferences for product aspects, providing superior performance over generic methods and improving eCommerce personalization systems.

Abstract: We explore predicting eCommerce user preferences for product aspects such as brand, size, and color - a task we define as Aspect Affinity. Solving this task improves customer understanding and enables fine-grained personalization in recommendation, search, and marketing. We frame Aspect Affinity as a temporal prediction task: forecasting a users future aspect choices from their time-ordered interaction history, capturing long-term preferences that evolve beyond the current session. To this end, we propose DeepAffinity, which leverages Small Language Models (SLMs) with structured prompts and specialized prediction heads fine-tuned for this task. We show DeepAffinity outperforms standard generative fine-tuning methods, while general-purpose open-source LLMs perform poorly without task-specific tuning, highlighting their limits in modeling nuanced behavior. Finally, DeepAffinity enhances recommendation quality on a large-scale multinational eCommerce platform.

</details>


### [122] [RINSE: Robust Target-Time Normality Estimation for Zero-Shot Graph Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02497)
*Taufikur Rahman Fuad,Md Abrar Jahin,Amir Hussain*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RINSE is a gradient-free framework for zero-shot graph anomaly detection that sequentially estimates target normality, representation calibration, and evidence reliability from the target graph without modifying the source-trained detector.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Domain shift in zero-shot graph anomaly detection can make source-derived notions of normality unreliable when deployed to unseen target graphs, necessitating robust target-time estimation approaches.

Method: RINSE keeps the source-trained detector fixed and: 1) identifies reliable subset of low-residual target nodes, 2) constructs trimmed target-aware normality model, 3) combines anomaly evidence through reliability-gated rank fusion and encoder ensembling - all without gradients or target labels.

Result: Across eight unseen target graphs, RINSE achieves the highest average AUPRC among evaluated methods under two separate preprocessing protocols, with block ablations and sensitivity analyses supporting the combined design.

Conclusion: Robust target-time estimation provides a practical approach to generalist graph anomaly detection without requiring target labels, gradients, or per-target tuning, enabling reliable deployment of trained detectors to unseen domains.

Abstract: Zero-shot graph anomaly detection seeks to deploy a detector trained on source graphs to unseen, unlabeled targets, yet domain shift can make source-derived notions of normality unreliable. We introduce RINSE (Robust Iterative Normality Self-Estimation), a gradient-free target-time framework that keeps the source-trained detector fixed while sequentially estimating target normality, representation calibration, and evidence reliability from the target graph. Its core idea is to identify a reliable subset of low-residual target nodes, use them to construct a trimmed target-aware normality model, and combine complementary anomaly evidence through reliability-gated rank fusion and encoder ensembling. Across eight unseen target graphs, RINSE achieves the highest average AUPRC among the evaluated methods under two separate preprocessing protocols, while block ablations and sensitivity analyses support the combined design. These results support robust target-time estimation as a practical approach to generalist graph anomaly detection without target labels, gradients, or per-target tuning.

</details>


### [123] [Rethinking the Teacher-Student Framework for Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02507)
*Damian Sójka,Marc Masana,Bartłomiej Twardowski,Sebastian Cygert*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TTA methods using teacher-student frameworks suffer from error accumulation over long sequences; using an intransigent (non-updating) teacher instead of EMA-weighted teacher improves stability and performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) methods using teacher-student frameworks with exponential moving average (EMA) teacher weights still suffer from error accumulation over longer deployment sequences than typically tested, challenging the assumed long-term stability of these approaches.

Method: Proposes replacing the conventional EMA-weighted teacher in teacher-student TTA frameworks with an "intransigent teacher" that does not update its weights at all, maintaining fixed weights throughout adaptation.

Result: The intransigent teacher approach significantly improves TTA performance across multiple datasets with longer scenarios, increases robustness to hyperparameter changes, and works effectively with various architectures including semantic segmentation.

Conclusion: A simple change to use an intransigent (non-updating) teacher instead of EMA-weighted teacher in teacher-student TTA frameworks addresses error accumulation, improves long-term stability, and enhances performance across diverse scenarios.

Abstract: Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) has recently emerged as a promising strategy that allows the adaptation of pre-trained models to changing data distributions at deployment time, without access to any labels. To mitigate error accumulation, researchers have widely adopted the teacher-student framework, though its long-term stability is often taken for granted. In this work, we challenge the common strategy of setting the teacher weights to an exponential moving average of the student by showing that error accumulation still occurs, although it is mostly apparent on longer sequences compared to those commonly utilized. We analyze the stability-plasticity trade-off within the teacher-student framework and propose to use an intransigent teacher that does not update its weights. Surprisingly, we show that this simple change allows TTA methods to significantly improve their performance on multiple datasets with longer scenarios and result in increased robustness to changes in hyperparameters. Finally, we show that those changes can be seamlessly and effectively applied to various architectures and experimental setups, including semantic segmentation. The code is available at https://github.com/dmn-sjk/intransigent_teacher.

</details>


### [124] [Spectral Initialization and Scheduled Graph Smoothness for Uncertain Knowledge Graph Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02519)
*Md Abrar Jahin,Taufikur Rahman Fuad,Jay Pujara,Craig A. Knoblock*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QUEST improves UKG completion by using spectral graph eigenvectors for entity initialization and mini-batch Dirichlet energy regularization, achieving better accuracy and stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing UKG methods discard the global community and hub structure of confidence-weighted graphs during entity embedding initialization, limiting performance and causing training instability on dense graphs.

Method: QUEST uses smallest non-trivial eigenvectors of confidence-weighted graph Laplacian for entity initialization to incorporate community/hub structure pre-training, plus unbiased mini-batch Dirichlet energy regularizer for structural consistency.

Result: Improves confidence and link prediction on 6/8 metric-dataset pairs, matches previous best on remaining 2, eliminates instability spikes on dense graphs, with no added trainable parameters.

Conclusion: Spectral structural priors with graph Dirichlet energy regularization enhance accuracy, training stability, and checkpoint reliability for UKG completion tasks.

Abstract: Uncertain knowledge graphs (UKGs) extend knowledge graphs by assigning each triple a continuous confidence score. Since most possible triples lack observed confidences, recent methods rely on semi-supervised learning to generate pseudo-labels. These methods initialize entity embeddings without using the confidence-weighted graph, discarding its global community and hub structure. We introduce QUEST, which adds no trainable parameters to the standard confidence-distribution learning pipeline. First, QUEST initializes entity embeddings using the smallest non-trivial eigenvectors of the confidence-weighted graph Laplacian, incorporating community and hub structure before training. Second, QUEST applies an unbiased mini-batch Dirichlet energy regularizer to enforce early-stage structural consistency. On two UKG datasets, QUEST improves confidence prediction and link prediction on six of eight metric-dataset pairs over prior methods and matches the previous best on the remaining two, while removing the instability spike observed on dense graphs. These results indicate that spectral structural priors combined with a graph Dirichlet energy regularizer improve accuracy, training stability, and checkpoint reliability in UKG completion.

</details>


### [125] [A Comparative Study of Graph Representations for GNN-Based Power Grid Control in L2RPN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02538)
*Adrian Degenkolb,Qiong Huang,Benjamin Schäfer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Different graph representations for power grid control are experimentally compared, showing task-graph complexity matching is more important than maximal representation richness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph construction is a critical but underexamined design choice in deep reinforcement learning for power grid control. The authors identify a research gap in systematically studying different graph representations for this domain.

Method: Controlled experimental comparison of different graph representations including physical topology, electrical-sensitivity, and hybrid variants for topology control in the Learning to Run a Power Network (L2RPN) environment.

Result: Findings indicate that matching graph complexity to task granularity is more important than maximizing representational richness. The study highlights the value of controlled representation studies at scale.

Conclusion: Systematic study of graph representations reveals that optimal representation depends on task characteristics rather than simply using the most complex or rich representation. Controlled experiments at scale are valuable for understanding design choices in RL for power grid control.

Abstract: Graph construction is a critical but underexamined design choice in deep reinforcement learning for power grid control. We present a controlled experimental comparison of different graph representations, including physical topology, electrical-sensitivity, and hybrid variants for topology control in the Learning to Run a Power Network (L2RPN) environment. Our findings indicate that matching graph complexity to task granularity is more important than maximizing representational richness, and highlight the importance of controlled representation studies at scale.

</details>
