### [126] [TrajMind: Chaining Role-Specialized LoRAs for Fast-and-Slow Collective Trajectory Anomaly Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02540)
*Jiahao Wu,Zhenqun Yang,Chen Jason Zhang,Qing Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TrajMind is a fast-and-slow framework for diagnosing collective anomalies from urban trajectories that separates always-on screening from on-demand diagnosis, using specialized LoRA adapters over a frozen vision-language backbone to provide verifiable event details with low latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches for diagnosing collective anomalies from urban trajectories have limitations - existing detectors only provide scores/labels while vision-language pipelines offer richer semantics but cannot couple verifiable diagnosis with low-latency monitoring. The key challenge is recognizing collective patterns and recovering exact event details from source trajectories without running full diagnostic pipelines for every monitored window.

Method: TrajMind separates always-on screening from on-demand diagnosis using a fast-and-slow framework with three role-specialized LoRA adapters over a frozen vision-language backbone. The slow path (TrajMind_slow) chains canvas-based typing, type-conditioned localization over serialized trajectories, and executable verification for structured evidence-backed diagnoses. The fast path (TrajMind_fast) screens each window in a single text-only pass for efficient structured alerts.

Result: TrajMind_slow outperforms strongest baselines by at least 15.3 percentage points in anomaly typing and 13.8 percentage points in localization. These gains persist under cross-city transfer. TrajMind_fast reduces latency by 41.1% while maintaining binary balanced accuracy of at least 93.5%.

Conclusion: TrajMind delivers accurate, evidence-backed diagnoses across cities and efficient front-line monitoring by separating screening from diagnosis, enabling both low-latency alerting and verifiable detailed event analysis.

Abstract: Diagnosing collective anomalies from urban trajectories is increasingly important for traffic governance, as it reveals what happened, who was involved, and where and when the event occurred. Existing detectors efficiently produce scores or labels, whereas vision--language pipelines provide richer semantics; neither couples verifiable diagnosis with low-latency monitoring. The central challenge is to recognize collective patterns and recover exact event details from the source trajectories without running the full diagnostic pipeline for every monitored window. We therefore separate always-on screening from on-demand diagnosis: screening raises alerts, while diagnosis releases only source-verified what--who--where--when records. We present TrajMind, a fast-and-slow framework that switches three role-specialized LoRA adapters over one frozen vision--language backbone. Its slow path, \textit{TrajMind$_{\text{slow}}$}, chains canvas-based typing, type-conditioned localization over serialized trajectories, and executable verification, yielding structured, evidence-backed diagnoses. Additionally, the fast path, \textit{TrajMind$_{\text{fast}}$}, screens each window in a single text-only pass, delivering efficient structured alerts. Extensive experiments show that, TrajMind$_{\mathrm{slow}}$ outperforms the strongest baselines by at least $15.3$ percentage points in anomaly typing and $13.8$ percentage points in localization. These gains persist under cross-city transfer, and TrajMind$_{\mathrm{fast}}$ reduces latency by $41.1\%$ and maintains binary balanced accuracy of at least $93.5\%$. Together, TrajMind delivers accurate, evidence-backed diagnoses across cities and efficient front-line monitoring.

</details>


### [127] [Learn from Whoever Is Right: Answer-Verified Multi-Teacher Distillation for Multi-Domain LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02548)
*Xixiang He,Xingming Li,Baiqi Wu,Qiyao Sun,Xuanyu Ji,Ao Cheng,Qingyong Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MT-SDPO is a new distillation method that unifies multiple specialized teachers into a single student model by selecting the most reliable teacher per sample rather than per domain, using verification mechanisms to ensure correct supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM distillation approaches route samples to teachers based on domain labels, but this assumes domain expertise always applies to individual samples. In reality, the matched teacher may be wrong for specific samples while other domain teachers could be correct. There's a need for sample-level reliable teacher identification rather than domain-level routing.

Method: MT-SDPO uses three key components: (1) self-anchors where correct rollouts supervise their own group, (2) answer-verified eligibility where teachers must pass a verification check to supervise samples, and (3) privileged distillation that merges verified feedback into a context for an exponential moving average self-teacher while keeping a single deployable policy.

Result: Across five students from three model families, MT-SDPO improved the weakest domain of Qwen3-8B by 14.79 points and reduced its domain gap by 74.7%. This outperformed serving one matched teacher per domain and achieved better balance.

Conclusion: Teacher reliability should be determined per sample through verification rather than assumed per domain. MT-SDPO effectively unifies multiple frozen teachers into a single student model that maintains deployment simplicity while leveraging sample-level expertise.

Abstract: Modern large language models (LLMs) rely on reinforcement learning to build strong capabilities in individual domains, but integrating those capabilities into a single deployable model remains challenging. By routing each sample to the teacher whose domain matches it, existing approaches let a domain label decide which teacher provides supervision. However, domain expertise holds only on average: the matched teacher is not always correct on a given sample, while a teacher from another domain sometimes is. The reliable teacher therefore has to be identified per sample, not per domain. In this paper, we introduce Multi-Teacher Self-Distillation Policy Optimization (MT-SDPO), an on-policy distillation method that unifies several frozen teachers into one student model. MT-SDPO consists of three components: (1) self-anchors, where a rollout is supervised by a correct rollout from its own group; (2) answer-verified eligibility, where a teacher may supervise a sample only if its own answer passes a verifier; and (3) privileged distillation, which merges the anchor and all verified feedback into one context that an exponential moving average self-teacher reads and the student does not, thereby keeping one policy at deployment. Across five students from three model families, MT-SDPO lifts the weakest domain of Qwen3-8B by 14.79 points and narrows its domain gap by 74.7%, a better balance than serving one matched teacher per domain. Verified reliability, not domain membership, should decide who teaches. Code is available at https://github.com/hexixiang/MT-SDPO.

</details>


### [128] [ProbeMatchDTI: Probe-Driven Multi-Scale Biochemical Pattern Matching for Drug-Target Interaction Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02549)
*Quan Hao,Mengyue Fan,Zifan Dong,Youru Li,Jianduo Zhao,Lechuan Xu,Hao Zhang,Fei Xia,Jigang Wang,Chong Qiu,Liguo Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProbeMatchDTI is a pattern-probe-driven framework for drug-target interaction prediction that uses learnable probes to preserve weak biochemical patterns and strengthen multi-scale correspondences between drugs and proteins.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current biochemical representation learning methods for DTI prediction have limitations: their passive feature aggregation tends to favor dominant molecular patterns while suppressing weak yet binding-relevant signals like functional groups and residue-context patterns, limiting the modeling of multi-scale biochemical correspondences.

Method: ProbeMatchDTI comprises two main components: IterProbe (explicitly retains contextual states across refinement depths and uses learnable probes to select them at each position before cross-entity matching) and BindingProbe (characterizes cross-entity drug-protein complementarity at local biochemical-unit and whole-pair levels). The framework jointly models fine-grained interactions and multi-scale correspondences while preserving weaker binding-relevant associations.

Result: Extensive experiments show ProbeMatchDTI's superiority, achieving 2.0% higher AUC-ROC on BindingDB and 0.5% higher on DrugBank compared to previous methods. Feature-level pattern analyses characterize its probe-driven behavior in cross-scale biochemical pattern matching, and downstream applications demonstrate utility for candidate refinement and validation planning.

Conclusion: ProbeMatchDTI successfully addresses the limitations of previous DTI prediction methods by preserving weak biochemical patterns through probe-driven pattern matching, leading to improved prediction performance and practical utility in drug discovery workflows.

Abstract: Drug-target interaction (DTI) prediction is an important task in AI-driven drug discovery. Although recent biochemical representation learning methods have improved DTI prediction, their passive feature aggregation tends to favor dominant molecular patterns while suppressing weak yet binding-relevant signals, such as functional groups and residue-context patterns, limiting the modeling of multi-scale biochemical correspondences. To address this issue, we propose ProbeMatchDTI, a pattern-probe-driven framework comprising IterProbe and BindingProbe. IterProbe explicitly retains contextual states across refinement depths and uses learnable probes to select them at each position before cross-entity matching, thereby preserving weak biochemical patterns and strengthening associations among functional groups, local motifs, and molecular scaffolds. BindingProbe then characterizes cross-entity drug-protein complementarity at local biochemical-unit and whole-pair levels, jointly modeling fine-grained interactions and multi-scale correspondences while preserving weaker binding-relevant associations. Extensive experiments demonstrate the superiority of ProbeMatchDTI, achieving 2.0% and 0.5% higher AUC-ROC on BindingDB and DrugBank, respectively. Feature-level pattern analyses further characterize its probe-driven behavior in cross-scale biochemical pattern matching. We further connect ProbeMatchDTI predictions with an evidence-guided downstream drug-discovery workflow, demonstrating their utility for candidate refinement and validation planning. Our code is available at https://github.com/developer-hq/ProbeMatchDTI

</details>


### [129] [Online Reinforcement Learning in the Met Office Unified Model through Distributed Model-Agent Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02566)
*Pritthijit Nath,Sebastian Schemm,Peter Haynes,Emily Shuckburgh,Mark Webb*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Distributed RL agents coupled with a global weather prediction model successfully reduced forecast errors through online learning of potential-temperature corrections, demonstrating feasibility for operational bias correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine-learned corrections for numerical weather prediction need to adapt to evolving model states while maintaining dynamical consistency and numerical stability within operational forecasting systems.

Method: Coupling the Met Office Unified Model with distributed RL agents using DDPG architecture; training with nudged forecasts toward operational analysis targets; applying bounded potential-temperature corrections to model tendencies with weight sharing across vertical levels.

Result: Successfully completed training with numerical stability; reduced Z500 MAE in 4 of 6 latitude bands (45.8% in northern tropics, 40.8% in southern tropics); reduced MSLP error in 3 bands (max 27.3% at 0-30°N).

Conclusion: Demonstrates significant promise for distributed online learning followed by non-nudged inference in operational weather forecasting, laying groundwork for RL-based bias correction and parametrizations.

Abstract: Machine-learnt corrections can complement numerical weather prediction only if they adapt to the evolving model state while preserving dynamical consistency and numerical stability. To test this within a global forecasting model, we couple the Met Office (UKMO) Unified Model (UM) with distributed RL agents through rank-local tensors. A DDPG actor shares weights across the 70 vertical model levels of each atmospheric column and applies bounded potential-temperature corrections to the model tendencies. Across ten nudged training forecasts, nudging calculations towards the UKMO operational analysis provides an immediate counterfactual target. The frozen policy is then evaluated in a non-nudged forecast for inference. The coupled workflow successfully completes training and remains numerically stable in the evaluated case. Relative to a matched native UM forecast at +6 h, the learnt policy reduces Z$_{500}$ MAE in four of six latitude bands, including reductions of 45.8% and 40.8% in the northern and southern tropics. MSLP error too decreases in three bands, with a maximum reduction of 27.3% at 0-30°N. This single-case experiment demonstrates significant promise and feasibility of distributed online learning followed by non-nudged inference, laying the groundwork for RL-based bias correction and parametrisations within operational systems.

</details>


### [130] [Source Distribution Estimation by Posterior Averaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02622)
*Trung-Dung Hoang,Lisa M. Koch*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel expectation-maximization approach for source distribution estimation that iteratively refines both the likelihood surrogate and source distribution, outperforming fixed-surrogate methods on benchmark tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for source distribution estimation (SDE) use a fixed proposal prior and train a likelihood surrogate once, which fails in parameter space areas where the surrogate is inaccurate due to lack of training data.

Method: Proposes an expectation-maximization approach: E-step trains amortized posterior on fresh simulations from current source estimate; M-step refits source distribution to the average posterior over observed data. Two parameterizations: (1) separate source and posterior flows, (2) single shared conditional flow.

Result: Method outperforms existing fixed-surrogate approaches and iterated variants on three benchmark tasks. Most significant improvement on Lotka-Volterra: baselines score ≥0.96 data-space C2ST while proposed methods reach 0.64-0.68 in three of four initial-prior settings.

Conclusion: Iterative expectation-maximization approach with fresh simulations effectively addresses limitations of fixed-surrogate methods in source distribution estimation, demonstrating superior performance particularly under challenging conditions.

Abstract: Simulation-based science often requires a distribution over simulator parameters whose push-forward reproduces a set of real observations: this is the source distribution estimation (SDE) problem. Existing methods fit the source against a likelihood surrogate trained once from a fixed proposal prior. Their objective is therefore stated only in terms of the surrogate instead of the true simulator, which may fail for inaccurate areas in parameter space where the surrogate was never trained. We instead solve SDE by expectation maximization: an E-step trains an amortized posterior on fresh simulations from the current source estimate, and an M-step refits the source to the average of that posterior over the observed data. We give two parameterizations, (1) separate source and posterior flows and (2) a single shared conditional flow. We evaluate our method on three benchmark tasks under both broad and misspecified initial priors. Both improve on existing fixed surrogate approaches and on iterated variants of each, most clearly on Lotka--Volterra, where no baseline falls below 0.96 data-space C2ST while our methods reach 0.64-0.68 in three of four initial-prior settings.

</details>


### [131] [Oracle, will I ever learn? A study of prediction convergence and complementarity across link prediction models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02638)
*Guillaume Méroué,Fabien Gandon,Pierre Monnin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Different link prediction models capture complementary knowledge about knowledge graphs, but this complementarity saturates quickly as more models are added, leaving some queries permanently unsolvable by current approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Different link prediction models (or even different training runs of the same model) produce substantially different predictions for the same queries, suggesting variability in how they capture underlying knowledge. This raises fundamental questions about whether models capture complementary knowledge and how much could be recovered by combining them, which is crucial for building robust Web applications that rely on knowledge graphs.

Method: Proposes measuring model complementarity through an oracle that selects the best prediction among a set of models for each query, providing an upper bound on achievable performance through model combination. This approach is tested across several architectures and benchmarks to analyze complementarity patterns.

Result: 1) Substantial gap between individual models and their oracle, revealing that different models do capture complementary knowledge. 2) Complementarity rapidly saturates as more models are added. 3) Persistent subset of queries remains unsolved even by large numbers of models, indicating fundamental limits to what current models can collectively recover.

Conclusion: The findings reveal both the potential of model complementarity (significant performance gains possible through combination) and a fundamental limit to what current link prediction models can collectively recover. This highlights the need for further research to build truly robust Web applications that rely on knowledge graphs.

Abstract: Knowledge graphs have become an important source of structured knowledge for Web applications, including search, question answering, and recommender systems. In these applications, link prediction can serve either as a prediction task itself or as a means to enrich incomplete knowledge graphs for downstream tasks. Interestingly, different link prediction models, or even different training runs of the same model, can produce substantially different predictions for the same query. This suggests a variability in the capture of the underlying knowledge by models, thus raising a fundamental question: to what extent do different models capture complementary knowledge, and how much of this knowledge could be recovered by combining them? We propose to measure model complementarity through the performance of an oracle that, for each query, selects the best prediction among a considered set of models, hence providing an upper bound on the performance achievable through model combination. Across several architectures and benchmarks, we find a substantial gap between individual models and their oracle, revealing that different models capture complementary knowledge. Yet, this complementarity rapidly saturates as more models are added, leaving a persistent subset of queries unsolved even by a large number of models. These findings reveal both the potential of model complementarity and a fundamental limit to what current link prediction models can collectively recover; thereby highlighting the need for further research to build robust Web applications.

</details>


### [132] [Differentiable Electricity-Market Clearing for Gradient-Based Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02646)
*Luca Mungo,Maarten P. Scholl,Arnau Quera-Bofarull*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Differentiable optimization enables gradient-based planning of data center locations by treating electricity market clearing as a differentiable layer, allowing planners to optimize site allocations through backpropagation rather than just simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Planning large data centers is challenging because they affect electricity prices through market clearing, creating a feedback loop. Traditional simulation can evaluate plans but doesn't provide guidance on how to improve them, making optimization difficult.

Method: Treat electricity market clearing as a differentiable optimization layer where forward pass solves the market (constrained optimization), and reverse-mode automatic differentiation propagates planning costs back through cleared prices to the plan. Apply gradient optimization to allocate 50MW data-center load across six candidate buses in two synthetic networks with fixed site costs over 36 operating states.

Result: Gradient optimization recovers continuous allocations almost exactly (2.3%-8.5% worst-case objective gaps), but shows systematic error near discrete site transitions: smooth relaxation shrinks sites rather than closing them, delaying discrete transitions. Validated gradients against finite differences.

Conclusion: Differentiable market clearing transforms market-aware planning into a gradient-searchable problem, enabling efficient optimization of data center locations despite complex market feedback effects, though discrete site transitions require special handling.

Abstract: Planning a large data center is difficult because a facility big enough to matter changes the electricity prices it will pay. Those prices are set by market clearing, a constrained optimization problem solved anew in every operating condition. However, simulating the market tells a planner how a candidate plan performs but not how to improve it. Here we treat market clearing as a differentiable optimization layer: each forward pass solves the market, and reverse-mode automatic differentiation propagates the planning cost back through the cleared prices to the plan. After validating these gradients against finite differences, we apply them to a concrete problem: allocating 50 MW of data-center load across six candidate buses in two synthetic networks, under a fixed cost per active site, evaluated over 36 operating states. Judged against exhaustive enumeration of all site combinations, gradient optimization recovers the continuous allocations almost exactly, with worst-case objective gaps of 2.3\% and 8.5\% of the cost difference between the best and worst single site. Its one systematic error is instructive: near the costs at which a site should close, the smooth relaxation of the discrete site count shrinks the site rather than closing it, so discrete transitions arrive late. Differentiable market clearing thus turns market-aware planning into a problem gradients can search.

</details>


### [133] [Unfolding the Leech Lattice: Fused Multi-Shell Decoding and VRAM Layouts for 2-Bit LLM Weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02652)
*Pier-Jean Malandrino*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper implements a multi-shell decoder for Leech-lattice vector quantization and evaluates its serving costs, showing efficient 4-bit and 2-bit GEMV kernels that outperform FP16 while maintaining quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Leech-lattice VQ has strong 2-bit quality but lacks a practical multi-shell decoder implementation needed for the required rate. The paper addresses this gap by providing a working decoder and measuring its serving costs for real-world deployment.

Method: 1) Developed a serving path for full 301-class codebook with offline GPU layout expansion and fused dequantize-plus-matvec kernel without warp divergence. 2) Explored different bit-exact layouts showing binary bit planes outperform one-hot masks. 3) Implemented deployed 4-bit (AWQ) and 2-bit (QTIP) GEMV kernels that run in same process. 4) Tested on multiple model sizes (4B, 8B, 14B) with various output heads.

Result: The trellis kernel reads 2.40x fewer bytes than served layout and runs 2.27x faster. Binary bit planes beat one-hot masks at constant bandwidth (4.80 bits/weight, 2.15x FP16). With int8 output head, served 4B model reaches 87.0 tok/s in 2.60 GB. Quality cost is 1.38x perplexity and 14.7 MMLU points at 4B, improving with larger models.

Conclusion: The paper successfully implements a practical multi-shell decoder for Leech-lattice VQ, demonstrating efficient 4-bit and 2-bit quantization with significant speed improvements over FP16 while maintaining acceptable quality degradation that diminishes with larger model sizes.

Abstract: Leech-lattice vector quantization holds the strongest reported 2-bit quality under its own evaluation protocol. Its kernel decodes one shell; we found no implementation of the multi-shell decoder the rate requires. This paper supplies one and measures its serving cost for decode-phase GEMV at batch 1. First, a serving path for the full 301-class codebook: an offline expansion into GPU layouts and a fused dequantize-plus-matvec kernel reading them without warp divergence, verified against f64. Second, the in-VRAM rate is a design axis distinct from the on-disk rate. Four bit-exact layouts timed in one process show binary bit planes beating one-hot masks on size and speed at constant bandwidth (4.80 bits per weight, 2.15x FP16). Below 4.3 bits a second, irregular stream enters; at 3.6 the decode stops being shifts and masks. Third, deployed four-bit (AWQ) and two-bit (QTIP) GEMV kernels run in the same process. The trellis kernel reads 2.40x fewer bytes than our served layout and runs 2.27x faster at near-equal fractions of their byte bounds: the time gap tracks the traffic gap, the price of unfolding a codebook too large for a lookup table. Fourth, the validity envelope: the trellis kernel outruns our no-weights control, so our launch geometry sets that floor, and on a second memory hierarchy every lattice arm falls below FP16. With the output head held identical across arms, the kernel-and-format path gains 1.11x, 1.29x and 1.41x end to end at 4B, 8B and 14B; with an int8 output head the served 4B reaches 87.0 tok/s in 2.60 GB. The quality cost, 1.38x perplexity and 14.7 MMLU points at 4B, shrinks across the three sizes measured.

</details>


### [134] [H3DNAS: Hardware-Aware ONNX-Native 3D Point Cloud Model Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02684)
*Anchit Mulye,Rhythm Baghel,Sujay Kumar Ingle,Hardik Jain*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: H3DNAS is a hardware-aware compression framework for 3D point cloud models that works directly on ONNX binaries without source code, achieving significant parameter reduction and speedup while maintaining accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying 3D point cloud models on edge hardware like NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano is constrained by compute and memory. Existing compression methods require access to original source code, making them incompatible with ONNX binaries commonly used by vendors.

Method: Uses Channel Dependency Graph (CDG) to classify ONNX operators into constraint classes and prove topological invariants. Employs Two-Stage Hierarchical Search: prunes candidates via L1-importance channel selection, ranks by output fidelity as zero-shot proxy, and applies GhostConv mutation to Pareto-optimal candidates.

Result: On ModelNet40: reduces parameters in PointNet (65.5%), PointNet++ (43.2%), PointMLP (49.1%) while achieving inference speedups of 1.99×, 1.29×, and 1.67× respectively with negligible accuracy loss.

Conclusion: H3DNAS provides the first source-code-free compression pipeline for 3D point cloud models, operating entirely via ONNX graph surgery without requiring original architecture definitions, making it practical for real-world deployment scenarios.

Abstract: Deploying 3D point cloud models on edge hardware such as the NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano is severely constrained by compute and memory budgets. Existing compression methods require access to the model's original source code, rendering them inapplicable to the Open Neural Network Exchange (ONNX) binaries commonly distributed by vendors and model repositories. We present \textbf{H3DNAS}, a hardware-aware model compression framework that operates directly on ONNX computational graphs without requiring original source code, architecture class definition, or gradient access during search. H3DNAS makes three contributions: (1) a \textbf{Channel Dependency Graph (CDG)} that classifies ONNX operators into four constraint classes and formally establishes that the free parameter fraction $ρ_f$ is topological invariant, a provable compression ceiling computable in $\mathcal{O}(|V|+|E|)$; (2) a \textbf{Two-Stage Hierarchical Search} that prunes candidate architectures by $L_1$-importance channel selection, ranks them by output fidelity as a zero-shot label-free proxy, and applies GhostConv structural mutation to Pareto-optimal candidates; and (3) the \textbf{first source-code-free compression pipeline for 3D point cloud models}, operating entirely via ONNX graph surgery with no original architecture definition required. On ModelNet40, H3DNAS reduces the number of parameters in PointNet, PointNet++, and PointMLP by $65.5\%$, $43.2\%$, and $49.1\%$, respectively, while achieving $1.99\times$, $1.29\times$, and $1.67\times$ inference speedups with negligible loss in accuracy. The source code is publicly available\footnote{https://github.com/ClarityLab-Org/h3dnas}.

</details>


### [135] [LoRA-TSD: Tangent-Space Spectral Descent for LoRA via Muon-Style Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02734)
*Dmitrii Andriianov,Andrey Veprikov,Aleksandr Beznosikov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LoRA-TSD is a novel optimizer for low-rank adaptation that treats LoRA updates as tangent vectors on fixed-rank matrix manifolds, providing more geometrically accurate optimization with better performance than existing LoRA methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard LoRA fine-tuning has a fundamental problem: when its two factor matrices are trained independently, the update ignores the geometry of the low-rank weight change it induces, leading to suboptimal optimization.

Method: LoRA-TSD treats every LoRA update step as a tangent vector on the fixed-rank matrix manifold. It takes the spectral-norm steepest-descent step inside that tangent space (using Muon method) and maps the result back to factors through a retraction native to LoRA parametrization. The method computes tangent-projected gradients from factor gradients alone.

Result: LoRA-TSD outperforms all competing LoRA optimizers across six commonsense and natural-language-inference benchmarks with models ranging from 1B to 32B parameters. It's up to 2.8× cheaper than truncated-SVD retractions used by prior manifold methods and stays robust to adapter rank.

Conclusion: LoRA-TSD provides a geometrically correct optimization approach for LoRA fine-tuning with proven convergence guarantees, better performance, and computational efficiency compared to existing methods.

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) is the standard way to fine-tune large models, yet when its two factors are trained independently, the update ignores the geometry of the low-rank weight change it induces. We introduce LoRA-TSD, an optimizer that treats every LoRA step as a tangent vector of the fixed-rank matrix manifold and takes the spectral-norm steepest-descent step of Muon inside that tangent space, mapping the result back to the factors through a retraction native to the LoRA parametrization. The step avoids expensive operations on full weight matrices, and its retraction is up to $2.8\times$ cheaper than the truncated-SVD retraction used by prior manifold methods. We prove that the Frobenius-norm version of our surrogate recovers LoRA-Pro, and we identify the tangent-projected gradient, the Riemannian gradient of the manifold, as the stationarity measure natural to LoRA training and computable from the factor gradients alone. Under this measure we give the first global convergence guarantees for both LoRA-Pro and LoRA-TSD, with rates that drive the factor-gradient norms to zero. Across six commonsense and natural-language-inference benchmarks with Llama-3.2-1B, Llama-3.1-8B and Qwen3-32B, LoRA-TSD outperforms every competing LoRA optimizer and stays robust to the adapter rank. Code is available at https://github.com/brain-lab-research/LoRA-TSD.

</details>


### [136] [Do Tabular Foundation Models Know Physics? Contamination, Units, and the Deterministic Limit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02766)
*Wassim Tenachi,Yashar Hezaveh,Laurence Perreault Levasseur,Pierre-Luc Bacon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TFMs excel at tabular prediction tasks but fail to learn true physical models because their priors cannot represent noiseless mechanisms or physical units.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand if tabular foundation models learn physics during their training on tabular data, since tables are the primary format for physical measurements.

Method: Probed the priors of four TFMs (TabPFN-3, TabICLv2, TabDPT and Real-TabPFN-2.5) against six baselines using datasets sampled from 316 physical equations, testing both in-domain and out-of-domain scenarios.

Result: TFMs dominate over baselines, both out-of-the-box and after tuning. However, their priors cannot represent noiseless mechanisms or physical units.

Conclusion: While TFMs interpolate physics well, they cannot act as true physical models because their learned priors lack essential physical representations.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) learn to fill in tables the way language models fill in text, and tables are arguably the format in which most physical measurement arrives. Did they learn any physics in the process? They are Bayesian by construction, so the question is what their prior contains. We probe it directly, evaluating four of them (TabPFN-3, TabICLv2, TabDPT and Real-TabPFN-2.5) against six baselines on datasets sampled from 316 physical equations, in and out of domain. TFMs dominate, out of the box and after tuning. But we show that their prior can represent neither a noiseless mechanism nor physical units, which is why they interpolate physics without yet being able to act as physical models.

</details>


### [137] [Cliff: Learning Process Rewards from the First Mistake](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02817)
*Peixuan Han,Runhui Wang,Ketan Ramaneti,Jie Hao,Gerald Friedland,Chris Kong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cliff is a reward shaping strategy for RLVR that uses an off-the-shelf LLM teacher to identify the first mistake in reasoning rollouts, then assigns token-level advantages based on correct prefixes and incorrect suffixes, outperforming existing methods without needing specialized reward models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR with coarse outcome rewards provides limited guidance on intermediate reasoning processes. Existing approaches like process reward modeling require specialized reward models, while on-policy distillation assumes identical reasoning patterns between teacher and student, limiting their effectiveness and practicality.

Method: Cliff uses an off-the-shelf LLM as a teacher to identify the first mistake in reasoning rollouts, decomposing each rollout into a correct prefix and incorrect suffix. It converts this signal into token-level advantages - assigning positive advantages for correct prefixes and negative feedback for suffixes after the first mistake - without requiring specialized reward models.

Result: Experiments across 12 different scenarios show Cliff consistently improves reasoning performance, outperforming on-policy distillation by 15% and standard GRPO by 7%, even with modest-capability teachers. The method is effective without relying on ground truth annotations.

Conclusion: Cliff establishes a simple, general, and effective approach for improving RLVR with fine-grained supervision by leveraging the insight that once reasoning first goes wrong, subsequent reasoning provides limited additional information, enabling more targeted reward shaping.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for large language model (LLM) post-training, but its reliance on coarse outcome rewards leads to limited guidance on intermediate reasoning processes. Existing approaches such as process reward modeling and on-policy distillation introduce additional constraints, such as reliance on a specialized reward model or assuming identical reasoning patterns between teacher and student. Nevertheless, we observe that once a reasoning process first goes wrong, evaluating the subsequent reasoning provides limited additional information, as it is already conditioned on an invalid prefix. Therefore, we propose Cliff, a reward shaping strategy that utilizes an off-the-shelf LLM as a teacher to identify the first mistake in each rollout. As a result, the rollout is naturally decomposed into two parts: a correct prefix and an incorrect suffix. Cliff then converts this signal into token-level advantages, assigning positive advantages for the correct prefix and negative feedback afterward. Experiments across 12 different scenarios demonstrate that Cliff consistently improves reasoning performance, outperforming on-policy distillation by 15% and standard GRPO by 7%, even with teachers of modest capability. Furthermore, we analyse the role of ``ground truth'' in Cliff and investigate its training dynamics. These results establish Cliff as a simple, general and effective approach for improving RLVR with richer, fine-grained supervision.

</details>


### [138] [UE5M3 FP4 Block Scaling for Stable Language Model Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02846)
*Robert Hu,Carlo Luschi,Paul Balanca*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new block scaling method using unsigned E5M3 with E2M1 payloads for stable 4-bit FP pretraining, outperforming NVIDIA's Transformer Engine approach with simpler recipe and better performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stable 4-bit floating-point (FP4) pretraining is challenging because the E2M1 format has limited range, requiring complex workarounds like NVIDIA's Transformer Engine approach with randomized Hadamard transform and bfloat16 final layers.

Method: Pairs E2M1 payloads with unsigned E5M3 block scales for wider range, enabling periodic tensor scaling. Uses selective stochastic rounding for backward gradients, omits randomized Hadamard transform, and applies FP4 to all eligible internal linear layers.

Result: Pretrained Nemotron-H 8B model for 190B tokens, achieving lower training loss and validation loss than Transformer Engine. Quantized-inference downstream performance higher on all three aggregates. Native execution shows 21.2% throughput improvement without RHT and bfloat16 exemption.

Conclusion: Demonstrates effective end-to-end software-emulated FP4 pretraining with simpler recipe, motivating native hardware support for unsigned E5M3 block scaling as a superior alternative to current FP4 approaches.

Abstract: Stable 4-bit floating-point (FP4) pretraining is difficult because the E2M1 payload represents only a narrow range of magnitudes. NVIDIA's Transformer Engine \nv{} recipe addresses this with current-tensor scaling, a randomized Hadamard transform (RHT), and bfloat16 (BF16) final layers, adding work outside the FP4 matrix multiplications. We instead pair E2M1 payloads with unsigned E5M3 (\ue{}) block scales. Their wider range permits periodic tensor scaling, while our recipe applies selective stochastic rounding to backward gradients, omits RHT, and uses FP4 in all eligible internal linears.
  We pretrain a Nemotron-H 8B model for nearly 190 billion tokens. Compared with Transformer Engine \nv{}, the proposed block-16 recipe finishes with lower final-window training loss and, under their respective quantized-inference policies, lower validation loss measured as held-out negative log-likelihood. Its quantized-inference downstream point estimates are also higher on all three reported aggregates. A native \nv{} execution ablation that jointly removes RHT and the BF16 final-block exemption increases measured model-body token throughput by 21.2\%. These results demonstrate end-to-end software-emulated \uefp{} pretraining with a simpler recipe and motivate native support for \ue{} block scaling.

</details>


### [139] [The Implications of Linguistic Illegibility for LLM Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02852)
*James Mickens*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs' linguistic outputs don't reliably reflect their internal computations (linguistic illegibility), so security mechanisms relying on linguistic self-reporting can't be completely trusted; sandboxing needs isolation techniques like taint tracking and virtualization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs' external linguistic outputs and probed linguistic features can be unreliable indicators of internal model computation, creating a fundamental problem called "linguistic illegibility" where language artifacts fail to represent how models actually think.

Method: The paper introduces the concept of "linguistic illegibility" and argues it's unavoidable for LLMs due to their math-based internal computations. It proposes taint tracking as a promising sandbox approach that monitors system state influence by model-produced data, plus additional mechanisms like robust virtualization and third-party auditing.

Result: The paper establishes that linguistic illegibility undermines security mechanisms relying on linguistic self-reporting (chain-of-thought monitoring, constitutional self-critique, activation probing). Taint tracking policies can provide better security by defining system state that should never be influenced by model-produced data.

Conclusion: Sandboxing mechanisms must include isolation techniques whose guarantees don't depend on reading linguistic state, using approaches like taint tracking, robust virtualization, and third-party auditing to mitigate risks from linguistic illegibility, as demonstrated by their potential to counter recent frontier model exploits.

Abstract: LLMs are trained to generate natural language. However, various strands of evidence indicate that an LLM's externalized linguistic outputs and mechanistically-extracted linguistic features can be an unreliable lens for understanding internal model computation. We introduce the term ``linguistic illegibility'' to broadly refer to scenarios in which an LLM's externalized or mechanistically-probed language artifacts fail to represent how the model actually thinks. We argue that the specter of linguistic illegibility is unavoidable for LLMs whose internal computations are not directly expressed via language, but rather math over activation spaces (with lossy translations between activation spaces and natural language happening at the bookends). If linguistic illegibility is always possible, then security mechanisms that rely on a model's linguistic self-reporting (e.g., chain-of-thought monitoring, constitutional self-critique, activation probing for linguistically-defined feature vectors) can never be completely sound; the model sandbox will always need isolation techniques whose guarantees do not depend on reading a model's linguistic state at all. We argue that observing a model's outputs using taint tracking is a promising approach for an effective sandbox: regardless of how a model linguistically self-reports, a taint tracking policy can define, a priori, various pieces of system state that should never be influenced by model-produced data. We also discuss several additional sandboxing mechanisms (e.g., robust virtualization, third-party auditing of sandboxing configurations) which collectively provide a critical floor beneath linguistic monitoring, and would have mitigated recent sandbox exploits by frontier models.

</details>


### [140] [Graph Machine: Towards Better Pretraining via Edges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02881)
*Lintai Hou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Graph Machine (GM) is a novel architecture with O(n)-sized state and dynamic sparse routing via differentiable pointer-like edges, showing competitive performance when replacing 75% of dense Transformer layers in a Qwen3-0.6B model with minimal performance degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods either use fixed-size states or have sparse but static routing, which restricts potentially accessible state size to O(1). There's a need for architectures that maintain O(n) complexity in sparse layers while allowing dynamic access to larger state spaces.

Method: The Graph Machine (GM) architecture maintains an O(n)-sized state with sparse, dynamic routing. It uses edges (pointer-like objects) that are updated differentiably through a referral mechanism resembling pointer chasing. The researchers replaced 75% of dense Transformer layers in Qwen3-0.6B with GM sparse layers and pretrained from scratch on 15.7B tokens.

Result: With only 2 of 4,096 tokens retrieved per KV head in each sparse layer, loss degrades only slightly. With 4 tokens retrieved, the best model shows marginal improvement in loss. The approach demonstrates that GM sparse layers can replace most dense Transformer layers while maintaining competitive performance.

Conclusion: The Graph Machine architecture successfully enables sparse layers with O(n) complexity while allowing dynamic access to larger state spaces through differentiable pointer-like edges, offering a promising direction for more efficient model architectures.

Abstract: We introduce the Graph Machine (GM), an architecture that maintains an $O(n)$-sized state and accesses it through sparse, dynamic routing. Unlike methods with fixed-size states or sparse but static routing, GM preserves $O(n)$ complexity in its sparse layers without restricting the potentially accessible state size to $O(1)$. Instead, GM uses edges - pointer-like objects updated differentiably by a referral mechanism resembling pointer chasing. We replace 75% of the dense Transformer layers in Qwen3-0.6B with GM sparse layers and pretrain from scratch on 15.7B tokens. With only 2 of 4,096 tokens retrieved per KV head in each sparse layer, loss degrades only slightly; with 4, the best model marginally improves loss.

</details>


### [141] [A Common Measure of Communication for Speech Brain-Computer Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02887)
*Dulhan Jayalath,Benjamin Ballyk,Oiwi Parker Jones*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces open-vocabulary mutual information (OVMI), an information-theoretic metric that enables fair comparison of speech BCI systems by measuring how much information they convey relative to a user's intended vocabulary distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speech BCIs lack a common measure of progress because systems use different datasets, recording methods, speech types, and vocabularies, making reported scores incomparable. Two fundamental questions remain unresolved: (1) what distribution of words should a speech BCI enable communication of, and (2) how much information from this distribution can a system convey.

Method: The authors derive open-vocabulary mutual information (OVMI), an information-theoretic quantity that measures information conveyed by a decoder relative to a reference distribution over the words a user may wish to communicate. This allows systems with different vocabularies to be evaluated on a common communication scale.

Result: The paper shows that traditional metrics like accuracy and word error rate computed only over system-supported words can overstate communication capability. Using OVMI, the authors compare existing systems, expose trade-offs between vocabulary coverage and decoding accuracy, demonstrate that comparisons depend on expected user communication, and show that vocabulary selection optimized for OVMI yields up to 16.3% relative accuracy improvement across three speech domains.

Conclusion: OVMI provides the speech BCI community with a principled way to compare heterogeneous systems, improve vocabulary design, and measure progress in the field by addressing fundamental questions about what distribution of words systems should support and how much information they can convey from this distribution.

Abstract: Speech brain-computer interfaces (speech BCIs) translate neural activity into language, offering a path towards restoring speech for people with paralysis and, more broadly, enabling new forms of natural human-computer interaction. Despite this promise, the field lacks a common measure of progress because systems use different datasets, recording methods, types of speech, and vocabularies, so their reported scores are rarely comparable. Underlying this measurement problem are two unresolved questions: (i) what distribution of words should a speech BCI enable a user to communicate, and (ii) how much information from this distribution can a system convey. We address both by deriving open-vocabulary mutual information (OVMI), an information-theoretic quantity that measures the information conveyed by a decoder relative to a reference distribution over the words a user may wish to communicate. This allows capabilities measured under different conditions, such as distinct vocabularies, to be evaluated on a common communication scale. We show that ordinarily reported accuracy, word error rate (WER), and other metrics computed only over the words a system supports can overstate how much of a user's intended speech the system can communicate. We then use OVMI to compare existing systems, expose trade-offs between how much of the user's language a system supports and how accurately it decodes those words, show that these comparisons depend on what the user is expected to communicate, and demonstrate that selecting a vocabulary to maximise OVMI yields up to 16.3% relative improvement in accuracy across three speech domains. OVMI therefore provides the speech BCI community with a principled way to compare heterogeneous systems, improve vocabulary design, and measure progress in the field.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [142] [PRISM: An Agentic Multi-Model Architecture for Proactive Safety in Autonomous Transportation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01623)
*Joyjit Roy,Samaresh Kumar Singh,Sushanta Das*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: PRISM is a proactive safety system for autonomous vehicles that uses multi-model risk assessment to prevent accidents, especially with vulnerable road users, through graduated interventions instead of just reactive crash avoidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ADAS systems are reactive and only activate after hazards emerge, which is insufficient given rising VRU fatalities. There's a need to transition from reactive crash avoidance to proactive, continuous risk management in complex urban environments.

Method: PRISM uses inverse crash-probability modeling to convert binary classifiers into dynamic safety scores. It employs three specialized models for trajectory kinematics, environmental risk, and VRU interaction, coordinated by a reasoning layer with reinforcement learning, contextual memory, and feature-level attribution. The system provides four tiers of safety interventions.

Result: Validated across 1,296 scenarios from three naturalistic driving datasets, PRISM achieved a mean safety score of 68/100, classified 77.6% of scenarios as advisory, flagged 3.8% near-miss rate, with 11% escalating to intervention/emergency. Feature attribution identified trajectory risk and VRU proximity as key safety factors.

Conclusion: PRISM provides a unified, interpretable framework for proactive transportation safety that dynamically adjusts parameters in real time and effectively addresses VRU risk reduction in dense urban environments, moving beyond static rule-based systems.

Abstract: Autonomous and intelligent transportation systems operate in complex urban environments where safety depends on interactions among vehicle behavior, environmental conditions, and vulnerable road users (VRUs) such as pedestrians and cyclists. Most advanced driver assistance systems (ADAS) employ reactive mechanisms that activate only after hazards have emerged, a critical limitation underscored by rising VRU fatalities in the United States.
  This study introduces PRISM (Proactive Risk Intelligence and Safety Management), an agentic multi-model safety architecture that transitions from reactive crash avoidance to proactive, continuous risk management. PRISM employs inverse crash-probability modeling to convert binary crash classifiers into dynamic, interpretable safety scores. Three specialized models addressing trajectory kinematics, environmental risk, and VRU interaction operate concurrently, coordinated by a reasoning layer incorporating reinforcement learning, contextual memory, and feature-level attribution. The system provides graduated safety interventions across four tiers, from silent monitoring to emergency alerts.
  Unlike rule-based systems with static thresholds, PRISM dynamically adjusts safety parameters in real time. Validated across 1,296 scenarios from three naturalistic driving datasets without dataset-specific retraining, the system yielded a mean safety score of 68 out of 100, classified 77.6% of scenarios as advisory, and flagged a near-miss rate of 3.8%, with 11% of scenarios escalating to intervention or emergency response. Feature attribution consistently identified trajectory risk and VRU proximity as primary safety factors. PRISM provides a unified, interpretable framework for proactive transportation safety with emphasis on VRU risk reduction in dense urban environments.

</details>


### [143] [Differential Games for Compositional Handling of Competing Control Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01838)
*Joshua Shay Kricheli*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A novel Divide and Conquer control design framework using differential games for single-agent multi-objective systems, where each control objective competes as a virtual player in a Nash game to achieve balanced performance across conflicting goals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-agent dynamical systems often face complex, dynamically conflicting control objectives that make traditional global weighting approaches (like LQR with fixed weight matrices) intractable for large-scale applications. Engineers need intuitive, modular frameworks that allow parameter re-tuning throughout the design cycle.

Method: The approach associates each control objective with a virtual input and establishes a non-cooperative differential game among representative players over finite or infinite horizons. Each player optimizes a distinct virtual cost function tailored to its specific goal while accounting for other players' optimal policies. The Nash Equilibrium of this game synthesizes a composite controller, with mathematical derivations provided for both continuous-time and discrete-time systems.

Result: Developed an open-source Python package implementing a novel numerical algorithm for solving Coupled Algebraic Riccati Equations from infinite-horizon differential games. Evaluation on inverted pendulum and non-linear quadrotor benchmarks showed superior trajectory tracking and robust multi-objective regulation compared to classical LQR across various transient and steady-state metrics.

Conclusion: The Divide and Conquer differential game framework provides control engineers with an intuitive, modular approach to handle competing objectives in single-agent systems, achieving stable balance across goals and enabling easier parameter re-tuning throughout the design process compared to traditional methods like LQR.

Abstract: We introduce a novel Divide and Conquer control design methodology leveraging differential games in single-agent, multi-objective dynamical systems. The proposed framework associates each control objective with a virtual input and establishes a non-cooperative, finite or infinite horizon differential game among representative players. Each player optimizes a distinct virtual cost function tailored to its specific goal, the full system state, and the other virtual inputs, while accounting for the remaining players' optimal policies. By establishing a Nash Equilibrium for this game, we synthesize a composite controller that achieves a stable balance across competing objectives, providing control engineers with an intuitive and modular framework for parameter re-tuning throughout the design cycle. We provide formal mathematical derivations for both continuous-time and discrete-time dynamical systems, targeting large-scale single-agent applications where complex, dynamically conflicting control objectives make global weighting intractable. To demonstrate the methodology, we developed an open-source Python package implementing a novel numerical algorithm for solving Coupled Algebraic Riccati Equations arising in infinite-horizon differential games. We evaluate the approach on two benchmark case studies: an inverted pendulum on a cart and a non-linear hierarchically controlled quadrotor. The resulting closed-loop performance is compared against the classical Linear Quadratic Regulator (LQR) across various transient and steady-state control metrics, demonstrating superior trajectory tracking and robust multi-objective regulation.

</details>


### [144] [ArcticSwarm: Deferring Early Consensus in Long-Horizon Multi-Agent Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01870)
*Soyoung Yoon,Boyi Liu,Yite Wang,Ruofan Wu,Canwen Xu,Nikki Lijing Kuang,Seung-won Hwang,Yuxiong He,Zhewei Yao*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: ArcticSwarm introduces a multi-agent research architecture that separates evidence gathering from integration, using gated isolation and structured review to prevent premature consensus and improve long-horizon open-ended research tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent systems rely on reliable verifiers (like in coding tasks) or use majority voting/self-consistency as proxy verifiers. However, these approaches don't generalize well to open-ended, long-horizon research tasks without verifiers. Parallel agents explore the same evidence redundantly, and early access to peers' findings causes search to converge prematurely before exploring alternatives.

Method: ArcticSwarm separates evidence gathering from evidence integration. Subagents publish findings to a shared bulletin board, while gated isolation allows selected search tasks to maintain their own prior, preventing early consensus. Structured review at three commitment boundaries ensures only confident candidates are propagated. The architecture restricts peer reads during evidence gathering and strengthens commitment boundaries before hypotheses are shared.

Result: On BrowseComp-Plus, ArcticSwarm achieves 82.6% with Qwen 3.5-27B model, outperforming configurations without gated isolation (78.8%) and without structured review (74.5%), and significantly surpassing MiroFlow baseline (70.6%). On live-web BrowseComp, ArcticSwarm reaches 73.6% with GPT-5, exceeding both provider system (54.9%) and MiroFlow (63.4%).

Conclusion: Restricting peer reads during evidence gathering and strengthening commitment boundaries before hypotheses are shared can broaden search exploration and improve performance on long-horizon multi-agent deep research tasks, demonstrating ArcticSwarm's effectiveness for open-ended research without reliable verifiers.

Abstract: Multi-agent systems have shown strong performance in domains with reliable verifiers such as coding, where multi-parallel candidate generation selected by a verifier is effective. However, such pipelines would not generalize to open-ended, long-horizon research tasks without a verifier. While majority voting or self-consistency is often used to reach consensus as a proxy verifier, parallel agents repeatedly explore the same evidence, while access to peers' partial findings cause search to converge on an early candidate before alternatives are tested. We present ArcticSwarm, a multi-agent research architecture that separates evidence gathering from evidence integration. Subagents publish findings to a shared bulletin board, while gated isolation lets selected search tasks maintain their own prior, preventing early consensus. Structured review at three commitment boundaries enforce only confident candidates to be propagated. As a result, ArcticSwarm reaches 82.6% on the full BrowseComp-Plus set with the open-weight Qwen 3.5-27B model, compared with 78.8% without gated isolation and 74.5% additionally with structured review disabled, outperforming aligned baseline MiroFlow runs (70.6%). Extending to live-web BrowseComp, ArcticSwarm reaches 73.6% with GPT-5, which is well above the reported provider system (54.9%) and MiroFlow (63.4%). Overall, the results show that restricting peer reads during evidence gathering and strengthening commitment boundaries before a hypothesis is shared can broaden search and improve long-horizon multi-agent deep research.

</details>


### [145] [RideSkill: A Hierarchical Algorithm for Generalized Ride Sharing with LLM-Driven Automatic Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02250)
*Zijian Zhao,Sen Li,Xialiang Tong,Mingxuan Yuan*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: RideSkill is a hierarchical LLM-assisted method for ride-sharing that automatically designs algorithms to solve vehicle sharing problems without needing LLM calls during deployment, enabling real-time performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MARL solutions for ride-sharing suffer from limited generalization, low transferability, and training difficulties in large-scale systems. Current LLM approaches either don't support vehicle sharing (which exponentially expands state/action spaces) or require frequent LLM calls at inference time, making real-time deployment infeasible.

Method: RideSkill uses a hierarchical approach with LLM-assisted automatic algorithmic design: 1) A combiner assigns appropriate skills from a learned skill repository to each vehicle, enabling adaptive dispatch under varying scenarios/objectives, 2) A repositioner sequentially relocates idle vehicles to emerging regions while avoiding conflicts. All components (skill repository, combiner, repositioner) are trained using an LLM-based automatic evolutionary method.

Result: The method eliminates the need for LLM calls during deployment, ensuring high real-time performance while supporting vehicle sharing - a critical feature that expands state and action spaces exponentially.

Conclusion: RideSkill addresses key limitations in existing ride-sharing solutions by combining hierarchical design with LLM-assisted automatic algorithm development, enabling efficient real-time ride-sharing operations without inference-time LLM overhead.

Abstract: Ride-sharing, which allows multiple passengers with different origin-destination (OD) pairs to share a single vehicle, is a challenging operational problem, as it requires orders with different OD pairs to be efficiently bundled and assigned to vehicles under uncertain and varying scenarios. Although multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) solutions have achieved promising performance, they suffer from limited generalization (adapting to different environmental scenarios), low transferability (adapting to different platform objectives), and training difficulties in large-scale systems, such as the curse of dimensionality. Recently, motivated by the scaling of large language models (LLMs), several works have incorporated LLMs into ride-hailing systems, either by employing LLMs directly as decision-making agents or using them for automatic algorithm design. However, none of these approaches support vehicle sharing, which complicates the problem by expanding both the state and action spaces exponentially. Moreover, most of them require frequent LLM calls at inference time, making them infeasible for real-time deployment. To address these issues, we propose RideSkill, a hierarchical method for ride-sharing that leverages LLM-assisted automatic algorithmic design. RideSkill consists of a combiner that assigns appropriate skills to each vehicle from a learned skill repository, enabling adaptive dispatch under varying scenarios and objectives, and a repositioner that sequentially relocates idle vehicles to emerging regions, avoiding conflicts among vehicles. Crucially, the skill repository, combiner, and repositioner are all trained by an LLM-based automatic evolutionary method, eliminating the need for LLM calls during deployment and thus ensuring high real-time performance.

</details>


### [146] [Competitive Market Behavior of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02580)
*Pawel Struski,Jakub Swistak,Inez Okulska,Przemyslaw Biecek*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: LLM agents in double auctions show slower/no convergence to market equilibrium compared to humans, resulting in less efficient resource allocation and revealing substantial heterogeneity in trading behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With LLMs increasingly deployed as economic agents, there's little evidence on whether they perform effectively in market mechanisms designed for humans and whether these mechanisms deliver desired outcomes when LLM agents participate.

Method: Replicated seminal economic experiments by replacing human subjects with LLM agents in a double auction environment, then analyzed individual trading decisions, heterogeneity across model families/market roles, and conducted lexical analysis of Chain-of-Thought traces.

Result: Markets with LLM agents exhibit slower or no convergence towards market equilibrium compared to human markets, providing less efficient allocations. Substantial heterogeneity was found across model families and market roles. Lexical analysis showed trade execution decisions shift from strategic considerations toward urgency.

Conclusion: LLM agents demonstrate limited compatibility with fundamental market mechanisms, failing to achieve the same level of efficiency as human participants, highlighting a novel dimension of LLM alignment that needs addressing. The testing framework is publicly released for future evaluations.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as economic agents, yet there is little evidence whether LLM agents are suited for participating in market mechanisms designed for humans, and whether these mechanisms deliver desired outcomes when faced with LLM agents. We address this question by replicating seminal economic experiments, replacing human subjects with LLM agents. We place agents in a double auction environment, which is a widely-used market mechanism. We check whether such a market is able to deliver an efficient allocation of resources, thereby testing a novel dimension of alignment of LLM agents -- their compatibility with a fundamental market mechanism. We find that markets populated by LLM agents exhibit slower or no convergence towards market equilibrium, thus providing less efficient allocations than markets populated by humans. We then analyze agents' individual trading decisions and find substantial heterogeneity both across model families and market roles. We also run a lexical analysis of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) traces generated by the agents. We find that the decision to execute a trade rather than continue incrementally adjusting prices is associated with a shift from strategic considerations toward urgency. We publicly release our testing framework, which can be used for future evaluations.

</details>
